# SQLite — technique cards (187)

Each card is exactly 7 lines. Fetch one with: grep -A 6 "^### sqlite-NNN " sqlite.md

### sqlite-001 — Hunt down hidden quadratic work
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Look for per-item work whose cost grows with the total size: rescans from the start, nested searches, re-validating the whole batch on every append. Replace it with a remembered position, an inverse map or a single pass.
Why it is faster: An O(n) step inside an O(n) loop is invisible on small inputs and dominates on large ones. Typical fixes are a resume pointer (the next search starts where the last one stopped), a one-time map from key to position (O(1) per probe instead of O(m)), or pruning repeated visits. The fix is usually small, and the gain grows without bound as inputs get larger. These bugs typically surface as timeouts on fuzzer inputs or on unusually large real inputs, not in benchmarks.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: joining two lists with `a.find()` inside `b.map()` is O(n·m). Build a `Map` first. The same goes for N+1 lookups in request handlers. • Native / systems: next-fit pointers in free-list allocators. A clock hand in page replacement instead of rescanning from slot 0. • Data / databases: bulk-insert APIs that re-validate or re-index the whole batch per row. Incremental parsers that re-scan the buffer on every chunk.
Watch out: resume pointers go stale when the underlying list changes. Invalidate them on removal, or verify that the remembered element is still valid before trusting it.
Seen in SQLite: 324ccefeb5 2003-02-05 The shell ran `sqlite_complete()` on the accumulated buffer after every line. It now calls it only if the input ends in a semicolon, so statements that span thousands of lines no longer cause quadratic rescans. • a963896796 2010-02-04 The page cache's search for a page that needs no sync restarted from the head of the dirty list each time. Storing the result back in `pCache->pSynced` fixed "a performance glitch that appears for large transactions". • 3afd2b4d6d 2015-01-05 Long `VALUES` lists skip a per-row WITH push/pop walker callback that was O(N²). 2647dfa292 2015-04-13 DETACH collapses the database array instead of resetting and later re-parsing every schema. • 5b27ecafbc 2024-04-24 `exprNodeIsConstantFunction()` returns `WRC_Prune` so deeply nested function calls are not re-walked at every level. • 8b62a82ae3 2025-01-28 INSERT column matching scanned the M listed columns for each of N table columns. An `aTabColMap[]` inverse map built once made each probe O(1): "about 0.8%" faster, and a union field was removed. • f212fb3362 2025-03-15 fts3 pre-scanned each token's remaining input for barred characters, which was quadratic on long expressions. It now tokenizes optimistically and re-tokenizes only the short prefix when the token covered a barred character.

### sqlite-002 — Choose the container by size, density and key type
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: No single container is best at every size. Use a linear list or plain comparisons for a handful of items, a hash or tree for many, a bitmap for small dense integers, and an integer-keyed structure when the keys are integers. Switch representation when the data crosses a threshold.
Why it is faster: Hashing and tree building have fixed setup costs: bucket allocation, hashing, comparator calls. Below about 10 items a linear scan of contiguous memory wins. Generic variable-length keys pay for encoding and comparator dispatch that integer keys do not need. A bitmap tests membership of small integers in one load with no allocation. Hybrid nodes that change representation with density keep both memory and time good across the whole range. Thresholds should come from measurement: SQLite measured the IN-list cut-off at exactly 2 terms.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `array.includes()` on 3 items beats building a `Set`. HTTP header maps that scan linearly when headers are few. • Embedded: bitmaps for small ID sets (fds, opcodes, slots) instead of lists. No allocator needed. • Native / systems: small-vector and small-map types. Roaring bitmaps with array, bitmap and run containers. Specialized int-keyed hash maps instead of boxed generic ones. • Data / databases: counting sort vs comparison sort by key range. Hash join vs sort-merge by relative input sizes.
Watch out: every extra representation is code to test, and the switch-over itself has a cost. Measure the cut-over point on real data. Cap growth (bucket counts, bitmap ranges) so a single hostile input cannot force a huge allocation.
Seen in SQLite: f5e7bb513c 2008-02-18 Bitvec tracks journaled page numbers. Each fixed-size node is a plain bitmap when the range is small, a small open-addressing hash when the set is sparse, or a radix split into children. It "reduces memory consumption and runs faster than the bitmap approach it replaced". 643a392270 2009-09-09 scales the node size with the pointer width. • 8a1e594c9f 2009-04-28 Symbol tables with fewer than 10 entries use a linked list instead of a hash, and the bucket count is capped to prevent huge allocations. • 41a05b7bee 2008-10-02 `WHERE rowid IN (x,y,z)` stores the set in an integer-keyed b-tree instead of the "usual (slower) variable length keys B-Tree". • 42191f6248 2014-08-05, bb53ecb1db 2014-08-02 IN with 2 or fewer right-hand terms, or with a right-hand side that changes per row, becomes a sequence of comparisons instead of an ephemeral table. e80c9b9ad5 2014-08-01 records the measurement: the table is "clearly better for all list lengths greater than 2". 2b59b3a4c8 2014-03-20 rewrites `X IN (?)` as `X=?`. • 80738d9c3e 2016-02-15 Application-defined functions moved from a fixed 23-bucket hash, which degraded to linear search with thousands of entries, to a growable hash. Built-ins stay in the static table. • d23f210377 2023-07-11, 0dac350f3c 2023-07-15 fts5 tombstone rowids moved from a flat list to an open-addressing hash with 4- or 8-byte keys, split across several records when large. • 58332de7a6 2026-08-16 A two-word bitmap of small `?NNN` parameter numbers answers "seen before?" in O(1). Only larger numbers fall back to the list. • c1d5978a4f 2026-09-03, 7740d55183 2026-09-03 A WAL checkpoint either sorts (frame, page) pairs, which is best for a small WAL, or builds a direct page-to-frame array, which is best when the WAL is larger than the database. The code picks whichever is cheaper for the inputs.

### sqlite-003 — Make key lookups cheap: compare a hash or identity before the key, and order by frequency
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Store a hash with each entry and compare it before the expensive key comparison. Compare pointers first when equal keys are usually the same object. Put the most frequent keys first in each chain, and replace linear scans of static tables with binary search or hashing.
Why it is faster: Key comparisons (case-insensitive strings, `memcmp` of long buffers) cost far more than an integer compare. A stored hash rejects almost every mismatch with one compare, and it also makes rehashing free on resize. Even a 1-byte hash rejects about 255 of 256 mismatches. Pointer identity short-circuits the common case where the caller passes the same interned or cached object. Ordering hash chains by key frequency makes the hottest lookups finish after one probe. Static tables known at build time can be sorted or perfectly hashed offline.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: header and route maps that store the hash and compare it first. Reference equality before deep-equal in memoized UI code. • Native / systems: symbol tables in compilers and linkers. Interned strings compared by pointer. • Embedded: keyword and command tables sorted or perfectly hashed at build time and kept in ROM. • Data / databases: column-name resolution in ORMs and dataframes. Dictionary-encoded string columns compared by code.
Watch out: a stored hash costs memory per entry. Pointer-identity shortcuts are only valid if equal keys really are interned, so assert that in debug builds. Frequency ordering must be revisited when the workload changes.
Seen in SQLite: 297a95bad5 2025-02-08, f62d053b49 2025-02-08 `HashElem` stores the full hash. Lookups test `h==elem->h` before `sqlite3StrICmp`, and resizing reuses the stored hash: "faster lookups with fewer calls to sqlite3StrICmp()". • d44390c8c5 2020-04-06 `Column.hName`, a 1-byte hash of the column name, prefilters name resolution. a192807c13 2020-07-20 and 3e992d1ab7 2021-01-01 reuse it. 44980e8169 2025-01-27 applies it to INSERT column lists: "about 1.8% faster overall". • 21d4f0cc7b 2023-09-02 Client-data lookup tests `zName==p->zName` before `strcmp`. 03075fa868 2023-12-01 The JSON parse cache matches by pointer first, then by length plus `memcmp`. f305e1921b 2026-05-21 compares interned collation objects by pointer instead of by name, with an assert. • bd0f0eb037 2019-11-01 The generated keyword hash gives SELECT, FROM, WHERE, AND and INSERT priority 10 and moves high-priority keywords to the front of their chains: "Slightly faster keyword hash table". • 9ccd8659ff 2013-09-13 The PRAGMA parser uses binary search over a generated sorted table. 84c309b6ae 2013-08-08 uses binary search over STAT4 samples. d310393158 2025-10-05 Printf conversion characters are found by `c % 23` with collision chains tuned offline, replacing a linear search. • bb23aff3df 2010-05-10 The WAL index is an open-addressing hash with at most half its slots full (2 slots per entry), so expected probes stay near 1.

### sqlite-004 — Start searches from where you already are (finger search)
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Successive operations usually target keys near the previous one. Before a full root-to-leaf search, check whether the cursor is already at the target, or whether the target is the next entry or on the current leaf. Bias the binary search toward the expected position.
Why it is faster: A tree search costs O(log n) comparisons plus page visits, and each comparison may decode a record. If the target is usually adjacent, as in sequential scans, auto-increment inserts or sorted bulk loads, a check against the current position costs one or two comparisons. Biasing the first probe of a binary search to the right end turns appends into about one comparison while ordinary searches stay centered. The fallback is always the full search, so correctness does not depend on the guess.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::map::emplace_hint`, last-position caches in skip lists and ropes, galloping search in merges. • Data / databases: append-heavy B-trees and LSM memtables (check the rightmost leaf first). Time-series inserts with monotonic timestamps or UUIDv7 keys. • Web / backend: virtualized lists that look up the next row from the current index. Cursor pagination that resumes from the last key instead of using OFFSET.
Watch out: the hint check is pure overhead on random-access workloads, so keep it to one or two comparisons. Any write that moves entries must invalidate cached cursor state.
Seen in SQLite: f1d68b3096 2007-03-29 The b-tree binary search starts at the upper bound for inserts: "makes append run much faster". e4d9081307 2007-03-29 turned the bias into a parameter (right for `OPFLAG_APPEND`, center for searches), with "about a 15% reduction in calls to sqlite3VdbeRecordCompare". • a2c20e433b 2008-03-29 `sqlite3BtreeMoveto` becomes a no-op when the cursor is already on the target cell. 9c3acf3cfe 2009-05-02 stopped clearing the cursor before an insert seek, which speeds up auto-rowid inserts. 3f632d5bd2 2009-05-02 leaves the cursor on the last entry after an append and makes `sqlite3BtreeLast` a no-op there. • 451e76d5b5 2017-01-21 When the cursor is valid and the target key is larger, try the next cell on the same leaf first. 7f11afaba1 2017-01-21 calls `sqlite3BtreeNext()` when `nKey+1==intKey`, which also crosses to the adjacent page. • c5a55db75d 2022-03-07 `sqlite3BtreeIndexMoveto()` compares against the first and last cells of the cursor's leaf when it is on the last page. It returns at once or searches from there: "almost 4 million CPU cycles". "This case is more common than you would expect."

### sqlite-005 — Give the append-at-the-end case its own cheap path
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: When keys arrive in increasing order, skip the general insert machinery. Position once at the end, append with a "use seek result" hint, and when the last node overflows, start a fresh right sibling instead of redistributing entries.
Why it is faster: General insertion repeats a full search and redistributes items across siblings to keep nodes half full. That is pointless when every new key is larger than all existing keys: the search always ends at the rightmost leaf, and half-full nodes will never receive inserts. A dedicated append path does no search, touches one new page plus one parent entry, and packs pages fully. Bulk loads, index builds and VACUUM are all naturally sorted.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: right-most split optimization for monotonically increasing keys. Sorted bulk-load builders that fill pages completely. • Native / systems: building a sorted array, rope or interval tree by appending when input is known sorted. • Web / backend: event stores and audit logs keyed by time, where every write is an append.
Watch out: fully packed pages split immediately on the next out-of-order insert. Use this for append-dominated or read-mostly data, and keep the general path as the fallback when a key is not the largest.
Seen in SQLite: ac245ec5af 2005-01-14 `balance_quick`: when an insert overflows the rightmost leaf, put the new cell on a fresh right sibling and add one separator to the parent. 615ae553fc 2005-01-16 enabled it by default. 6e465eb0fc 2007-08-21 stopped journaling an extra page in that path. • b18e60b3e4 2015-04-01 CREATE INDEX emits `OP_Last` before `OP_IdxInsert`. e34162b14f 2015-04-01 VACUUM does the same for BINARY-collated indexes. c9b9deaee2 2016-11-15 VACUUM uses `OP_Last` with `OPFLAG_USESEEKRESULT|OPFLAG_APPEND` for rowid tables: "about 9% faster". • 207c817365 2015-06-29 Table inserts bypass `btreeMoveto()` when the position is already known. • 7d6885ae8a 2012-08-08 Bulk-load balancing distributes cells among 3 siblings instead of 4. It produces identical files "but is slightly faster".

### sqlite-006 — Return what you already computed; never recompute it downstream
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: If a callee already knows a value the caller needs next (a length, a size, a classification, a seek result, an end-of-data state), return it. If a general helper computes much more than the caller needs, compute only the needed part.
Why it is faster: Recomputing a byproduct means a second pass or dispatch over the same data: `strlen` after formatting, re-parsing a cell to get its size, re-parsing a number with a second routine to learn whether it was an integer. Returning it costs one register. Distinct status codes (EMPTY, DONE) let every caller drop a follow-up state check. The cost is a slightly wider interface.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: parsers that return bytes consumed. Encoders that return the encoded size. Upserts that reuse the position found by the existence check. • Native / systems: hash-table insert returning the existing slot. Iterators returning a sentinel instead of separate `hasNext()` and `next()` calls. • Data / databases: number parsers that return int-or-float in one pass. Lexers that return token type and length together.
Watch out: a returned byproduct becomes part of the contract. Callers will rely on it, so the callee cannot later stop computing it cheaply.
Seen in SQLite: 9bfdc25062 2014-09-24 `clearCell()` returns the cell size, removing a separate `cellSizePtr()` call. 6200c88123 2014-09-23 `fillInCell()` stops calling `btreeParseCellPtr()`, "since most of what btreeParseCellPtr() computes is ignored". • be37c12423 2015-10-16 `sqlite3VdbeSerialType()` also returns the serial length, removing a second dispatch in OP_MakeRecord. • 8a3884efae 2019-05-29 `sqlite3AtoF` returns 1 for a pure integer, so `applyNumericAffinity` calls `sqlite3Atoi64` only when needed: "Much faster". • fbde3f50bf 2023-01-03 `sqlite3Int64ToText()` returns its length, removing a `strlen`. b7fd951be4 2023-12-04 JSON uses `sqlite3_value_bytes()` instead of `strlen`. e98f62fb3f 2023-10-20 computes `nToken` once. • 44548e7218 2017-08-14 `moveToRoot()` returns `SQLITE_EMPTY` for an empty table, which "simplifies the callers, making them smaller and faster". 2ab792e4c0 2017-05-30 `sqlite3BtreeNext/Previous` return `SQLITE_DONE` instead of using an out-parameter. • de630353d8 2009-05-04 OP_Insert accepts the `seekResult` of a prior seek, so a UNIQUE check followed by an insert seeks once. 93889d9335 2013-11-06 has `OP_NoConflict` pass its seek result on to the following insert. • Also seen in curl (curl-009): 03bb481598 2016-09-05 HTTP/2 send drops the per-header `content-length` scan and uses `state.infilesize` directly. • f4b85d24b2 2021-04-24 Callers of `Curl_client_write` pass the length instead of 0, which had triggered a `strlen`. • b807219292 2022-02-07 `STRCONST()` turns `Curl_dyn_add(lit)` into `Curl_dyn_addn(lit, sizeof(lit)-1)`; 9fff7feb82 does the same for `checkprefix`. • 186340c9cd 2022-02-10 mprintf skips `strlen` on empty strings, 24 such calls "alone when doing a simple GET from https://curl.se". 8f384577b8 2022-10-24 cookies reuse the `strcspn()` result; 458c70db6a 2023-08-27 tests `*endptr == '\0'` instead of comparing with `str+strlen(str)`. • 6694a42aa0 2025-12-01 Windows IDN conversion reuses the length returned by the UTF-8 to wide conversion and passes it to `WideCharToMultiByte()`, "saving length calculations within that API too". • aad438d535 2026-09-21 mprintf: unbounded `%s` passes the known length to the run writer instead of rescanning for the terminator.

### sqlite-007 — Sort with buffered runs and a k-way merge, not by inserting into a tree
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Buffer records in memory, sort them in bulk, spill sorted runs sequentially when memory fills, and merge the runs with a tournament tree. Stream the final merge straight to the consumer, and skip disk entirely if everything fits.
Why it is faster: Sorting by inserting each record into a b-tree costs O(log n) page work per row, with random I/O. A list merge sort touches each record a logarithmic number of times in sequential memory. Spilled runs are written and read sequentially, and a tournament tree merges N runs with log2(N) comparisons per output instead of N. Feeding the final merge directly to the consumer saves writing and re-reading a full final run. A stable sort keeps input order for equal keys, so no tie-breaking sequence column is needed.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: shuffle spill files in Spark or Hadoop. Index builds in search engines. Merging per-shard sorted results. • Web / backend: sorting exports larger than memory in Node or Python with chunked runs and a heap-based merge. • Native / systems: `sort --parallel`-style log processing. Merging sorted log streams with a loser tree.
Watch out: run size and fan-in are tunables. SQLite raised the minimum PMA size to 250 pages and then lowered it back to 10 after measuring. Dropping the tie-breaker column depends on the sort really being stable.
Seen in SQLite: a20fde64eb 2011-07-12 Offline merge sort for CREATE INDEX. c6e734554f 2011-08-04 Runs became packed arrays (PMAs) instead of b-trees, "easier to control the number of disc seeks", and the tournament tree (`aTree[]`, padded to a power of two, EOF compares greater) arrived with it. 1e74e602ec 2011-08-06 Each run stores its own size, so offsets need not stay in RAM. • 5134d1357e 2011-09-02 An in-memory linked list plus merge sort replaced the temp b-tree. 9fed558df9 2011-09-02 No temp files if the data fits. d40807ddef 2011-09-03 The sorter also serves ORDER BY and GROUP BY. • 8b1ea14f8c 2011-09-03 Merging keeps the unchanged side's key unpacked (`bKey2InSpace`) instead of decoding it on every compare. • f87684189d 2014-03-17 Worker threads sort runs in parallel. f77ceba594 2014-04-14 The final merge passes keys "directly to the VDBE, instead of going via a final PMA". 4be4c406fa 2014-04-11 limits merge fan-in. • dd23c6bfb4 2014-03-24, 78d5843245 2014-03-25 Because the merge sort is stable, the `OP_Sequence` tie-breaker column was dropped from GROUP BY and ORDER BY sorter records. fad9f9a8a6 2014-04-01 CREATE INDEX assumes keys arrive in primary-key order. • b982bfea37 2016-05-20 Linked-list merge loops assert non-empty inputs and handle exhaustion inside the loop: "10 bytes smaller and use 0.03% fewer CPU cycles". • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-013): 957146dcec9 1999-10-16 logtape.c multiplexes logical tapes into one file and recycles blocks, reducing peak disk usage "to essentially just the volume of data being sorted, whereas it had been about 4x"; 26c48b5e8cf built the generalized tuplesort on it. • cf627ab41ab 1999-10-30 Replacement selection keeps current-run and next-run tuples in one heap ("Knuth is smarter than I am") and merges read multiple tuples per tape for locality; earlier 712ea2507ef and b0ccd78479b used qsort for initial runs and skipped merging when there was a single run. • 1ee26b77647 2000-06-18 Materialize uses a tuplestore that stays in memory and spills to a temporary BufFile only past its budget, instead of a temporary relation. • df700e6b401 2006-02-19 Variable merge order (many tapes); b34aa3372fa allocates tape buffers lazily; 43ceb3d4493 sorts the free-block list only when blocks are consumed; 8db05ba411e stops tracking free blocks during the final on-the-fly merge. • 0711803775a 2016-04-08 Runs are quicksorted, with replacement selection kept only for a small first run; e94568ecc10 pre-reads into logtape buffers in the compact on-disk format with one tuple per tape in memory. • 9da0cc35284 2018-02-02 Parallel external sort powers parallel B-tree builds, often 2-3x faster. • 896ddf9b3cd 2020-05-26 Logtape preallocates blocks so concurrently written spill partitions stay contiguous; 07589649639 disables this for sorts.

### sqlite-008 — Don't fully sort when you only need part of the order
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: For top-K, keep a bounded structure of K items. For a median or percentile, use selection (quickselect). For "process in cost order", use a priority queue instead of a sorted list maintained by insertion.
Why it is faster: A full sort is O(n log n) time and O(n) memory. Bounded top-K is O(n log K) time and O(K) memory. Quickselect recurses into only one partition, O(n) expected. Insertion into a sorted list costs O(n) per insert, so a work queue becomes O(n²). A heap or bucketed queue costs O(log n) per insert.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: leaderboards and "latest N" feeds with a heap (`heapq.nsmallest`). Latency percentiles with selection or a sketch instead of sorting all samples. • Native / systems: `std::nth_element` and `std::partial_sort`. Schedulers and timers with heaps. • Data / databases: top-K operators in analytics engines. Approximate percentiles in metrics pipelines.
Watch out: quickselect is O(n²) in the worst case unless pivots are chosen carefully. If the caller later needs the full order after all, the partial work was wasted.
Seen in SQLite: d59ba6ce61 2006-01-08 ORDER BY with LIMIT keeps at most LIMIT+OFFSET rows in the sorter, deleting the largest whenever it overflows. Memory becomes O(K). 4db38a7092 2005-09-01 introduced the bounded-memory sort code. • 36e9e015de 2026-06-13, a0dc22438d 2026-06-15, 6bf7825ac5 2026-06-06 `median()` and `percentile()` process only the partition holding the requested index (`percentSort(a,n,iReq)`). • 2a0e00b436 2011-03-30 The fuzzer virtual table replaced insertion sort with bucketed, merge-sorted queues: "An insertion sort was way too slow". • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-014): d26559dbf35 2007-05-04 Bounded top-N heap sort for ORDER BY ... LIMIT; avoiding spill-to-disk for large M "is actually the most attractive thing about it". • a2ff18e89ff 2021-12-14 `binaryheap.c` and `logtape.c` sift through a hole, as tuplesort already did. • 9bfd44bbde4 2023-09-19 Parallel pg_restore's ready list became a binary heap; re-sorting had been a field-reported bottleneck with many tables. c103d073819 added arbitrary-node removal to binaryheap; 79273cc7d29 had already used a heap for pg_dump's topological sort. • 849110dd3ee 2024-10-30 Sift-down compares the two children first, then the parent with the larger child: 2 comparisons instead of 3. • 3b4e53a075e 2025-11-03 LSN waiters live in pairing heaps so flush and replay can check the minimum in O(1) before waking anyone.

### sqlite-009 — Drive recursive and best-first evaluation from a work queue
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Evaluate fixpoints breadth-first from one FIFO queue instead of copying "current" and "next" tables each round. Walk spatial or scored trees best-first with a priority queue so results come out in order and the search can stop early.
Why it is faster: Table swapping copies the whole frontier every iteration. A queue touches each item once when it is produced and once when it is consumed. A best-first walk visits nodes in score order, so nearest-first queries can stop after K results instead of exploring the whole tree depth-first. A small cache for the head of the queue keeps the hottest entries out of the heap.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: worklist algorithms in compilers (dataflow fixpoints). A* and Dijkstra in games and routing. • Data / databases: k-nearest-neighbour search in geospatial indexes. Graph BFS. Incremental view maintenance. • Web / backend: crawlers and dependency resolvers driven by a deduplicated queue instead of repeated full passes.
Watch out: a queue needs duplicate suppression to terminate on cyclic data, and that dedup structure has its own cost (SQLite kept tuning its UNION index). A priority queue can grow large on wide trees, so bound it when a LIMIT is known.
Seen in SQLite: e73f059093 2014-01-21, 953dfa4e60 2014-01-22 WITH RECURSIVE switched to a queue: "Runs about 25% faster on the sudoku solver query". 7e4efaecad 2014-02-26 made UNION duplicate removal in the queue more efficient. cfe24586a8 2014-01-22 avoids an extra seek in the dedupe index. • 96ce1b36aa 2014-04-15, 22596b6b3c 2014-04-16 R-Tree queries walk the tree with a priority queue (`aPoint` heap plus an `RTREE_CACHE_SZ` node cache) and expose `sqlite3_rtree_query_callback()`.

### sqlite-010 — Edit in place with the minimum movement
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a structure changes a little, change only that part. Overwrite a same-size record instead of deleting and reinserting it. Shift only the items that cross a boundary. Pad an encoding so its size does not change. Skip restructuring when a cheap test shows it would do nothing.
Why it is faster: Delete-plus-insert and full rebuilds cost work proportional to the whole container: freeing, defragmenting, copying every item, and rewriting every page. A local edit costs work proportional to the change. Same-size overwrites also leave neighboring bytes and pages clean, which reduces downstream write and journal work. A no-op test such as "is it already balanced?" costs one comparison.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: in-place updates of fixed-width records. BSON or MessagePack patching with padded lengths. LSM compaction that reuses unchanged blocks. • Web / backend: UI list reconciliation that moves only the boundary items. Gap buffers and ropes in editors. • Embedded: overwrite flash records in place when the size matches, to save erase cycles.
Watch out: the local-edit path must produce exactly what the full rebuild would. SQLite fixed a memcpy bound in the same-size overwrite right after introducing it (2d08343a91 2016-12-09). Padding trades a few bytes of space for less data movement.
Seen in SQLite: 33ea486603 2014-10-09, 09c6840153 2014-10-11, 53838629ce 2014-10-27 `balance_nonroot()` gained `editPage()`, which adds or removes only the cells at the edges of each sibling and merges adjacent frees into one `freeSpace()` call. Full `rebuildPage()` is the fallback. • 80159da6c6 2016-12-09 `sqlite3BtreeInsert()` overwrites a cell in place when the new cell has the same size and no overflow. c6c9e158c5 2016-11-10 REPLACE on a WITHOUT ROWID table with no secondary indexes overwrites directly. • e6d065a81e 2017-02-24, 5328181db1 2017-02-25 `defragmentPage()` with two or fewer free blocks just memmoves the blocks and adjusts cell offsets instead of rebuilding every cell. • de9484858e 2022-03-29 `sqlite3BtreeDelete()` skips `balance()` when `nFree*3 <= usableSize*2`, using an integer multiply instead of a precomputed division. • 3a7042e0e2 2025-04-01 `jsonBlobOverwrite()` pads a smaller replacement with a wider length header so the JSONB blob keeps its size. A small edit then avoids memmoving everything after it.

### sqlite-011 — Shape linked structures for the dominant operations
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Keep data only on leaves so interior nodes have high fanout. Derive ancestry from the traversal path instead of storing parent pointers that every split must rewrite. Use pointer-to-pointer back links so unlinking needs no special case.
Why it is faster: Interior nodes that hold only keys and child pointers fit more entries per node, so the tree is shallower, and full scans walk only leaves. Parent pointers make every split or merge rewrite every moved child ("reparenting"), which is write amplification on the hottest path. A cursor already knows the root-to-leaf path for free. A `**ppPrev` back link turns unlink into a single store with no head-of-list branch.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: clustered indexes and on-disk key-value stores as B+trees. Path stacks in B-tree, trie and rope iterators instead of parent links. • Native / systems: intrusive lists with `pprev` (as in Linux `hlist`). Zippers for persistent trees. • Web / backend: immutable tree updates that rebuild only the path to the root.
Watch out: without parent pointers you cannot walk upward from a node you reached some other way. Every such walk must start from a cursor that recorded its path.
Seen in SQLite: 8b18dd4fb5 2004-05-12 implemented a B+tree option (all data on leaves). 940762585e 2004-05-14 uses B+trees for tables. • d326064e0d 2008-09-26 stopped calling `reparentChildPages()` from `balance_nonroot()`. 71d5d2cd49 2008-09-29 made each cursor track the ancestry of its current page (`apPage[]`, `aiIdx[]`) instead of storing parent pointers in pages. 00a696d6d1 2008-09-29 removed reparenting entirely. • e5928b17ca 2022-08-23 The statement list uses `Vdbe **ppVPrev`, saving "a few bytes and a few CPU cycles".

### sqlite-012 — Shortcut trivial, degenerate and no-op inputs before the general algorithm
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Test for the empty, the unchanged and the most common simple argument first, and answer those directly. Run the general machinery only for what is left.
Why it is faster: General algorithms pay setup costs (searches, formatting engines, locks, scaling loops) that are pointless for an empty free list, a request that matches the current state, or an argument value such as 0. A one-comparison test in front costs almost nothing and often covers most real calls. Sometimes the shortcut also changes the complexity: SUBSTR on a huge string with small positive offsets no longer measures the whole string.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: setters that compare before writing and notifying (state-update bail-out on equal values). Integer fast paths in JSON serializers instead of a general formatter. • Native / systems: early return on empty arrays and strings. Skip a merge when one side is empty. • Embedded: special-case zero and small-integer arguments in math and formatting routines to avoid pulling in float code.
Watch out: every shortcut is a second implementation that must agree with the general path on edge cases (signs, NULL, overflow). Add a shortcut only for cases that profiling shows are common.
Seen in SQLite: 4adc4cb93b 2009-11-11 `substr()` measures the full UTF-8 length only when the start is negative: "dramatically faster in the common case". • 147e176aff 2010-02-17 `round(x)` with zero digits uses an integer cast instead of `sqlite3_mprintf("%.*f")` plus atof. 2458a2e742 2011-10-17 `atof` with exponent of 342 or more returns 0 or Inf at once instead of looping through scale multiplications. • 7bc4c454da 2014-08-20 `freeSpace` shortcuts an empty page free list. 4c04f3c8bd 2014-08-20 `allocateSpace` searches the free list only if the header says it is non-empty. 380d685133 2013-11-20 skips loading overflow pages for zero-length content. • b68d63158a 2025-06-20 `sqlite3BtreeSetPageSize()` returns at once if page size and reserve are unchanged. 6c2d8c88f7 2026-10-16 skips a zero-length `memmove()` when inserting a cell at the end. • ac178b3d7f 2016-12-14 `sqlite3SrcListAppend()` initializes the first element directly on first allocation: "Three times faster" in the common case.

### sqlite-013 — Prune tree walks that cannot find anything
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Mark nodes that cannot contain what a walker looks for (leaves, node kinds that cannot yield the property), and return "prune" as soon as a subtree's answer is known. Don't descend into subtrees no callback cares about.
Why it is faster: Generic visitors touch every node and probe every child pointer. One flag set at construction time lets walkers, deleters and copiers return from leaves at once. Returning "prune" after a decision prevents re-walking the same subtree from each ancestor, which is what makes nested input quadratic. Skipping subquery descent when no callback needs it removes whole branches of work.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: static-subtree hoisting in virtual-DOM diffing. `path.skip()` in Babel plugins. • Native / systems: AST visitors in compilers and linters. GC marking that skips objects with no pointer fields. • Data / databases: query rewriters that skip subqueries when the property cannot apply. Spatial indexes pruning by bounding box.
Watch out: a wrong "cannot contain" flag silently skips real work. Keep the list of node kinds that can match explicit, so new node types fail safe rather than getting pruned by default.
Seen in SQLite: 209bc522b0 2016-09-23 The parser sets `EP_Leaf` on terminal expressions. `sqlite3WalkExpr`, `sqlite3ExprDelete` and `exprDup` check it and return early. 50922cfc7c 2016-01-11, 922802c44e 2016-08-10 and d43075bcaf 2016-09-19 sped up the walker further. • 5b27ecafbc 2024-04-24 returns `WRC_Prune` for constant-function checks on deeply nested SQL functions. • 016f94d074 2026-11-10 sets `xSelectCallback = 0` to "Avoid unnecessary scanning of subqueries". d3265d1473 2026-05-16 lists the only five node kinds that can return a subtype and prunes the rest. 8c8443a38a 2025-07-07 stops flagging non-EXISTS subqueries, which avoids later work.

### sqlite-014 — Fuse separate passes over the same data
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: If two steps both scan the same input (validate and parse, measure and copy, convert and check), do them in one scan. Filter by several keys in one merge pass instead of one pass per key.
Why it is faster: Each pass streams the whole input through cache and pays loop overhead again. A fused pass reads every byte once. A merge over a sorted list of requested keys turns O(keys × length) into O(length). The cost is a more complex single loop.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: validate while parsing streaming JSON instead of validating and then parsing. Compute a checksum while copying an upload. • Data / databases: merging sorted posting lists in one pass. Projection plus filter in a single scan operator. • Embedded: compute CRC and length while receiving a frame, not in a second loop.
Watch out: fused loops are harder to read and to vectorize. If one of the passes is usually skipped, fusing can make the common case slower.
Seen in SQLite: 4ee2e72c58 2025-11-30 Removed a separate `strlen(zSql)` length-limit check. The tokenizer, which reads every byte anyway, enforces the limit. • 32c05e993f 2002-11-11 Replaced `atoi()` plus a separate `isInteger()` scan with one `toInt()` that converts and reports whether the whole string was an integer: "a dramatic speed improvement for some kinds of queries". • 0fa433b40c 2020-12-02 `fts5IndexExtractColset()` walks the position list once while advancing through the sorted column array. When one column is requested it returns a pointer into the input instead of copying.

### sqlite-015 — Step forward a bounded number of times, then fall back to a seek
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: When the next target is probably only a few entries ahead, step forward linearly. Cap the steps at about the cost of one seek (roughly log N), then do a real seek. You get the speed of a scan when the guess is right and the cost of a search when it is wrong.
Why it is faster: A seek costs O(log N) comparisons and page visits, while stepping to an adjacent entry costs one comparison. With unknown data distribution, neither strategy alone is safe: pure stepping can scan millions of rows, and pure seeking wastes work on dense targets. A bounded scan with fallback is never worse than about twice the better strategy, which makes it robust against bad row estimates.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: galloping (exponential then binary) search in merges, as in Timsort. Linear probing for a few steps before a binary search. • Data / databases: hybrid sequential and random I/O in storage scans. Posting-list intersection that skips with seeks only when gaps are large. • Web / backend: cursor pagination that scans a few rows before re-querying from an index.
Watch out: the step budget is a tunable tied to the seek cost. Set it from data size, not as a constant, or it will be wrong for both tiny and huge tables.
Seen in SQLite: 68cf0ace3d 2020-09-28 introduced `OP_SeekScan` for IN on a later index column. fad4dd0fd1 2020-09-30 "gives up and falls back to doing a seek if the number of rows scanned grows too large". 4f65b3bbfb 2020-09-30 sets the step limit from index size, `(aiRowLogEst[0]+9)/10`. b034a24140 2021-06-02 and 946b65793c 2021-06-04 refined it after a forum report.

### sqlite-016 — Prove a step unnecessary, then delete it; drop clever algorithms that buy little
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Once data is provably always in some form (already sorted, never shorter than N, always adjacent), assert it and remove the code that handles the general case. Likewise, remove a sophisticated algorithm when benchmarks show its quality gain does not pay for its CPU cost.
Why it is faster: Code for impossible cases still costs branches, instruction cache and maintenance, and sometimes whole algorithms (an O(N²) sort of data that is always sorted). An assert keeps the invariant checked in debug builds at zero release cost. Heuristics added for marginal density or quality often cost more CPU than they save in later lookups.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip re-sorting data that comes from an ordered source. Skip dedup when upstream keys are guaranteed unique. • Native / systems: drop rebalancing heuristics in tree variants. Use a simpler eviction policy (CLOCK instead of an ARC variant) when hit rates barely differ. • Data / databases: skip perfect-hash or index generation for small maps.
Watch out: invariants hold only as long as every writer maintains them. Corrupt or hostile input can break them, so release builds still need a safe failure mode, not undefined behavior.
Seen in SQLite: fe647dc9ee 2015-06-23 Overflow cells on a page are always adjacent and sorted, so the O(N²) `btreeSortOverflow()` was removed and asserts were added: "small size reduction and performance increase". • 1e24dc987a 2023-06-19 No keyword is shorter than 2 characters, so the `n>=2` guard became an assert, and the tokenizer exits early when the second character cannot belong to a keyword. • 7de8ae22f7 2023-09-13 Removed the R*-tree forced-reinsert algorithm. Most benchmarks run faster at the cost of slightly less dense nodes (33 instead of 34 entries). 386e359044 2023-09-13 calls `cellArea()` fewer times in ChooseLeaf. • e6f85e71e9 2004-12-25 Removed planner code and found the simpler optimizer did "a better job".

### sqlite-017 — Make backtracking matchers skip ahead and abort early
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: After a wildcard, jump to the next position where the following literal can match instead of recursing at every position. Propagate a "cannot match even with more wildcard" result so the whole search stops instead of retrying.
Why it is faster: A naive recursive glob matcher tries every split point after each `*`, which is exponential with several wildcards. Scanning for the next literal (both cases, for case-insensitive matching) removes most recursive calls. A three-valued result (MATCH, NOMATCH, NOWILDCARDMATCH) encodes the fact that if a sub-match failed after consuming the wildcard, advancing further cannot help either.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: glob-based routers, CORS origin patterns and ACL wildcards. • Native / systems: `.gitignore` and shell glob libraries. First-character optimization in regex engines. • Data / databases: LIKE evaluation in query engines. Log search with wildcards.
Watch out: early-abort logic is subtle with escapes, character classes and multibyte text. Fuzz the matcher against a slow reference implementation.
Seen in SQLite: 9fdfdc893b 2014-09-25 After `*` or `%`, `patternCompare` precomputes the other case of the next literal and scans for either before recursing. 88b3322f27 2014-09-25 merges escape and character-set handling. 328d913cbd 2014-09-25 removes the `prevEscape` state ("Size reduction and performance improvement"). • 698a01ca2f 2016-12-01 `patternCompare()` returns `SQLITE_MATCH`, `SQLITE_NOMATCH` or `SQLITE_NOWILDCARDMATCH`, and callers stop on anything but NOMATCH. This speeds up patterns with two or more wildcards. • f8a62a56e1 2026-10-15, b495f75d81 2026-10-16 check the next literal against the input inline before recursing: "Avoid unnecessary recursive calls in LIKE and GLOB matching".

### sqlite-018 — Use logarithmic and exact-integer numeric algorithms
Lens: algorithms · Source: SQLite
Principle: Compute powers by squaring, not by repeated multiplication. Convert between binary floats and decimal with integer mantissas and a compact power-of-ten table instead of extended-precision floating point. Choose an output precision that is correct in one pass rather than print, verify and retry.
Why it is faster: 10^e by repeated multiplication is O(e). Square-and-multiply is O(log e). Integer mantissa times normalized power of ten with a 128-bit multiply (the Ryu and Eisel-Lemire family) avoids long-double or double-double arithmetic. A 27-entry exact table plus a coarse table gives any power with at most one extra multiply, instead of storing hundreds of entries. Seventeen significant digits always round-trip a binary64, so a format-parse-compare-reformat loop is unnecessary.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON number printing and parsing (Ryu, fast_float). CSV writers that print floats once at round-trip precision. • Embedded: compact power tables where flash is tight. Integer-only conversion on cores without an FPU. • Native / systems: modular exponentiation in crypto. Fixed-point scaling.
Watch out: correctly rounded conversion is easy to get subtly wrong. SQLite needed a rounding fix to its table (7ac310d0c6 2026-02-27) and later more precise approximations. Test against a reference on billions of random values.
Seen in SQLite: 02a43f6def 2017-12-26 `sqlite3Pow10()` uses square-and-multiply, replacing `while(e%22) scale*=10` loops in `sqlite3AtoF`: "Faster and smaller". • db84cdb747 2026-02-14 introduced a 696-entry power-of-ten table. adbb756155 2026-02-15 replaced it with `powerOfTen()` built from 27 exact entries plus a scale table, removing about 730 lines. c47bc0ffca 2026-02-16 replaced double-double multiplication with `sqlite3Multiply128()`. dfd31e158c 2026-03-22 credits rsc/fpfmt. a6640846dd 2026-03-16 moved to 96-bit approximations for accuracy. • c409eaaf14 2026-03-28 `quote()` replaced print at 15 digits, parse back, compare, and print at 20 on mismatch with a single `%!0.17g`.

### sqlite-019 — Use order that already exists instead of sorting
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: If rows already arrive in the required order (from an index, from an earlier GROUP BY, from a subquery, from a loop that yields one row), skip the sort. If only a prefix is ordered, sort within groups. Store data in the order queries usually want.
Why it is faster: A sort is O(n log n), needs memory or temp files, and blocks until all input is read. Reading an index in order (forward or backward) costs nothing extra, streams results immediately, and lets LIMIT stop early. Uniqueness helps too: if a unique key covers a prefix of the ORDER BY, ties cannot occur, so the rest of the sort is redundant. If an ordered index feeds DISTINCT, each row only needs comparing with the previous one.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cursor pagination on indexed columns instead of sorting in the app. Skip `.sort()` on results from an ordered source. • Data / databases: merge joins on pre-sorted streams. Sorting within time buckets or partitions instead of globally. • Native / systems: reverse iteration in LSM stores for "latest N". Newest-first storage for timelines.
Watch out: order guarantees break silently under parallelism, hash-based operators and NULL ordering rules. Each elimination needs a proof that the order truly holds, which is why SQLite required NOT NULL for its DISTINCT shortcut.

034967bdcbb extended it to inheritance trees through MergeAppend. • 959d00e9dbe 2019-04-05 Ordered partition scans use Append instead of MergeAppend, removing comparisons and starting only the first child. • d2d8a229bc5 2020-04-06 Incremental Sort sorts only within groups of equal prefix keys; 3c6fc58209f applied it to DISTINCT and 625d5b3ca09 to KNN-ordered GiST scans; 4a29eabd1d9 removed a pessimistic cost penalty. • 1349d2790bf 2022-08-02 ORDER BY and DISTINCT aggregates consume presorted input, choosing the order that serves the most aggregates. • 0452b461bc4 2024-01-21 GROUP BY keys are reordered to match available orderings, avoiding sorts. • 66c0185a3d1 2024-03-25 UNION can be a Merge Append of presorted children plus Unique, enabling cheap-startup LIMIT plans.

### sqlite-020 — Answer from the narrowest structure that has the data
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: If every column a query needs is in an index, never touch the table (covering or index-only scan). For full scans and counts, scan the smallest structure available, or count from page metadata. Reuse an indexed expression's stored value instead of recomputing it.
Why it is faster: A secondary-index lookup followed by a table lookup costs two searches per row. A covering index costs one. An index is narrower than the table, so scanning it reads fewer pages. Counting from per-page cell counts needs no row decoding. Constant propagation can make a partial index covering by replacing a column with the value the index already guarantees.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: INCLUDE columns and index-only scans. Summary or projection tables. Counts kept as metadata in column stores. • Web / backend: serve list views from a denormalized projection or cache entry without fetching full records. • Native / systems: keep small "key plus hot fields" side arrays so scans never touch the wide records.
Watch out: wider covering indexes cost space and slow every write. Size estimates for "the index is smaller" can be wrong when the index has as many columns as the table.
Seen in SQLite: 9012bcbc0a 2004-12-19 uses a `colUsed` bitmask to detect covering indexes, "saving a single BTree search per row … could potentially double the speed". 21de2e755c 2007-11-29 reads any column available in the index from the index, so the deferred table seek often never happens. • 3f4d1d1b02 2012-09-15 costs a full scan of a covering index slightly below a table scan, on the theory that the index is smaller. 4139c99eba 2010-04-07 makes automatic indexes always covering. • e2d7b24d08 2009-02-23 answers `count(*)` by scanning an index instead of the table. a55331620e 2009-02-24 adds `sqlite3BtreeCount()`, one iteration per page. • 0c733f67d8 2011-11-16 uses the rowid stored at the end of each index entry to serve a range constraint after all index columns are matched. • 8ea0056d5c 2016-01-30 lets the OR optimization use covering indexes. 47b427423d 2023-09-25 propagates constants from a partial index's WHERE to increase "the likelihood of the partial index being a covering index". • 805055f734 2022-10-19 reads the value of an indexed expression from the index wherever it appears. 08535840c6 2022-10-17 extends this to virtual columns and f3adb7c430 2022-10-15 to aggregate arguments.

### sqlite-021 — Push filters and limits toward the data source
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Apply each predicate at the earliest point where its inputs are available: inside subqueries, before materialization, in outer loops instead of inner loops, before aggregation, and in the storage or virtual-table layer itself.
Why it is faster: Every row removed early is a row that later stages never copy, join, sort or aggregate. A filter moved from an inner loop to an outer one runs once per outer row instead of once per inner row. Pushing LIMIT or ranges into the source lets it stop producing rows, or skip subtrees, instead of generating rows that are thrown away.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: push filters and pagination into the database query instead of filtering in the app. GraphQL resolvers that pass arguments down to data sources. • Data / databases: predicate pushdown into Parquet or object storage scans. Filter before join in dataframe pipelines. • Native / systems: recursive directory walkers with a depth limit. Packet filters in the kernel instead of userspace.
Watch out: pushing a predicate past an outer join, a window function or a LIMIT can change results. Each push-down needs explicit legality rules, and SQLite skips several of them when window functions are present.
Seen in SQLite: 0f35a6b529 2008-02-12 applies the WHERE clause while materializing a view for UPDATE or DELETE, so fewer rows are copied. • 69b72d5a29 2015-06-01, 4c393a82df 2015-06-02 push WHERE terms into non-flattenable FROM-clause subqueries, which "run faster and/or use less memory". d59afcfbfe 2024-04-08, de6a4be9b5 2024-04-06 extend push-down to terms with uncorrelated subqueries. • a1f5c0c90b 2017-05-02 moves HAVING terms on GROUP BY columns into WHERE. cd0abc24d1 2018-03-20 makes that use "less space and less CPU". • 0c41d22920 2013-04-22 uses transitivity to move constraints "into the outer loops … reducing the amount of work … in inner loops". • c525e6e817 2025-07-03 passes LIMIT to virtual tables in more correlated-subquery shapes. 6facd9566d 2025-06-05 gives the fsdir virtual table a `level` column so `level<N` stops it from descending deeper directories. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-026): cdd230d6289 2001-06-05 For `(a=1 or a=2) and b=42`, `b=42` is added to each OR arm's index quals. • 3b167a4099c 2005-06-10 LIMIT is pushed into UNION ALL arms. • cd30728fb2e 2012-02-13 LEAKPROOF functions let quals be pushed through security-barrier views. • d222585a9f7 2014-06-27 WHERE quals are pushed into subqueries containing window functions when safe. • 3452dc5240d 2017-08-29 Row bounds pass through Gather and Gather Merge so workers exit early and the Sort below becomes bounded; 1f6d515a67e pushes limits through subqueries. • 8ad51b5f446 2022-12-30 postgres_fdw ANALYZE samples remotely with TABLESAMPLE or `random()` instead of transferring the whole table. • 0ee83dd4a99 2026-08-19 postgres_fdw pushes joins with IMMUTABLE function RTEs such as `unnest(...)` to the remote side; b6178c942ea allows LIMIT pushdown when all ORDER BY pathkeys are redundant. • a0edc1c318f 2026-09-01 pg_dump restored a logically redundant filter on its attribute-statistics query because removing it had blocked pushdown and caused a significant regression.

### sqlite-022 — Rewrite predicates into forms an index can use
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Turn constructs an index cannot serve into equivalent ones it can: OR chains into IN, a prefix LIKE into a range, BETWEEN and IS NULL into index ranges, DISTINCT into GROUP BY, `x=A OR x>A` into `x>=A`, single-element IN into equality, and point lookups into direct seeks.
Why it is faster: The difference between a predicate the index can use and one it cannot is the difference between O(log n) and O(n). The rewrites are purely syntactic and cost nothing at run time. Collapsing several terms into one range also means one scan instead of several.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORM query builders that emit `IN` instead of OR chains and ranges instead of `LIKE 'abc%'` on non-text indexes. • Data / databases: prefix queries on keyword fields in search engines. Normalizing search-API filters before execution. • Native / systems: route matchers that turn literal prefixes into trie or range lookups.
Watch out: LIKE-to-range rewrites depend on collation and case rules. A rewrite that is valid under BINARY collation is wrong under NOCASE.
Seen in SQLite: 6c30be8e51 2005-07-29 OR to IN. ed37800693 2005-07-28 BETWEEN uses indexes. 50b3996899 2006-10-28 IS NULL uses indexes. d99f70680f 2002-06-08 IN-operator optimizations. • d2687b7731 2005-08-12 turns the non-wildcard prefix of LIKE/GLOB into inequality constraints. 93ee23cc03 2010-07-22 optimizes LIKE with no wildcard like an equality range. 8342e49f62 2010-07-22 relaxes the collation requirement. 1ad93a003d 2016-11-02 allows a partial index with `IS NOT NULL` for LIKE. • 3c4809a201 2007-11-12 DISTINCT to GROUP BY, because "GROUP BY clauses may be optimized by indices, DISTINCT qualifiers cannot". • 9a96b66810 2007-11-29 `IN (SELECT col FROM t)` probes t's existing index instead of building an ephemeral table. • 8426636cdc 2015-03-16, 29894b6d2b 2015-03-16 combine OR terms with identical operands into one operator. • 95b395901a 2020-03-26 `x IN (y)` becomes `x==y`. 281bbe2a95 2012-10-16, 6d42097622 2012-12-14 pass IN constraints to virtual tables. • 341d60838c 2006-09-12 answers full-text queries for a particular rowid by direct lookup instead of a table scan.

### sqlite-023 — Stream intermediate results instead of materializing them
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Run producer and consumer as coroutines so rows flow one at a time, with no temporary table. Merge-join sorted streams instead of building a temp table and then sorting it. If a result must be materialized, delay that until something actually reads it.
Why it is faster: Materializing costs a full write and read of every row plus memory or disk, and nothing is returned until it finishes. A coroutine keeps only the current row and starts producing output immediately, so LIMIT can stop the producer early. Compiling a big literal list as a coroutine also shrinks the compiled program. Lazy materialization does no work at all on paths that never read the result.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: async iterators and streams instead of arrays for ETL and exports. Streaming JSON responses. • Data / databases: Volcano or pull iterators. Merging sorted shards in distributed queries. Generator-based bulk insert instead of building one giant statement. • Embedded: stream sensor data through a pipeline with constant memory instead of buffering a full batch.
Watch out: a stream can be consumed only once. If a result is read several times, or needs an index built on it, materializing once is cheaper. See the next entry.
Seen in SQLite: f46f905a1e 2002-06-22 UNION ALL without ORDER BY avoids an intermediate table. 142e30df99 2002-08-28 INSERT…SELECT without one. e159fdf21c 2004-06-21 aggregates without GROUP BY skip the temp table. • e00ee6eb37 2008-06-20 uses coroutines for INSERT…SELECT. 92b01d53c2 2008-06-24, 0acb7e4849 2008-06-25 run both sides of a compound SELECT with ORDER BY as coroutines and merge them like merge sort. • 21172c4cc0 2012-10-30, 6e24ff83d8 2012-10-30 code single-use FROM-clause subqueries and views as coroutines. 832ee3d4cc 2012-12-18 no longer manifests a parenthesized single table. 70ac36e877 2022-12-14 makes the planner "more aggressive about using co-routines rather than materializing". • 43a511927d 2024-03-18 VALUES-as-coroutine: large INSERT VALUES lists "prepare and run in about half the time and with half the memory". f0f4323824 2024-03-14 lays out its output registers so INSERT reads them with no copy. • 40822eb262 2022-05-21, 43baa1de76 2022-05-23 defer the materialization subroutine until first use.

### sqlite-024 — Compute a shared sub-result once and reuse it
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: If the same view, subquery, CTE or expression is used in several places, evaluate it once and share the result through a duplicate cursor, a subroutine, or a signature-keyed lookup. Guard per-statement setup with a run-once flag.
Why it is faster: Re-running a subquery per reference, or per outer row, multiplies its cost. Sharing turns k evaluations into 1. A cheap signature (select ID plus affinity), with a tiny bitmask to skip the search when no candidate can exist, finds reusable results without expensive comparison.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: request-scoped memoization of resolver results keyed by (field, args), as in DataLoader. Memoized selectors. • Data / databases: CTE materialization. Common subexpression elimination across query branches. Caching identical sub-requests in a batch API. • Native / systems: `std::call_once` for per-object lazy setup. Compiler CSE.
Watch out: sharing forces materialization, which blocks streaming and push-down into each consumer. Use it only when there really are several consumers, and the result must be deterministic.

### sqlite-025 — Build a transient index when it beats repeated scans
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: If an inner loop would scan a table once per outer row, build a temporary index once and probe it. Build it only when the loop really repeats, make it covering, make it partial when that gives the same answer, and defer building until the first probe.
Why it is faster: A nested-loop scan is O(n·m). Building an index costs O(m log m) once, then each probe is O(log m). It pays off only when the inner loop runs many times, so a single scan should not build one. Deferring the build until first use costs nothing when the outer loop produces no rows. Non-selective columns make poor indexes and should be refused.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: build a `Map` by key before a nested loop over two collections. Hash-join in application code. • Data / databases: hash joins in dataframes. Memoized lookups in ETL. • Native / systems: build an acceleration structure (grid, BVH) only when the query count justifies it.
Watch out: the build cost is real and paid up front. A wrong loop-count estimate makes this a loss, so tie the decision to cardinality estimates and skip it for single scans.
Seen in SQLite: 8b307fbfa0 2010-04-06 "Automatically generate transient indices for tables in joins that would otherwise have to use a full table scan." 4139c99eba 2010-04-07 makes them covering. 0013e72edf 2010-04-08 drops duplicate columns. • 969e5595e8 2011-07-02 `if( pParse->nQueryLoop<=1 ) return;`: "no point in building an automatic index for a single scan". • cc04afdad0 2013-08-22 defers creation "until the index is actually used", so it is skipped when outer loops yield no rows. • 75bf6b984a 2013-06-17 adds automatic indexes on FROM-clause subqueries. 059b2d50e1 2014-10-24 builds a partial automatic index "when doing so gives the same answer for less work". 9978e6a680 2014-06-17 skips them on constant constraints. • b23f92b286 2024-05-27, 18970dac18 2024-05-28 refuse automatic indexes on non-selective columns. 50c4420030 2025-01-18 extends them to WITHOUT ROWID tables.

### sqlite-026 — Drive choices with measured statistics and a calibrated cost model
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Estimate selectivity from sampled data (histograms, per-column stats) instead of fixed guesses. Include every real cost: the lookup from index to table, row width, inner-loop cost. Calibrate the heuristics against benchmarks and fix systematic misestimates as they are found.
Why it is faster: Plan quality depends on estimates. A wrong join order or index choice can cost orders of magnitude more than any executor tweak. Histograms capture skew that fixed guesses miss. Weighting step costs by row size makes narrow-index scans correctly cheaper. User hints such as `likelihood()` give the planner knowledge it cannot get from stats.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: keep cardinality sketches in a custom query engine. Pick hash vs nested-loop joins in dataframe libraries from real sizes. • Web / backend: route requests or choose cache strategies from measured hit rates and latencies, not constants. • Native / systems: profile-guided branch hints. Allocator size-class tuning from request histograms.
Watch out: stats go stale, and a better estimate can expose a plan that is fast on average but fragile. SQLite fixed several regressions caused by cost changes. Prefer plans with a good worst case when estimates are uncertain.

6cda3ad8feb uses average column width. • 6bb6a62f3cc 2025-03-10 Hash join bucket-size estimates use extended multi-column statistics; 1ea5bdb00bf includes hash-table overhead in memory estimates, which had been off about 3x; a1b4f289bee counts batch-file buffers. • c95cd2991f1 2026-03-19 `scalararraysel()` returns 0 as soon as a NULL is found in a `NOT IN` array, skipping per-element estimation; f84ff0c6d4e checks that both sides have MCV statistics before loading either. • 733f20df537 2026-03-20 Generic index cost estimation subtracts metapage and non-leaf pages, which had caused up to 100% error on small partial indexes. • be69a5ff1fd 2026-07-01 UNION output is estimated as the sum of per-child distinct counts instead of total input rows; 906b1e4a19a propagates distinct-count statistics through GROUP BY subqueries and CTEs, a significant factor in TPC-DS plans.

### sqlite-027 — Do cost arithmetic in small log-scale integers
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Represent costs and row counts as small integers proportional to their logarithm. Multiplying costs becomes adding, overflow cannot happen, and comparison is an integer compare. Add two values with a tiny correction table, and compute the log from float exponent bits or count-leading-zeros.
Why it is faster: Planners multiply and compare huge numbers of estimates. Doubles risk overflow and are slower on some targets. Linear integers overflow quickly. Log-scale integers fit in 16 bits, keep about 10% precision, which is all an estimate deserves, and turn the dominant operation (multiplication) into addition.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: cost-based optimizers in any engine. Log-scale histogram buckets (HdrHistogram). • Native / systems: log-domain probability math (log-sum-exp). Priority computation in schedulers. • Embedded: integer log tables instead of floating point on cores without an FPU.
Watch out: precision is coarse by design. Do not use log-scale values where exact counts matter, such as LIMIT arithmetic or memory sizing.
Seen in SQLite: b8a8e8a5d2 2013-06-10, 8b76656ad4 2013-06-11 "Use a logarithmic rather than linear cost and row-count measures". `whereCostAdd()` uses a difference table. • 23fec451a6 2013-06-07 "Must faster computation of estimated logarithm" via the float's exponent bits. ceb4b1dbdd 2017-08-17 uses `__builtin_clzll` in `sqlite3LogEst`. • cfc9df76e1 2014-04-25 stores sqlite_stat1 values as LogEst in memory.

### sqlite-028 — Bound the planner's own work: fast paths, beam search and budgets
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Plan the common simple query with a special-case routine. For joins, keep the N best partial plans per step (beam search) instead of enumerating everything. Gate expensive analysis passes on cheap summary flags, and cap total search effort.
Why it is faster: Exhaustive join ordering is exponential, and prepare time counts when statements are short-lived. Most real queries are single-table lookups on a unique key, so a fast path skips the general machinery. Beam search gets near-optimal plans in polynomial time. A summary bit recorded while building the input (for example "any OR term?") skips passes that cannot apply. A budget stops pathological queries from planning forever.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: point-lookup fast paths in ORMs and GraphQL layers that skip the general query builder. • Native / systems: beam search in instruction scheduling and route optimization. Time or step budgets in solvers and game AI. • Data / databases: skip optimizer passes whose trigger pattern is absent. Cap planning time relative to expected run time.
Watch out: a narrow beam can miss the best plan. Widening it costs prepare time on every query, so tune it on realistic workloads.

### sqlite-029 — Rewrite to a cheaper equivalent shape
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Transform the request algebraically before planning: derive implied predicates and propagate constants, drop joins that cannot affect output, reduce outer joins to inner joins when NULLs are rejected anyway, turn correlated EXISTS into a semi-join, turn count over a union into a sum of counts, and weaken an operator when a LIMIT hides the difference.
Why it is faster: Each rewrite either removes work outright (an unneeded join, a deduplication) or exposes a cheaper plan: inner joins can be reordered, semi-joins can use indexes and stop at the first match, and derived equalities give extra index constraints. The rewrite runs once at prepare time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: replace N+1 "exists" checks with a join or a Set lookup. Skip joins whose columns the response does not use. • Data / databases: semi-join planning, join elimination and empty-relation propagation in Spark or Presto-style engines. • Native / systems: partial evaluation and algebraic simplification in compilers.
Watch out: each rewrite has narrow legality conditions. SQLite restricted the UNION weakening to LIMIT 1 for soundness. Rewrites also cost prepare time, so gate expensive ones on cheap checks.
Seen in SQLite: f218ee2dd4 2013-01-17, 7a5bcc0f08 2013-01-16 exploit transitivity of join constraints. 660ee55663 2018-07-26 adds WHERE-clause constant propagation, which 7810ab6464 2018-07-27 limits to joins to save prepare time. • 692b9a43dd 2013-06-26, bc71b1d453 2013-06-21 omit LEFT JOIN tables "if their presence makes no difference". • c6c5ab80ee 2018-03-22 reduces LEFT JOIN to inner join when WHERE rejects NULLs on the right. 179c32cb57 2023-06-01, 038158e94a 2023-06-02 generalize this to RIGHT and FULL JOIN. • 5525ac1049 2024-06-07, 9a13a21223 2025-07-08 turn correlated EXISTS into a join term that stops after its first match. 8bc112e3e4 2025-07-05 extends it to EXISTS without WHERE and to nested EXISTS. • af03eb3241 2023-02-22 rewrites `count(*)` over UNION ALL as the sum of per-branch counts. • ccfd5e0941 2026-08-22, 46c7844d76 2026-08-25, 61387ffc4e 2026-08-25 run UNION with LIMIT 1 through the cheaper UNION ALL path, since deduplication cannot change a single-row answer. • b531aa8fd9 2019-03-01 marks WHERE terms implied by a partial index's predicate as already coded. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-023): 9e7e29e6c9d 1999-07-27 LIKE or regex with a fixed prefix becomes `>=`/`<` index quals, and an exact pattern becomes `=` (8ae9ad1cb8c). • bdfbfde1b16 2003-01-20 `IN (subselect)` became a semi-join or a join over DISTINCT. • 290166f9340 2005-11-25 `x IN (list)` became one indexable `= ANY(array)` node instead of an OR tree whose planning cost was superlinear (3d376fce8dd did the parser side). • 8ec5429e2f4 2017-10-08 `X = X` with a strict btree operator becomes `X IS NOT NULL` inside code that already inspected the pattern, giving far better selectivity estimates at almost no cost. • 42473b3b312 2025-11-27 `COUNT(nonnull_expr)` becomes `COUNT(*)`, so the column need not be deformed. • 0a379612540 2026-02-10 `IS DISTINCT FROM` becomes `<>` (and `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` becomes `=`) when both sides are non-nullable;
