WHERE rhs.col IS NULL` on a column that cannot be NULL in matched rows becomes an anti-join; a812cf647a8 uses strict quals inside the right side as proof, 2ce5a57bc0d handles FULL JOIN, and ad4b80b6f49 removes the now-redundant IS NULL quals. • 383eb21ebff 2026-03-12 `x NOT IN (SELECT y ...)` becomes an anti-join when neither side can be NULL, with non-null proofs from NOT NULL constraints and strict expressions.

### sqlite-030 — Use declared constraints to delete runtime work
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: NOT NULL, UNIQUE, index order and literal values are known at compile time. Use them to fold checks to constants, drop "advance to next" steps on unique lookups, skip deduplication, and remove sorts or null handling that cannot matter.
Why it is faster: A runtime check that can never fire still costs a branch per row. A loop on a unique key can match at most one row, so its iterator machinery is dead. Expressions that are always false at parse time make whole branches of the plan unnecessary. The analysis runs once per statement.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `findUnique` instead of `findMany()[0]`. Skip re-validation of data that already passed a typed schema. • Native / systems: type-guided null-check elimination in compilers. • Data / databases: skip dedup or hashing when grouping by a primary key. Drop sort keys after a unique key.
Watch out: the optimization is only as good as the constraint is enforced. A constraint that is declared but not checked (or bypassed by bulk loads) turns these shortcuts into wrong answers.
Seen in SQLite: 95e037ba39 2011-03-09 uses `OP_Noop` instead of OP_Next for a UNIQUE equality loop. e39a732ce2 2014-02-03 lets B-tree Next be a no-op for unique-index equality. f3ea92cceb 2024-05-01 extends this to IN on every column of a unique index. • 553818a0aa 2014-07-23, f8c7e053ed 2014-07-24 make ANALYZE skip change tests on UNIQUE indexes. 0f65cce825 2014-07-22 skips testing the right-most column, which must change if no earlier column did. • d0aa2f3afd 2013-08-20 ignores IS NOT NULL on NOT NULL columns. eca6d60bfa 2021-02-26, 0433789863 2023-03-03 fold `x IS NULL` to a constant when x is NOT NULL. 67e2bb92df 2020-02-27 lets `min(x)` on a NOT NULL indexed column read the index endpoint directly. • 5fb52caadf 2012-03-31 folds AND to 0 when either side is always false. • 9ed1dfa802 2008-01-02 EXISTS subqueries discard DISTINCT, ORDER BY and their result columns. bb30123178 2021-07-15, 0fb78f0cca 2021-07-16 drop ORDER BY in FROM-clause subqueries when it cannot affect output.

### sqlite-031 — Stop as soon as the answer is fixed
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: If an input of a join or set operation is empty, jump straight to the end. If LIMIT is satisfied and rows arrive in order, leave the inner loop. On an inner lookup miss, jump back to the outer loop the miss depends on. Fail equality seeks at the first mismatch.
Why it is faster: Nested loops otherwise finish iterations that cannot change the result. An empty inner table makes the whole inner join empty, but a naive loop still walks every outer row. Backjumping skips the intermediate loops whose iterations cannot fix a miss. These checks cost one branch, and the savings can be whole loop nests.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: short-circuit fan-out when one required input is empty. Stop scanning once a page of results is full. • Native / systems: conflict-directed backjumping in SAT and constraint solvers. • Data / databases: empty-relation propagation in query engines. MapReduce stages skipped for empty partitions.
Watch out: early exits interact badly with outer joins and side effects. A LEFT JOIN must still emit NULL rows, which is why SQLite targets the outermost non-LEFT-JOIN loop.
Seen in SQLite: 3a3b420abb 2017-02-15 detects empty tables in a join and jumps to the halt address of the outermost loop. 33f3273705 2025-07-02, 6245e5a46b 2025-07-02 give each loop level its own halt address. 216676664d 2025-07-02 skips the right side of EXCEPT/INTERSECT when the left is empty (`OP_IfEmpty`). 3d21dcc924 2025-06-30 covers RIGHT JOIN. • 1524c672e9 2016-05-20 exits an inner loop early under ORDER BY … LIMIT. a04a8be2c2 2016-01-13 exits a block-sorted scalar subquery after the first row. • 349d1438eb 2026-09-29, fc96a3c453 2026-10-09, a32ed5cef2 2026-10-10 unwind join loops on a lookup miss (TreeTracker), with separate miss targets on OP_SeekGE/LE. When the key comes from an automatic index, the entry that can never match is also deleted. • 3adf8783b0 2015-11-06 an `OPFLAG_SEEKEQ` seek fails at once on a mismatch and jumps over OP_IdxGT, "saving a call to the key comparison functions".

### sqlite-032 — Evaluate cheap and selective work first; verify expensive terms on survivors
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Order predicates so cheap ones that eliminate the most rows run first, and expensive ones (correlated subqueries, giant posting lists, row fetches) run last, on the candidates that remain. Skip an expensive operand when the cheap one already decides the result.
Why it is faster: An expensive predicate costs per row evaluated. Running it after cheap filters means it sees only the survivors. In a search AND query, loading the posting list of a very common term costs more than re-checking the few candidate documents that the rare terms produced. A NULL operand or a decided AND/OR makes the subquery's value irrelevant.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: check cheap in-memory conditions before calling a remote service. Order middleware so cheap rejections run before expensive auth or I/O. • Data / databases: late materialization in column stores. Trigram-index filter, then regex verification. WAND-style conjunctive search. • Native / systems: bounding-box tests before exact geometry. Lazy evaluation in rule engines.
Watch out: reordering is only legal for side-effect-free terms. Selectivity estimates can be wrong, so measure. A "cheap" term that rarely eliminates anything only adds cost.
Seen in SQLite: bc43995f99 2017-04-29 evaluates WHERE terms that need only index columns before the table seek, "thereby avoid seeking the table row if index terms are false". 90e39ec349 2017-07-10 evaluates "index-covered constraints first, correlated subquery constraints last". • 4f7c5e684a 2010-10-19 FTS: "selectively avoid loading very large doclists". Common terms are checked by re-tokenizing candidate rows. 5b217c308f 2011-06-27 improves the choice of which tokens to defer. c5f21892a8 2011-06-17 avoids loading infrequent phrase terms twice. • e24f20a4f5 2025-06-30, 280559b446 2025-06-30 evaluate the non-subquery operand first and skip the subquery when it is NULL. 99f1aa03fb 2025-06-30 skips scalar subqueries whose result cannot change the expression. ca574fa1d5 2025-09-24 reworks AND/OR short-circuit code generation. • ae6bb9570b 2009-11-11 compiles COALESCE and IFNULL as jumps so later arguments are not evaluated, "a performance win when the unused argument is a subquery".

### sqlite-033 — Put a Bloom filter in front of expensive probes, but only where it can pay
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: While building one side of a join or IN set, also fill a small bit array of key hashes. Test it before each expensive lookup, and skip the lookup on a negative. Build filters only for operators that actually probe, size them from row estimates, and make sure the hash spreads the keys.
Why it is faster: In star-schema joins and selective IN subqueries, most probes find nothing, and each one is a B-tree seek. A Bloom test is a hash plus one bit load and rejects most misses. Running all filters before any seek removes rows as early as possible. The filter costs memory and build time, so it is a loss for one-shot or non-probing uses.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: runtime join filters in distributed SQL. SSTable Bloom filters in LSM stores. • Web / backend: negative caches in front of an RPC, a CDN origin, or a "username taken?" check. • Native / systems: filters in front of disk-resident hash tables or deduplication stores.
Watch out: a Bloom filter bug fails silently. A badly sized or badly hashed filter still returns correct answers, just slowly, as the 1/8-usage bug showed. Export hit and miss counters so the benefit can be checked.
Seen in SQLite: 2db144c33b 2021-12-01 adds Bloom filters to automatic indexes. fa35f5c5a7 2021-12-04 applies them to star-schema queries (`OP_FilterAdd`/`OP_Filter`). 770dade262 2021-12-04 restricts them to equality terms. 50fb7e09b4 2021-12-06 sizes them from sqlite_stat1. 35685d3e5e 2021-12-05, 6ae49e67cc 2021-12-05 run all filters before any seek. 23d41e63f2 2021-12-06 adds hit and miss counters. • 6172e4355f 2024-07-03 builds a 10,000-byte filter while materializing `IN (subquery)`. • 16702ec69d 2023-06-12 fixes a modulus that used bytes instead of bits, so only 1/8 of the filter had been in use. a353369f57 2023-02-28 skips filters on text keys whose collations hash everything to a constant. 83f39c1788 2026-07-23 allows text and blob keys under BINARY collation. • 450a90097f 2026-02-05 stops "generating unnecessary Bloom filters associated with IN operators" on cloned IN subqueries.

### sqlite-034 — Seek over runs of uninteresting rows instead of stepping through them
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: When the leading index column has few distinct values, iterate those values and seek each one (skip-scan). For DISTINCT over an ordered prefix, seek past all duplicates of the current prefix. In multi-column IN lookups, abandon the IN loop as soon as the fixed prefix has no rows.
Why it is faster: Stepping costs one comparison per row, and a seek costs about log N. If a run of skipped rows is longer than log N, seeking wins. Statistics decide whether runs are long enough. If no row matches `a=?`, no IN value for b can match, so checking the prefix once can avoid K wasted seeks.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: loose index scans for DISTINCT and GROUP BY. Group-skipping in time-series stores. • Web / backend: faceted search that checks a prefix before running many filter combinations. • Native / systems: distinct-over-sorted-file by binary search to the next key instead of reading every record.
Watch out: without statistics the break-even is a guess, and SQLite gates these optimizations on stat1 being present. Short runs make seeking slower than stepping.
Seen in SQLite: cd8629e4bb 2013-11-13 adds skip-scan. c964c39fb0 2013-11-27 tunes the threshold "from 50 to 18 … approximate break-even point". f3f69ac942 2014-08-20 handles a skipped middle column. • 1a17b44231 2017-04-14 emits `OP_SeekGT/SeekLT` for ordered DISTINCT when stat1 is present. 839fa6d814 2017-04-13 requires about 12 rows per group: "Otherwise, OP_Next is just as fast". • 8c2b6d784b 2018-06-05 adds `OP_SeekHit`/`OP_IfNoHope` "to reduce the number of unnecessary sqlite3BtreeMovetoUnpacked() calls". 83193d0133 2018-06-07 adds the IN-early-out optimization.

### sqlite-035 — Answer OR with a union of index lookups
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: When each term of an OR can use some index, look up each term separately and merge the row IDs, with deduplication, instead of scanning the whole table.
Why it is faster: One indexable term gives a seek, but an OR of terms on different columns defeats every single index, which forces a full scan. k index lookups plus a dedup set cost O(k log n + matches), versus O(n). The dedup structure should be cheap to insert into and test, such as a chunked row set.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: bitmap-OR index scans. Unions of posting lists in search engines. • Web / backend: in-memory filters that keep one index per field and merge the per-condition ID sets.
Watch out: when one OR term matches most rows, a single scan is cheaper than many lookups plus dedup. The cost model must compare both options.
Seen in SQLite: dd5f5a6231 2008-12-23 "Multi-index OR-clause optimization". 294352578e 2008-12-28 extends it to AND, BETWEEN and LIKE subterms. a7e770612b 2009-01-14 accounts for sort cost. 336a5300de 2009-04-24 moves duplicate handling to RowSet. 1d46146b58 2009-04-21 applies it to virtual tables. • 3526319b48 2014-07-22 adds `OP_ReopenIdx`, a no-op when the same index is already open, for the OR optimization.

### sqlite-036 — Produce only the columns someone reads
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Track, with a bitmask, which fields each consumer references. Load, compute, materialize and sort only those. Replace unused subquery result columns with NULL. Don't store a value twice in sort records.
Why it is faster: Every column produced costs decode, compute and copy work, and in materialized or sorted data it also costs storage and I/O. Dead columns in subqueries can hide expensive expressions. A bitmask of referenced columns is cheap to compute at compile time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: select only the columns a template or API response uses. GraphQL field selection pushed down to SQL. • Data / databases: projection pruning in Spark and dataframe planners. Columnar readers that skip unselected columns. Change capture that emits only touched columns. • Native / systems: tree-shaking unused exports. Dead-store elimination.
Watch out: pruning across set operations and window functions has edge cases. SQLite limits subquery column pruning to UNION ALL and skips it when window functions are present.

### sqlite-037 — Do modifications in one pass and never repeat a seek
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a statement can touch at most one row, or no trigger or constraint needs a stable snapshot, modify rows in the same pass that finds them instead of collecting IDs first. Carry cursor positions from one step to the next, and reuse cursors that are already open on the right object.
Why it is faster: Two-pass DML scans once to collect IDs, then seeks each row again. One pass halves the searches and drops the ID buffer. Within a statement, constraint checks often already positioned the cursor where the insert or delete needs to happen, so a second seek repeats O(log n) work. Reopening an already-open cursor in a loop costs setup per iteration.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: update by key in one statement instead of select-then-update. An identity map so the ORM does not re-fetch rows it already holds. • Data / databases: upserts that reuse the position from the conflict check (insert with a hint). Statement and cursor caches. • Native / systems: file readers that remember the offset instead of seeking before each read.
Watch out: one-pass updates are unsafe when the modification changes the scan's own index order (the Halloween problem) or when triggers read the table. Keep the two-pass path for those cases.
Seen in SQLite: 08c88eb0d3 2008-04-10 uses a one-pass algorithm for single-row updates. e73e067187 2013-11-18 adds one-pass DELETE. e402dc57ae 2015-09-14 uses it whenever no triggers or foreign keys need the rowid-collection pass. • 5c82f4df9f 2013-11-19 avoids seeking the main table in UPDATE when an index suffices. fc8d4f96b4 2013-11-08, 392ee21d1a 2013-11-08 avoid seeks for WITHOUT ROWID UPDATE and REPLACE. ce60aa469a 2013-11-08 removes a NotExists that constraint checks already made unnecessary. • f64ece143c 2017-01-28 marks two-pass UPDATE with `WHERE_SEEK_UNIQ_TABLE` to "avoid redundant table b-tree cursor seeks". ad1d9a8707 2017-01-07, 4da91eed80 2018-04-20, fb2213e1ff 2018-04-20, 3b908d41a0 2016-11-08 remove redundant seeks on REPLACE and UPSERT. • 53ad7e48ab 2014-07-22 adds `OP_ReopenIdx`. 5e71497404 2025-07-01 reuses a virtual-table cursor that is already open on the same table instead of reopening it inside correlated loops.

### sqlite-038 — Specialize plans on bound values, and re-plan only when the value class changes
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a parameter's value changes the best plan (a LIKE prefix, a LIMIT, a range bound), read the bound value during planning and record a dependency. Re-prepare only when a rebinding could change the plan: when the value actually differs, or moves into or out of a special class.
Why it is faster: A generic plan for an unknown value must be safe for every value and is often much worse for the actual one. Seeing the literal lets the planner use the index range or the exact LIMIT. Re-planning on every bind would throw away the prepare cost the statement cache exists to save, so the dependency must be precise.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: custom versus generic plans. Parameter-sensitive plan caches keyed by value class. • Native / systems: JIT specialization on observed constant arguments with deoptimization guards. • Web / backend: compiled template or regex caches keyed by the argument classes that change the compiled form.
Watch out: value-dependent plans make performance less predictable from run to run. SQLite turns this off when plan stability is requested. Over-broad dependencies cause re-prepare storms.
Seen in SQLite: 937d0dea4f 2009-10-15 adds an experimental `sqlite3_reoptimize()`. 1d2ce4f804 2009-10-19 removes it because statements prepared with prepare_v2 now re-prepare automatically when a relevant parameter is bound. c94b859560 2009-10-20 fixes invalidation through clear_bindings and transfer_bindings. • 4703b7d092 2024-06-06 lets `sqlite3ExprIsInteger()` read bound variables (for example LIMIT) at plan time and set the variable mask. It is disabled under the query planner stability guarantee. • c60dd4af07 2026-08-18 adds a mask that re-prepares only when a small-integer parameter changes class. 6f1110ce05 2026-08-21 specializes `sqlite3_bind_int64()` to expire only on a move between {0,1} and other values. 0eaef28cf2 2026-08-22, e037ddd700 2026-10-12 never re-prepare "if the value does not actually change".

### sqlite-039 — Build persistent access paths for the query shapes you expect
Lens: query-planning · Source: SQLite
Principle: Give the planner structures that match frequent query shapes: indexes on prefixes for prefix search, partial indexes on the subset that is queried, and storage order matching the common scan direction. Fall back to a nearby structure when the exact one is missing.
Why it is faster: A prefix query over a term index otherwise merges every matching term's posting list. An index of N-character prefixes answers it with one lookup. A partial index is smaller and cheaper to maintain than a full one. An N+1 prefix index plus the term index still beats a full scan for length-N prefixes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: edge n-gram indexes for autocomplete. Prefix buckets in Redis sorted sets for typeahead. • Data / databases: filtered indexes on "active" rows. Descending clustering order for newest-first timelines.
Watch out: every auxiliary index costs space and write amplification. Build only the prefix lengths and subsets the workload actually queries.
Seen in SQLite: 09643ab745 2011-05-24 adds FTS `prefix=` indexes. 9d1f874ab3 2011-05-25 accepts a list of prefix lengths. 98b08e71f2 2011-05-25 uses the N+1 index for length-N queries. 4e76cc3650 2010-10-20 improves cost estimates for prefix terms. • b47e07f1c1 2013-08-02 adds partial indexes. • 0f599faa29 2011-05-04 optimizes ORDER BY docid on FTS tables, and b46ee91729 2011-06-04 lets tables store doclists in descending order.

### sqlite-040 — Create expensive resources on first real use
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite · Also: Flycast, Tokio, PostgreSQL
Principle: Don't open files, allocate buffers, load pages or build structures when an object is created. Create them the first time something actually needs them, so paths that never need them pay nothing.
Why it is faster: Opening a file costs syscalls and metadata writes. Loading a root page costs I/O. Many transactions turn out to be no-ops, many cursors are never stepped, and many callbacks never need the column array. Deferring creation turns a fixed per-operation cost into a cost paid only by operations that use the resource. A small in-memory buffer that spills to a real file only on overflow avoids the file entirely for small jobs.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: lazy database connections in serverless handlers. Dynamic `import()` of heavy modules on first use. Opening log files on the first write. • Native / systems: spooled temporary files that live in memory below a threshold. Lazy symbol binding. • Embedded: power up peripherals and allocate their buffers only when a feature is first used.
Watch out: laziness adds an "is it created yet?" branch to every access. When the resource is almost always needed, eager creation is faster: SQLite moved page-cache creation back to open time for a "noticable performance boost" (c3031c61ef 2014-08-26), and allocates b-tree scratch space when the first write cursor opens (3fbb022b98 2014-09-24).

0a51e7073c0 lets them skip ProcArrayLock at exit since they do not affect anyone's snapshot. • 6cb4afff33b 2014-10-29 Invalidation-message structures are allocated only for (sub)transactions that queue messages; ### sqlite-041 — Defer the expensive fetch until a row survives filtering
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite
Principle: Record where the full record lives, but fetch it only when a column that requires it is actually read. Evaluate everything that can be answered from cheaper data first. When sorting, carry a reference and fetch large columns after the sort.
Why it is faster: A table seek from a secondary index costs a full tree search. Rows skipped by OFFSET, rejected by index-only predicates or answered from the index never need it. With large OFFSETs this saves orders of magnitude. Sorting only keys plus a reference moves far less data through an external sort than sorting full wide rows.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: lazy-loaded ORM relations fetched only after filtering. Virtual scrolling that loads row details only when rendered. • Data / databases: late materialization in column stores. Sort IDs, then fetch documents for the page shown. Deferred joins for ORDER BY … LIMIT. • Native / systems: archive readers that seek to an entry only when its data is read.
Watch out: if most rows end up fetched anyway, the bookkeeping is pure overhead, and fetching after a sort turns sequential access into random access. Gate sorter references on column size.
Seen in SQLite: a11846b77a 2004-01-07 adds deferred seek: `OP_MoveTo` records the target, and the real seek happens on first data access. "On an indexed order search with a large OFFSET, queries times can be an order of magnitude faster." • 21de2e755c 2007-11-29 reads any column available in the index from the index, so the deferred seek often never happens. 6149526c25 2009-04-22 lets OP_Rowid read the rowid from a pending deferred seek without performing it. • 68c0c71065 2020-08-14 makes UPDATE and DELETE always use `OP_DeferredSeek`, emitting `OP_FinishSeek` only after all WHERE terms pass. 861889e4f8 2020-08-14 avoids "unnecessary main table seeks" for multi-column indexes. • dedff6be8a 2018-04-18 adds sorter references: columns larger than a threshold are fetched from the table after sorting instead of being carried through the sort.

### sqlite-042 — Answer from metadata without loading the payload
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: If the consumer only needs the type, length, null-ness or key of a value, read it from the header and never load the content. Tag the read operation with what the consumer actually needs.
Why it is faster: Large values span overflow pages, so loading them means extra I/O and copying, which can be megabytes per row. A record header already holds type and length. `length()`, `typeof()`, `IS NULL` and `octet_length()` can be answered in constant time regardless of value size. One flag on the column-read instruction carries the information.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HEAD and range requests. `stat()` instead of reading a file to learn its size. Content-Length checks before downloading. • Data / databases: answering counts and null checks from Parquet page statistics. `EXISTS` probes that select a constant. • Native / systems: length-prefixed formats that let readers skip values without decoding them.
Watch out: the shortcut must produce exactly what loading would have, including encoding conversions and corrupt-record handling, or `length()` will disagree with the real value.
Seen in SQLite: c461a455c4 2012-03-28 sets `OPFLAG_LENGTHARG`/`OPFLAG_TYPEOFARG` on OP_Column: "Avoid loading overflow pages just to satisfy typeof() or length()." • 921acff927 2022-10-13 emits a typeof hint before `OP_IsNull`/`OP_NotNull`, which "avoids loading large strings or blobs off of disk". • 077efc2730 2023-06-22 reads `octet_length()` from the record header via `OPFLAG_BYTELENARG`. 4bf24c8830 2024-11-04 `sqlite3_preupdate_old()` returns the integer primary key without unpacking the old record. • 433af7ab93 2024-05-14 fts5vocab skips computing "cnt" when it is unused. f5a9b58c8b 2024-08-27 fts5's xConnect no longer reads the structure record. • 9f8952390a 2018-01-24 unpacks sorter output starting from the rightmost column, so the record header is parsed once and later reads hit cached offsets. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-048): 03194432de7 2002-03-05 TOAST slicing fetches only the out-of-line chunks a substring needs; 4d0e994eed8 decompresses only the needed prefix and 11a078cf87f fetches only the compressed chunks that prefix requires. • 84fe8990ae9 2007-09-26 Copying an already compressed external value no longer decompresses it, and values too short to compress are skipped early; 7fdca537117 stopped decompressing unchanged inline-compressed fields on UPDATE. • c38b75947e0 2009-08-23 Index runtime keys are detoasted once up front; repeated detoasting had caused "order-of-magnitude slowdown" reports. • 1b393f4e5db 2011-01-18 `texteq`, `textne`, `byteaeq` and `byteane` compare raw sizes first, which are available without decompression or fetching. • f84ff0c6d4e 2022-11-18 Join selectivity checks that both sides have MCV statistics before loading either, which is expensive at high statistics targets.

### sqlite-043 — Skip derived-state maintenance when the inputs did not change
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite
Principle: When an update modifies some fields, redo only the derived work that depends on those fields: indexes, constraints, foreign keys, counters. If a new key is byte-identical to the stored one, skip the delete and insert altogether.
Why it is faster: Index maintenance, constraint checks and foreign-key lookups each cost tree searches and writes. Most updates touch a few columns, so most derived structures are unaffected. A compile-time check of which columns change, or a cheap runtime comparison, removes that work. A zero counter of deferred violations shows that a search for a fixed violation is pointless.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORMs that write only dirty columns. Validators that rerun only the rules for changed fields. Memoized UI components that skip unchanged props. • Data / databases: incremental view maintenance. Search indexers that skip unchanged documents. Triggers that test `OLD.col IS DISTINCT FROM NEW.col`. • Native / systems: incremental builds that rebuild only dependents of changed inputs, or hash outputs to stop propagation.
Watch out: the dependency analysis must be complete. A missed dependency (for example an expression index that references a column through a function) leaves derived state stale, which is silent corruption.
Seen in SQLite: e7a94d8128 2009-10-01 omits foreign-key processing when an UPDATE modifies no key columns. 788536b165 2009-09-23 runs ON UPDATE actions only if a value really changed. 0ff297eaa7 2009-09-25 `OP_FkIfZero` skips the "did this delete fix a violation?" search when the counter is zero. 8ff2d9561a 2013-09-05 skips FK actions for columns the UPDATE cannot modify. • 0466883300 2014-12-16, 1ac5fed3a7 2014-12-17 skip the parent lookup when the child key is guaranteed NULL, and avoid a statement journal for CASCADE, SET NULL and deferred FKs. • 2a0b527b37 2016-02-10 omits CHECK constraints whose columns an UPDATE does not modify. • e9816d82a7 2018-09-15 skips expression indexes that reference no updated column. 40b4e7a692 2018-09-17 does the same for partial indexes. 6974cdcec7 2018-03-20 skips writing sqlite_sequence when an explicit rowid is below the current max. • 2a07c6de2c 2026-04-22, e17ab401f0 2026-04-22 compare the current index entry with the new key during UPDATE and skip the delete and insert on a match, using an extra conditional jump so the hot insert opcode stays branch-free. • 87c52b58f0 2008-07-19 cuts pointer-map updates during rebalancing to entries that changed. 6067a9bbfe 2009-06-09 keeps `nFree` valid so `balance_quick()` skips a full page re-init.

### sqlite-044 — Don't produce what nobody consumes
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Audit for work whose results are ignored: statements compiled only to be discarded, validations whose output is unused, objects that can never be used in this mode, and copies or conversions done eagerly for a consumer that may never ask.
Why it is faster: Wasted work hides in setup and housekeeping paths that look necessary. Removing it changes nothing in behavior and saves its whole cost. Eager conversions such as NUL-terminating every result column move the cost from the few consumers that need it to every row.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: drop computed fields no client reads. Decode a DB driver's string columns only when accessed. Skip post-migration validation queries whose output nobody checks. • Native / systems: skip codegen for declarations only used for type information. Don't build write-path state on read replicas. • Data / databases: ETL steps that compute columns later dropped.
Watch out: "unused" must be proven for every caller. Removing an eager conversion moves the cost to consumers, which then need their own conversion logic.

### sqlite-045 — Don't encode what you are about to decode
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite
Principle: Pass already-decoded values between components instead of serializing them into a record for the callee to parse back. Work directly on the original bytes (pointer plus length plus encoding) instead of copying them into a canonical form. When copying between same-shaped containers, move encoded records without decoding them.
Why it is faster: A serialize and deserialize round trip costs two passes, an allocation and a copy, and produces nothing new. Parsers that require NUL termination or one specific encoding force a copy just to meet the interface. Raw record transfer between identical schemas skips decode and encode for every field.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in-process module calls should pass objects, not JSON strings. Parse numbers from a buffer slice instead of `toString()` first. • Native / systems: `string_view`/`&str` parsing APIs. Zero-copy formats (FlatBuffers, Cap'n Proto). • Data / databases: byte-level SSTable copy during compaction. Table rewrites that move serialized rows without decoding them.
Watch out: pointers into another component's buffer are only valid while that buffer is not moved or freed. Ephemeral references need a clear lifetime rule (here: until the cursor moves).

### sqlite-046 — Defer work until it is certainly needed, and skip work a later step redoes
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite
Principle: Move costly steps past the cheap tests that might make them unnecessary. Don't compute values that a later phase will recompute anyway, such as checksums before an overwrite or a copy of data that might not move.
Why it is faster: Work done before an early exit is wasted whenever the exit fires. Work redone later is always wasted. Reordering so that the expensive step comes after the decision point, or starts at the first item that actually needs it, removes that waste without changing results.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: recompute a Merkle root or ETag once per batch, not per change. Lay out once after a batch of DOM mutations. • Native / systems: copy-on-write buffers that copy only on the first real write. • Data / databases: build a join hash table only after the probe side proves non-empty.
Watch out: "a later step will redo it" is an invariant across modules. If someone removes the later step, the deferred work silently never happens.

This removes two type calls in the common case.

### sqlite-047 — Validate once, at the boundary or on the slow path
Lens: laziness · Source: SQLite · Also: Netty
Principle: If a check's result cannot change between calls, perform it once and skip it on re-entry. If a check only matters when expensive work happens (a cache miss, a page beyond the local buffer), move it onto that path. Validate arguments once outside a loop and call an unchecked inner routine.
Why it is faster: Repeated validation on hot paths costs branches and loads on every call even though it never fails after the first. Hits vastly outnumber misses in a cache, so checks placed on the miss path are almost free. Binding layers can validate an index once and then loop over a fast internal accessor.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: verify a JWT once per request, not in every handler. Validate at the API boundary and trust typed data inside. • Native / systems: bounds-check a buffer at creation, then use unchecked access in proven-safe loops. Checked and unchecked FFI entry points. • Data / databases: validate keys only on cache miss.
Watch out: moving a safety check off the hot path is safe only if nothing on that path can violate the property. Corrupt-input handling in particular must still be reachable.
Seen in SQLite: 7ad3eb63cf 2016-10-24 `moveToRoot()` skips root-page validation when the cursor already holds the root. 848a3326f7 2015-10-16 checks for an oversized header only when the header extends beyond the local page. cbed604f52 2016-12-13 checks for illegal page 0 only when creating a page. • eac750e9a6 2022-02-23 moves the `pgno==0` corruption check after the page-cache miss, so cache hits skip it. • d457639847 2026-09-18 The JS oo1 API validates a column index once and then uses an internal `__stmtGetColumn()` that skips validation inside loops. • Also seen in Netty (netty-028): 25f96b1644 2013-07-22 `DefaultChannelPromise` sets a `validated` flag to "reduce overhead during writes because of ChannelPromise validation". • c4130e0cf7 2013-11-13 Added a `validateHeaders` flag to HTTP decoders, messages and headers, reported as a 5k performance improvement when disabled. 8edfde3f26 2014-07-11 added the same for SPDY. • 6e108cb96a 2015-10-30 Copying between headers of the same kind skips name validation and rehashing. • 03ad809a3b 2019-10-23 The HPACK encoder checks whether a header is sensitive first, and uses the fast early-exit comparison for the rest. • 97d66ccf7a 2022-08-31 `DefaultCompositeBuffer.extendWith` trusts the ownership invariant (inputs are distinct and owned), so it drops a duplicate check and a concatenating iterator: extending with 64 buffers went from 16,571 to 34,222 ops/s. • 3b81f2c294 2024-01-12 Known HTTP/2 pseudo-header names are looked up before validation, so they skip it.

### sqlite-048 — Write immutable batches and merge them, instead of updating large structures in place
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite
Principle: Collect new entries into small sorted segments and merge segments into larger ones over time, rather than rewriting a big structure on every insert. Merge equal-sized batches like a binary counter so each element is merged only O(log n) times.
Why it is faster: Updating a large postings list or tree in place for each insert costs work proportional to the structure's size per update. Writing small immutable segments costs work proportional to the batch, and merging equal-sized segments gives each element O(log n) total merge work. Bigger first-level batches, such as one per transaction, reduce merge work further. Queries pay by consulting several segments.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: LSM trees (RocksDB, Cassandra). Lucene segment merging. The Bentley-Saxe logarithmic method for turning static structures dynamic. • Web / backend: append events to a log and compact periodically instead of rewriting aggregate documents on every event. • Embedded: flash-friendly logs that append records and compact in the background instead of erasing blocks per update.
Watch out: reads must check every live segment, so query cost grows with segment count. Without a merge policy, reads degrade over time. Merges themselves cause latency spikes unless they are made incremental (next entry).
Seen in SQLite: 8a235d4d3b 2006-10-12 fts2: "Groups of documents form segments … This performs 20x-25x faster than fts1 when loading the Enron corpus, and is only slightly slower for queries." • 06c69d2ed6 2007-03-29 buffers full-text updates per transaction rather than per update: "the total runtime is cut almost in half". • 3343b43915 2012-04-05 RowSet keeps a forest of sorted trees whose sizes grow like a binary counter, merging only equal-level trees. This speeds up workloads with many cycles between RowSetInsert and RowSetTest. 733bf1b1e2 2009-04-22 gave RowSet O(1) insert and O(N log N) sorting once per batch, with better worst-case bounds than the hash it replaced.

### sqlite-049 — Split big maintenance jobs into bounded, resumable steps
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite
Principle: Instead of merging or compacting everything at once, do a bounded amount of work per step, persist the progress, and resume later. The cost spreads across ordinary writes and no single write triggers a huge stall.
Why it is faster: Total work is about the same, but its distribution changes. A "crisis merge" of all segments makes one unlucky write wait for minutes, while bounded steps keep every write's latency predictable. Persisting the merge cursor means progress survives restarts, and other work can interleave with the steps.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: LSM compaction throttling. Background index rebuilds in chunks. Online schema migrations that copy rows in batches. • Native / systems: incremental garbage collection (tri-color marking slices). Incremental rehashing in hash tables (as in Redis). • Web / backend: cron-style cleanup jobs that process N rows per run with a stored cursor.
Watch out: if steps are too small, merging falls behind ingestion and the backlog grows without bound. Tie step size to write volume.
Seen in SQLite: 4ab6f2b9c1 2012-03-15 merges a minimum number of segments at a time. e81eaec754 2012-03-22 stores the merge context in `%_stat` so it can resume. • 5da0aa1603 2012-03-27 runs concurrent incremental merges "to prevent a large crisis-merge". 4ef9dfff97 2012-03-24 adds automatic incremental merge.

### sqlite-050 — Maintain aggregates and membership incrementally instead of scanning
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Keep a running sum, a per-slot count, a compact list of live entries or a sorted window up to date at each modification. Then questions like "which pages are dirty?", "is any cursor shared?" or "can this lock be taken?" are answered in O(1) or O(changed) instead of O(all).
Why it is faster: A scan at query time costs work proportional to the total size, even when almost nothing changed. An incremental update costs O(1) per modification. Commit then scales with what changed, not with cache size. A lock decision scales with lock slots, not with the number of connections. Choosing a cheaper aggregate (a sum of reference counts instead of a count of referenced items) can remove even the per-update zero-crossing check.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: dirty tracking in a unit-of-work ORM. Running counters instead of `COUNT(*)` on every page view. • Native / systems: reader-writer locks with counters instead of owner lists. Dirty-rectangle tracking in renderers. Dense sets with swap-remove. • Data / databases: rolling medians and sliding-window rate limiters. Incremental materialized aggregates.
Watch out: every mutation path must update the aggregate, or it drifts from reality. SQLite keeps a debug-only full scan (`assertLockingArrayOk()`) to cross-check its lock counts. Use the same trick for any incremental aggregate.
Seen in SQLite: 605ad8fb69 2006-05-03 links pages onto a dirty list when first modified instead of scanning every cached page at commit ("Faster :memory: database COMMITs"). • 95a0b37156 2015-09-03 replaced the count of referenced pages with `nRefSum`, the sum of all reference counts, "for a performance improvement and size reduction". • d49c0c83ef 2020-08-29 keeps a per-slot shared-memory lock count (-1 for exclusive) instead of scanning every connection on each lock operation, for "hundreds of connections to a single database within the same process". • 6918095d81 2015-06-25 `hasIncrblobCur` lets invalidation return at once in the usual case. 27fb746cde 2015-06-30 sets `BTCF_Multiple` only when two cursors share a root, so `saveAllCursors()` scans only then. • bea119cdeb 2016-04-11, 9b40d13f43 2016-09-30 keep valid column-cache entries compacted at the front with a count, so invalidation loops only over live entries. • ad8ec9db63 2024-08-31 percentile window functions sort once and then keep the buffer sorted with insertion and removal, instead of re-sorting for every row. • Also seen in curl (curl-005): 159834171e 2006-08-02 `num_alive` incremented on add and decremented on completion; `*running_handles = multi->num_alive` replaces a per-call loop. • 1bd4b3f4e2 2021-06-08 Cookie jar keeps `next_expiration`, and `remove_expired()` returns early when `now < next_expiration`: removal "needs to be a fast operation since we want to be able to perform it often and speculatively". • 89ca8fe80a 2026-09-09 The client writer keeps a running total of buffered bytes plus a tail pointer instead of rescanning its chunk list. • d7928029fc 2025-12-04 A per-connection sparse bitset of attached transfers became a `uint32_t` counter once nothing traversed it.

### sqlite-051 — Check rare conditions periodically, not on every step
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite
Principle: Poll for interrupts, progress callbacks and rare error flags only at loop back-edges or every N units of work. Have the code that sets a rare condition also raise a flag the loop already polls, so the rare condition costs nothing per iteration.
Why it is faster: A check on every instruction of an interpreter adds a load and a branch to the hottest loop in the program. Checking only when control jumps backward still bounds response latency, because every long-running computation must loop. Folding a rare flag into an existing poll removes its separate test entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: check a cancellation token every N iterations. Throttle progress updates to every 100 ms. • Native / systems: safepoint polls on loop back-edges in VMs. One "work pending" mask tested on syscall exit. • Embedded: kick the watchdog in the main loop, not in every function.
Watch out: moving a check changes when it fires. SQLite's first attempt broke reentrant cases and was backed out. Make sure every long path still reaches a polling point.
Seen in SQLite: 49afe3aaa0 2013-07-10 moves the progress-callback check to a shared label reached only from loop opcodes (OP_Next, OP_Prev and others), testing a counter difference. • fabe393da8 2016-02-03 moves the out-of-memory check from every opcode to that label: "about a 0.5% performance increase". e514f651d0 2016-02-04 backs it out because it mishandled reentrancy. 4a642b6060 2016-02-05 reinstates it properly: `sqlite3OomFault()` sets the interrupt flag the loop already polls. The merge 0539702b8e 2016-02-05 reports "almost 1% performance gain". • 94effc8a05 2023-01-12 fires the progress check in label resizing only when the label count crosses a new multiple of 100.

### sqlite-052 — Reset many flags in O(1) with a generation counter
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite
Principle: Instead of clearing an array of "done" flags at every run, store in each slot the generation number when it was set. A slot counts as set only if it equals the current generation. Starting a new run means incrementing one counter.
Why it is faster: Clearing N flags costs O(N) per run even when only a few are used. A generation compare costs the same as a flag test, and a reset costs one increment. The flag array can disappear entirely when the slot already has a spare field to hold the stamp.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: visited marks in graph searches reused across queries. Frame-number invalidation in game engines. • Web / backend: generation-tagged caches that are invalidated by bumping a version. • Data / databases: epoch-based invalidation of per-statement or per-transaction state.
Watch out: counters wrap around. Handle overflow with a full reset, as SQLite does at its threshold, or a stale slot will eventually match a new generation.
Seen in SQLite: 9e5eb9c8cc 2016-09-18 `OP_Once` compares its own P1 with a counter on OP_Init that is incremented at each run. The `aOnceFlag[]` array and its memset in frames were removed, and all P1 values are reset only when the counter passes a threshold. "smaller, faster, and uses less memory".

### sqlite-053 — Generate in blocks and serve from a buffer
Lens: amortization · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: When a producer has per-call overhead, generate a whole block of output per call and hand it out from a buffer. Fold per-item helper calls into one bulk loop that loads shared state and takes locks once.
Why it is faster: A byte-at-a-time generator pays function call, state loads and mutex costs per byte. A block function amortizes them over 64 bytes or more and runs a tight loop the compiler can optimize. The same holds for any producer with setup cost per call.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: fill one buffer with `crypto.getRandomValues` instead of many small calls. Generate IDs in batches. • Native / systems: block RNGs (ChaCha8, AES-CTR) with buffered output. Vectorized UDFs instead of per-row calls. • Data / databases: bulk-insert and multi-get APIs instead of per-item round trips.
Watch out: buffered output is state. For a cryptographic generator, buffered bytes must be protected and discarded on reseed or fork.
Seen in SQLite: 9113c87ef3 2022-08-16 The PRNG moved from byte-at-a-time RC4 to ChaCha20 with a 64-byte output buffer: "3x faster". • cf5ff12105 2013-08-21 inlines `randomByte()` into the `sqlite3_randomness()` loop so shared state and the lock are handled once per request. • Also seen in curl (curl-043): de3969f8fe 2026-10-24 `Curl_rand_bytes()` fills the whole buffer with one `Curl_ssl_random()` call, and the WebSocket encoder keeps a 256-byte mask pool refilled every 64 frames. ns per frame: 16 B 1,529 to 789 (-48%), 1 KB 1,646 to 899 (-45%), 16 KB 3,549 to 2,741 (-23%).

### sqlite-054 — Shrink hot structs: group fields by size and put hot fields first
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: Order fields so the compiler inserts no padding, put the fields the hot path touches (or copies, or must initialize) at the front, and move build-time-only or rarely used fields out of runtime objects.
Why it is faster: Mixing 4-byte integers between 8-byte pointers leaves holes on 64-bit targets. Removing them shrinks every instance, so more objects fit per cache line and per allocation, and memset or memcpy move fewer bytes. Putting the fields that are copied or initialized together in a contiguous prefix lets one partial `memcpy` or `memset(offsetof(...))` handle them all. On x86, a field at an offset under 128 also encodes as a 1-byte displacement, so code that touches hot fields gets smaller. The gains per change are small (often 0.1 to 1%) but they add up, and they come with lower memory use.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In Go, order struct fields largest first (tools like `fieldalignment` report the waste). In JS, keep hot objects small and with a stable shape so they stay in inline properties. • Native / systems: Put hot fields in the first cache line of kernel or engine structs. Split builder types from the immutable runtime type so the runtime object carries no construction-only fields. • Embedded: Pack protocol and state structs by size. Every byte saved per instance counts on a microcontroller with kilobytes of RAM. • Data / databases: Lay out tuple headers and in-memory row structs so fixed-size fields come first and copy as one block.
Watch out: Reordering for padding can split fields that are used together across cache lines, so measure on the real workload. Layout changes are easy to undo by accident; a static assert on `sizeof` keeps the savings.
Seen in SQLite: 6d156e464d 2005-05-20 Reordered `struct Pager` so debug counters and function pointers (`nHit`, `xDestructor`, `xCodec`) come after the hot `u8` flags, "to reduce code footprint". • 3fb757b487 2012-02-02 Reordered fields of `VdbeFrame`, `sqlite3_context` and `VdbeSorter` to remove padding. • 6ec6549158 2012-09-13 Moved `Expr.nHeight` next to the other ints: 8 bytes smaller on 64-bit. d815f17dab 2012-09-13 made `Table` 16 bytes smaller by "avoiding intermixing pointer fields with integer fields" and narrowing two fields to `i16`. • b376daeb67 2013-01-01 Shrank `Index` by 8 bytes. 14da87f8c5 2013-11-20 shrank `VdbeCursor` from 144 to 120 bytes. 7df89c8c8a 2014-02-04 repacked `Parse` for an 8% size reduction on x64. • 73d5b8f550 2013-12-23 Moved fields used only while preparing a statement out of `Vdbe` and into `Parse`, so the long-lived object does not carry them. • 035e563bf6 2014-09-16 Reordered `Mem` so the fields a shallow copy needs (`flags`, `n`, `r`, `z`, `zMalloc`) come first and one prefix copy suffices. • 45ac1c70b3 2015-12-18 Dropped `CellInfo.iOverflow`, taking the struct from 32 to 24 bytes, "for a small performance increase and library size reduction". a3f108e93d 2015-08-26 cut `WhereScan` by 24 bytes. • 0150c81cd3 2022-02-28 Put `z, n, flags, enc, eSubtype` first in `Mem` and the cold fields after (`MEMCELLSIZE` became `offsetof(Mem,db)`): about 750K cycles saved for 4 bytes of code. 4296357c90 2022-02-28 confirmed the gain came from the field order and the init loop, not a memcpy trick. • 80c438613a 2023-08-08 Reordered `Parse` so it "packs more tightly and uses less memory and less CPU to initialize".

### sqlite-055 — Overlap mutually exclusive fields and move rare fields to side objects
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: If two fields are never valid at the same time, store them in a union with a tag. If only some instances need a group of fields, allocate those fields separately, or allocate only the prefix of the struct that a given instance uses.
Why it is faster: The common object gets smaller, so it costs less to allocate, copy, zero and cache. Code often gets simpler too: when two pointers can never both be set, walkers and destructors test one of them instead of both. The cost is a tag check, and for side objects an extra pointer hop that only the rare instances pay.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Model variants as discriminated unions (TypeScript, Rust enums) instead of one record type full of nullable fields. • Native / systems: Use tagged unions or NaN-boxing for interpreter values and AST nodes, and keep rare extensions behind a pointer. • Embedded: Use variant records for protocol messages so RAM holds only the live variant. • Data / databases: Keep cold attributes of a hot entity in a side table or side map (rarely set DOM properties, cold ECS components).
Watch out: A union without an enforced tag invites type confusion; assert the invariant in debug builds. A side object only pays if the fields really are rare, since it adds an allocation and a pointer chase for instances that do use them.
Seen in SQLite: 6ab3a2ec8a 2009-02-19 Expression nodes are allocated at one of four sizes (`EXPR_FULLSIZE`, `EXPR_REDUCEDSIZE`, `EXPR_TOKENONLYSIZE`, `EXPR_SPANONLYSIZE`), flagged in the node, with `x.pList` and `x.pSelect` in a union. This reduces the heap used by schema objects such as triggers and views. • 74eaba4de2 2014-09-18 Moved `Mem.r` (the double) into the same union as the integer, with the invariant that MEM_Int and MEM_Real are never both set. • d10866798d 2017-07-07 Exploited the fact that `Expr.pRight` and `Expr.x` are never both set, so walkers and deleters test only one. • 25c3b8caf4 2018-04-16 Grouped seldom-used `NameContext` fields into a union. eda079cd2c 2018-09-20 put `Expr.pTab` and `Expr.pWin` in a union: 8 bytes smaller, less code, and "over 1 million cycles" faster. • 75fa266341 2020-09-28 Unioned `WhereTerm.iField` with `leftColumn`. f38524d20d 2021-08-02 split `Table` into table-only, view-only and vtab-only union members. d44f8b2385 2022-04-07 unioned ON and USING, since a join has only one: "faster and uses less memory". • 8797bd695f 2024-08-17 Combined `SrcItem` fields into a union. 1521ca4c20 2024-08-19 moved subquery fields out of `SrcItem` into a separately allocated `Subquery` object, which "reduces the memory requirements for SrcItem and makes the code run faster" for simple queries. • Also seen in curl (curl-051): 4ac2884003 2019-09-03 The `generic` protocol pointer replaced with dedicated typed pointers. • a95a6ce6b8 2020-11-23 `void *protop` became the union `data->req.p`, touching 22 files.

### sqlite-056 — Pack booleans into a flag word and size each field to its range
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Store related booleans as bits of one integer, use the narrowest integer type that holds the field's real range, and group flags that are copied together so one assignment moves them. Keep a whole byte for a single flag that the hot path sets on its own.
Why it is faster: A flag word is smaller than a row of `u8` booleans, and several flags can be tested, set or cleared in one AND or OR. Narrow types shrink the struct. The trade-off: a C bitfield costs a read-modify-write and extra mask and shift instructions on every access, so a flag the hot path writes often is faster as a whole byte.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Store permissions and feature toggles as a bitmask column or integer, not a set of boolean columns or properties. • Native / systems: Use one state word per object (as React fibers do) and test combined "needs work" masks with a single AND. • Embedded: Mirror hardware status registers as one word. Choose `uint8_t`/`uint16_t` by range, but keep counters at native width where arithmetic dominates. • Data / databases: Pack null bitmaps and row flags into header bytes.
Watch out: Bitfields in memory shared between threads cause lost updates, because neighbouring bits share a word. Narrow integer types can force sign- or zero-extension code; SQLite at one point widened parser types back to `int` to save bytes of code.
Seen in SQLite: 24fb627afa 2009-05-01 Stole one bit of a token's length for its "quoted" flag. a9121e4c8e 2008-02-19 used `u16` for in-page indexes and cell sizes, and 8677d30812 2009-11-04 made the variable-number type 16-bit unless more variables are allowed. • c91663499e 2012-01-05 Folded five `BtShared` booleans into `btsFlags`, so clearing two of them is one `&= ~(BTS_EXCLUSIVE|BTS_PENDING)`. 3343b43915 2012-04-05, a51009b251 2012-05-21 and a371ace426 2012-09-13 did the same for RowSet, NameContext and Column. • 1fd522ff49 2013-11-21 Went the other way: `VdbeCursor` bitfields became `u8` "to help the code to run a little faster". 036dbec01c 2014-03-11 combined `BtCursor` booleans into `curFlags` so two validity bits clear in one operation. • 2c3629e1c9 2020-03-21 Promoted `Vdbe.doingRerun` from a bitfield to `u8` for "slightly smaller code and better performance". 17c4865b83 2022-04-01 replaced a 32-bit magic number with a dense `u8` state enum. • d88fd53989 2022-05-02 Moved `ExprList_item` flags into a `.fg` substructure so duplication copies them in one assignment: "Faster and smaller code". • 23e57bc7cd 2026-02-13 Turned five rarely used `u8` flags of `Parse` into 1-bit fields to shrink the region that must be zeroed on every use. • Also seen in curl (curl-045): 62a2534e4c 2019-02-25 Every `bool` in urldata.h became `bit x:1` (with `typedef unsigned int bit`) and moved to the end of its struct: `Curl_easy` shrank from 6312 to 6000 bytes (-4.9%, gcc 8, x86_64). • e76b058f3c 2021-01-26 IP version stored in a single byte; 5ad377718d shrank `Curl_chunker` from 48 to 32 bytes on x86_64. • 3622bf4e79 2022-07-04 Timeouts changed from `long` to `unsigned int`/`int`, alongside 3fa343a35c (`buffer_size`) and ccc8092b05 (DNS cache timeout). • 15f7feac22 2022-08-08 `connectdata` fields sorted by descending size with char fields last, to "reduce the number of holes". • 1485e89213 2023-01-01 `BIT()` instead of `bool`, FTP state as `unsigned char`, `ftp_conn` sorted by size, IMAP/POP3/SMTP structs shrunk; d881b91133 removed 16 more by replacing `long` with fixed-width types. • fe703df949 2026-08-14 `SingleRequest`, `UserDefined` and `PureInfo` sorted by size with enums narrowed to `uint8_t`; ec0d5bb696 re-sorted `connectdata`; ### sqlite-057 — Encode each stored value in the fewest bytes, and omit what can be inferred
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: Offer enough size classes that each value uses the smallest width that holds it, store sorted IDs as deltas, and leave out bytes the reader can reconstruct (terminators, counts, trailing defaults). Keep values in their compact form when you rewrite them.
Why it is faster: On-disk and wire size drives everything downstream: more rows per page, fewer pages read, less cache pressure, less decode work. A missing size class (no 3- or 6-byte integer) pushes many common values into the next larger format. Implied fields cost nothing to store. The cost is a slightly more complex decoder, which is cheap next to the I/O it saves.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Use varint/zigzag wire formats (protobuf) and omit default-valued fields; length-prefix strings instead of terminating them. • Embedded: Pack telemetry with int24 and 6-byte millisecond timestamps instead of rounding up to 32/64 bits. • Data / databases: Delta-encode sorted IDs and timestamps in postings lists and time-series columns; keep dictionary or varint encodings intact through compaction. • Native / systems: Allocate small dense IDs from a free list so keys stay short and local.
Watch out: A file or wire format is forever; each new size class must stay decodable by every future reader. Delta encoding forces sequential decode, so add restart points if you need random access.
Seen in SQLite: 6f11bef7d6 2004-05-13 Moved more payload to overflow pages, "thus wasting less disk space". b4964b723c 2004-05-18 dropped the trailing NUL on stored UTF-8 strings and the row-count prefix in each record. • d8820e80fc 2004-05-18 Let the 9th byte of a varint carry 8 full bits, capping the length at 9 bytes instead of 10. • a19b775db9 2004-05-30 Added 3-byte and 6-byte integer serial types: "makes databases smaller and faster". 5284a05316 2008-05-08 fixed the 6-byte range constant so values up to 2^47 no longer spilled into 8 bytes. • 4240240f12 2006-09-11 FTS2 reuses deleted rowids, keeping IDs numerically small (shorter varints) and new rows on recently touched pages. • de163af26e 2006-11-13 Delta-encoded FTS doc IDs before varint coding: "around 22% reduction in index size", 5 to 6% faster. • a83ccca794 2009-04-28 Stopped storing the terminator of symbol-table names. • 585ce1923c 2017-01-25 Records omit trailing NULL columns beyond the last column with a non-NULL default, "for a reduced disk footprint"; readers treat missing columns as NULL. • dcc121999c 2024-03-26 DROP COLUMN copies REAL-affinity columns as NUMERIC so integer values are not widened "to the full 8-byte representation" during the rewrite.

### sqlite-058 — Use a flat, directly searchable representation instead of a pointer structure
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: Store data in a compact contiguous encoding that can be searched and edited in place, such as a sorted offset array inside a page or a binary document format, instead of building a linked or pointer-rich structure from it on every load.
Why it is faster: Building a node tree or pointer array at load time costs allocations and a full parse, and following pointers costs cache misses. A flat encoding can be binary-searched or walked where it sits, can be stored as is, and needs no rebuild. The trade-off is that in-place edits may need memmove, and the format has to be designed so sizes can be found without a full scan.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Pass FlatBuffers or Cap'n Proto between services instead of JSON that every hop re-parses into objects. • Data / databases: Use slotted pages with offset arrays; store JSON as a binary format (Postgres JSONB) that queries read without rebuilding a tree. • Native / systems: Use offset tables in mmap'd index files so a lookup touches the mapped bytes and allocates nothing. • Embedded: Keep configuration in a binary blob read in place from flash.
Watch out: In-place formats must be validated against corruption before trusting offsets. Edits in the middle of a large flat value can be O(n) memmoves; allowing non-minimal size headers (as JSONB does) lets an edit keep the total size unchanged.
Seen in SQLite: 436051582f 2004-05-29 B-tree pages got a contiguous array of 2-byte cell offsets at the front, replacing a per-cell "next" link and a heap-allocated `aCell[]` array built at load time. Binary search runs on page bytes directly. The file-format change made B-tree code "between 10 and 20% faster". • 50db3e8d51 2018-09-27 GeoPoly "always stores its polygons in the binary format, which is faster and more compact". • 6831cad03b 2023-09-21 Introduced JSONB, a binary JSON encoding with a type nibble and a 1, 2, 3, 5 or 9-byte size header. 77e00f24d7 2023-09-28 made `json_extract()` work on the blob directly "without the need to translate into an array of JsonNode objects. This is much faster." • 4b9ed1b256 2023-11-30 Removed the JsonNode tree entirely; JSONB became the internal format, with non-minimal size headers allowed so space can be reserved and patched later.

### sqlite-059 — Choose array-of-structs or struct-of-arrays by access pattern
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: When an array's elements mix wide and narrow fields, split it into parallel arrays to eliminate per-element padding. When separate arrays are always accessed together, merge them into one struct so related fields share a cache line.
Why it is faster: An array of `{pointer, u16}` pads each element to 16 bytes on 64-bit; two parallel arrays use 10. Conversely, parallel arrays read together cost two cache misses per element where a struct costs one. The right choice depends on whether fields are read together or separately.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: For large numeric datasets in JS, use parallel typed arrays instead of arrays of small objects. • Native / systems: Use SoA in ECS game engines for fields iterated separately, and AoS for fields always read together. • Embedded: Split constant lookup tables into parallel arrays to remove padding in flash. • Data / databases: Columnar layouts are SoA at scale; row stores are AoS. Pick per access pattern.
Watch out: Parallel arrays must be kept in sync on insert and delete, and an element no longer has one address to pass around.
Seen in SQLite: 00706be366 2004-01-30 Merged the VM's value stack and its separate string array into one `Mem` struct, so the fields of a value sit together. • 2cbd78b70e 2012-02-02 Split `MemPage.aOvfl[]`, an array of `{u8 *pCell; u16 idx;}`, into `apOvfl[]` and `aiOvfl[]`, reducing `MemPage` by about 32 bytes. • cfc45b1021 2018-12-03 Split the parser's `yyRuleInfo[]` table of `{u16, i8}` into two arrays "to reduce the size of the parser tables".

### sqlite-060 — Derive redundant state instead of storing it
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite · Also: curl, Tokio
Principle: A field that can be computed from other fields has to be kept in sync on every write. If it is cheap to derive, drop it: compute capacity from count, size from count times element size, a type from flags. Do not store the same value twice in one record.
Why it is faster: Every redundant field costs memory and a store on each update, plus bugs when it drifts out of sync. Deriving it on demand often costs one shift or multiply. Implicit power-of-two capacity is a classic case: if arrays always double, the code can grow exactly when `(n & (n-1)) == 0`, with no capacity field at all.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In React, compute derived values in render rather than syncing them into state. Drop cached `isEmpty` flags that mirror `length == 0`. • Data / databases: Drop denormalized counters that cost more on writes than they save on reads; do not store sort-key columns twice in shuffle payloads. • Native / systems: Use implicit power-of-two capacity for small vectors in compilers and AST builders. • Embedded: Recompute cheap derived values instead of spending RAM on them.
Watch out: This is the opposite trade to memoizing derived fields (see Caching). Drop a field when derivation is cheap or rarely needed; keep it when the derivation is costly and sits on a hot path.

### sqlite-061 — Use one bit per item when only membership matters
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: If a per-item array only records "seen" or "not seen", store a bitmap instead of a counter or pointer per item. For small integer keys, put a fixed bitmap in front of a slower general structure.
Why it is faster: A bitmap is 32 to 64 times smaller than an array of ints or pointers, so it fits in cache and allocates less. A test is a shift, a mask and a load. A small fixed bitmap answers most lookups in O(1) without allocation and falls back only for keys outside its range.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Visited sets for small dense IDs in crawlers and graph walks; feature-seen flags per request. • Native / systems: GC mark bits, free-block maps, file-descriptor sets. • Data / databases: Null bitmaps and deleted-row bitmaps instead of per-row flags. • Embedded: Fixed bitmaps for event or channel masks.
Watch out: A bitmap's size follows the key range, not the item count; for sparse large ranges use a hash or a hybrid structure.
Seen in SQLite: 1235bb1815 2012-04-03 `integrity_check` replaced an `int` reference count per page with a bit per page, cutting "the large allocation from 4 bytes to 1 bit for each page". • 58332de7a6 2026-08-16 Added a "Variable Number Bitmap Cache" (two `u64` words by default) so the parser can tell whether parameter `?NNN` was already seen without searching a list; 464f5129c2 2026-08-16 merged it.

### sqlite-062 — Store small values inline instead of in a side allocation
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: If a value fits in fields the object already has, encode it there (an integer in the node's value slot, a 64-bit constant split across two 32-bit operands) instead of pointing to a separately allocated copy or keeping it as text.
Why it is faster: A side allocation costs a malloc, a free, a pointer chase on every read, and for text a re-parse. Inline storage costs none of these. It works whenever the common values are small and the object already has spare bits or slots.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Small-integer tagging and NaN-boxing in VMs; inline small strings in tagged pointers. • Web / backend: Keep small payloads inline in message structs rather than as references to separate buffers. • Data / databases: Store short values directly in hash-table slots or index entries rather than via an indirection. • Embedded: Pack constants into instruction words instead of a constant pool.
Watch out: Two representations of the same value (inline and out-of-line) double the code paths that read it; keep the inline case strictly limited.

### sqlite-063 — Keep only the shortest distinguishing key in interior index nodes
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: An interior node of a search tree only needs a key that separates its children. Store the shortest prefix that does so, and prefix-compress neighbouring keys, so more children fit per node.
Why it is faster: Higher fanout makes the tree shallower, so lookups touch fewer nodes and fewer I/Os. Interior nodes are a small fraction of the tree but are read on every lookup, so shrinking them pays off on every query.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Suffix truncation in B-tree indexes on strings; prefix-compressed SSTable blocks. • Web / backend: Trie-like URL routing tables that store only distinguishing segments. • Native / systems: Radix trees with path compression.
Watch out: Prefix compression forces sequential decode inside a node; add restart points if nodes are large and need binary search.

### sqlite-064 — Use alignment padding as scratch space
Lens: memory-layout · Source: SQLite
Principle: Alignment holes in a struct are free storage. Use them to carry a value computed in one pass into a later pass instead of recomputing it.
Why it is faster: Two-pass algorithms (measure, then write) often compute the same per-item value twice. Storing it in padding that already exists costs no memory and removes the second computation.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Two-pass serializers caching per-field encodings in spare struct bytes; GC mark bits in pointer low bits. • Data / databases: Keep per-row temporaries in header padding during bulk encoding.
Watch out: The padding disappears if someone reorders or packs the struct; document the dependency and assert the size.

### sqlite-065 — Serve small, short-lived objects from a per-owner slab with a free list
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite
Principle: Give each owner (connection, request, thread) a fixed arena of equal-sized slots threaded on a singly linked free list. Allocations that fit take a slot in a few instructions; anything else falls back to the general allocator. Decide who owns a pointer at free time by checking whether it lies inside the arena.
Why it is faster: Most allocations by a parser or VM are small and die quickly. Popping a free list takes no lock, no size-class search and no header, and freeing is a range check plus a push. Locality improves because the slots are packed together. A private arena also avoids a global allocator mutex. The trade-off is memory reserved per owner, so slot size and count must match the request size distribution.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-request arenas freed in one shot at the end of the request; per-thread small-object free lists (tcmalloc-style). • Embedded: Preallocated fixed-block pools per task; no heap after startup. • Native / systems: Free lists for AST or IR nodes in compilers; object pools for particles and messages in games. • Data / databases: Per-query memory contexts (Postgres-style) for planner and executor temporaries.
Watch out: Long-lived objects in the slab pin slots and starve short-lived ones. Statistics kept on the fast path can cost more than the allocation itself; compute them on demand.
Seen in SQLite: 633e6d57d9 2008-07-28 Introduced the lookaside allocator: "makes the speed1.test script run about 15% faster". Ownership is a `pStart <= p < pEnd` check. cd5a9729d2 2009-04-21 served RowHash from it, and d9da78a2c8 2009-03-24 kept long-lived schema objects out of it so they do not pin slots. • 0b12e7f860 2010-12-20 Added lookaside hit and miss counters to `sqlite3_db_status()` so its effectiveness could be measured. • f94fdd832c 2016-06-04 Allocates KeyInfo objects from lookaside when possible. • 52fb8e1945 2017-08-29 Removed outstanding-count and high-water updates from the malloc and free fast paths (computed by walking the lists only when someone asks), and serves never-used slots from a separate `pInit` list so nothing needs threading at setup. • 4093b29610 2024-01-03 JSON functions switched from the global allocator to the per-connection one "to avoid hitting the global memory allocator mutex".

### sqlite-066 — Tune slab geometry to the request histogram and encode the size class in the address
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite
Principle: For a fixed memory budget, choose slot sizes and counts from the measured size distribution, using a few size classes rather than one. Lay each class out in its own address range so free can tell the class with a pointer comparison, and make the disabled state fail the same comparison.
Why it is faster: A single slot size wastes memory on small requests and runs out quickly; too-small slots overflow to malloc. Two classes cover more requests with less memory. Address ranges mean freed blocks need no header to record their size. Setting the threshold or bounds to zero when the slab is off folds "is it enabled?" into the size test the fast path already makes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Size per-request arena classes from a histogram of real allocations (headers vs bodies). • Native / systems: Tune jemalloc/tcmalloc bins; place each size class in its own region so free needs no header. • Embedded: Small and large partitions in RTOS fixed-block pools. • Data / databases: Set a cache's maximum entry size to 0 to disable it rather than adding an enabled flag.
Watch out: Geometry tuned on one workload can be wrong for another; keep the counters that let users measure hit rates.

### sqlite-067 — Keep a buffer when its contents change, and check capacity before resizing
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a value is replaced, keep its backing buffer and reuse it if it is big enough. Store the usable capacity next to the pointer so the check is one compare, call the resize path only on the rare growth case, and on reset free only buffers above a size threshold.
Why it is faster: Free-then-malloc on every assignment costs two allocator calls and often a copy. Most replacements fit in the old buffer. Keeping capacity in a field avoids asking the allocator (`malloc_usable_size`-style calls are not free). Invalidating by clearing a flag instead of freeing keeps the allocation for the next use.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Reuse `StringBuilder`/`bytes.Buffer` with a reset; Go `buf = buf[:0]` or `sync.Pool`; per-connection read buffers in servers. • Native / systems: Keep `capacity` beside `len`; `Vec::clear()` keeps capacity; shrink only when a buffer is huge. • Data / databases: Reuse row and value buffers across rows in drivers and executors. • Embedded: Static scratch buffers reused across calls.
Watch out: Kept buffers raise steady-state memory; cap what you retain and give it back under memory pressure.

### sqlite-068 — Grow transient buffers geometrically; size long-lived ones exactly
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite
Principle: Builders that are appended to should double (or grow by a constant factor) and start at a size that fits the common case, with a fast path for the first element. Objects that live a long time should be sized exactly so they do not waste memory.
Why it is faster: Growing by a fixed amount gives O(n) reallocations and O(n^2) copying; doubling gives amortized O(1) appends and a logarithmic number of reallocs. Starting large enough avoids the early reallocations that nearly every instance would hit. Long-lived objects (schema metadata kept for the life of a connection) pay for slack forever, so exact sizing wins there. Using realloc lets the allocator extend in place and skip the copy.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Preallocate arrays and string builders from known or typical counts; shrink-to-fit caches you keep. • Native / systems: `Vec::with_capacity`, `reserve()`; pick the growth policy by object lifetime. • Data / databases: Double sort and hash-table buffers during execution; size catalog structures exactly. • Embedded: Use realloc-in-place growth where the allocator supports it, and exact sizing for persistent tables.
Watch out: Doubling can overshoot by nearly 2x; for huge buffers use a smaller factor or a cap.

### sqlite-069 — Make one allocation for an object and its variable-size parts
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Allocate a header together with its trailing array or string, carve several arrays out of one block, and pack related strings back to back, so one malloc and one free serve the whole object.
Why it is faster: Each separate allocation costs a malloc, a free, per-block header overhead and a pointer hop on every access. One block halves (or better) the allocator calls and keeps the parts adjacent in memory. The cost is that growing one part means reallocating the whole block.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: C flexible array members; arena-allocated AST subtrees; small-vector types with inline headers. • Web / backend: Intern related strings in one buffer; one `ArrayBuffer` holding a header plus records. • Data / databases: Store a row header with its column data; pack catalog names in one string table. • Embedded: Flat dictionaries in a single byte buffer.
Watch out: Pointers into a coalesced block are invalidated when it is reallocated, and the block must be freed as a unit.

### sqlite-070 — Zero and write only the memory that will be read before it is written
Lens: allocation · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Do not memset whole objects or buffers that are filled before use. Group the fields that need initialization at the front of a struct and clear only that prefix, and skip stores that would write a value already present.
Why it is faster: Zeroing and redundant stores cost memory bandwidth and dirty cache lines. Large structs (a parser state, a cursor, a page) often have big arrays that are always written before they are read. Clearing only `offsetof(first_uninitialized_field)` bytes keeps the safety where it matters and drops the rest. On a hot routine, even initializing one local can be measurable.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Reset only the fields that matter on pooled request objects (Go `sync.Pool`); avoid `new Array(n).fill(0)` when every slot is written first. • Native / systems: Rust `MaybeUninit` or `Vec::with_capacity`+push instead of zero-filled vectors; partial value-init in C++. • Data / databases: Skip zeroing pages that will be fully read into; compare before writing to avoid dirtying cache lines. • Embedded: Initialize only state that is read before written to cut boot and per-call time.
Watch out: Uninitialized memory leaks old data and makes bugs nondeterministic; use sanitizers and valgrind in testing, and zero anything that crosses a trust boundary.
