### sqlite-105 — Turn checks that can no longer fire into debug-only assertions
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Once invariants, upstream validation or the data's structure make a run-time condition impossible, replace the release-build check with an assertion.
Why it is faster: A check that never fires still costs a compare, a branch and code bytes on every execution. Proving it impossible and moving it to an assert keeps the documentation and the debug-time safety net while removing the cost. Hard-coding a domain invariant (for example a minimum length) can delete whole code paths.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `debug_assert!` instead of `assert!` for internal invariants in Rust; contracts checked only in debug builds. • Web / backend: Validate once at the API boundary and remove duplicate validation in internal helpers. • Embedded: Hard-code fixed-format facts (HTTP methods are 3-7 uppercase letters, fixed-width IDs) instead of testing them per call.
Watch out: This is only safe with strong test coverage, ideally fuzzing, that exercises the invariant. Input that can be corrupt or hostile (files, network data) still needs real checks; only invariants the code itself establishes are candidates.
Seen in SQLite: a205a486d2 2011-08-27 A cursor on an empty table became a valid-but-empty cursor, so NULL checks in OP_Next, OP_Rewind and others became `ALWAYS()` or asserts. • fe647dc9ee 2015-06-23 Multiple overflow cells on a page are always adjacent and sorted, so an O(N²) sort routine was deleted and replaced by asserts: "small size reduction and performance increase". • c59b7a8053 2022-11-22 `ALWAYS(n>0)` became an assert "for a performance increase and size reduction". Also f0b41745c5, 289a0c849b, 5f289e8400, 509a6303d1. • 20e09ba6a8 2023-02-25 Dropped a redundant corruption test in `defragmentPage()`; 4f59d09648 moved a flag test into the only branch where the flag can be set. • 1e24dc987a 2023-06-19 No keyword is shorter than 2 characters, so the `n>=2` guard in `keywordCode()` became an assert and the tokenizer exits early when the second character cannot be part of a keyword.

### sqlite-106 — Hand bulk copying and scanning to optimized library primitives
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Use `memmove`, `memcpy`, `memcmp`, `memset`, `strchr` and `strcspn` instead of hand-written byte loops.
Why it is faster: libc primitives are tuned per CPU and use wide or SIMD operations; compilers also recognize and inline them for small constant sizes. A hand loop moves one element and re-tests a condition per iteration, and is more code. Computing a run length first and issuing one call beats a loop that appends one character at a time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `TypedArray.set`, `copyWithin` and `indexOf` in JS; `StringBuilder.append(char[],off,len)`. • Native / systems: `memchr` for newline or quote scans in CSV and line splitters; `copy_from_slice` in Rust. • Data / databases: Slotted-page insert and delete via memmove.
Watch out: For very short runs the call can cost more than an inline loop. SQLite added a build option (8674e49214) that replaces memcpy with an inline loop only so the profiler can attribute memcpy time to call sites.
Seen in SQLite: 9bb7c4ff4f 2013-12-09 With 8f518837f9: `memmove()` in `dropCell()` and `insertCell()` "for a size reduction and performance improvement", replacing the hand-written two-byte loop of 2011. b8a9bb4fb9 uses `memcmp()` instead of the BINARY collating function where possible. • eba21f9eb2 2017-10-30 After `%` in LIKE, the search for the next literal character uses `strcspn()` with both case variants. • 93df8109fc 2025-02-10 JSON rendering does one capacity check, then writes quote, memcpy, quote directly instead of three append calls. • 833fb1ef59 2025-10-05 printf finds the next directive with `strchr(fmt,'%')` instead of a byte loop.

### sqlite-107 — Shape loops for fewer operations per iteration: pointers, end pointers, do-while, deferred bookkeeping
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Iterate with a pointer against a precomputed end, use do-while when the first iteration is guaranteed, move element counters out of the loop, and merge per-item calls into one bulk loop.
Why it is faster: Indexing `base[i]` costs an add, and often a multiply, per access and keeps an extra register live. A pointer cursor removes both. A stored end pointer turns a computed limit into one compare. A do-while removes the entry test when the condition is known true. Updating a struct counter on every iteration is a load and a store; advancing a local pointer and computing the count once at the end is not. Calling a tiny function per item repeats call overhead and global loads that one bulk loop pays once.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Pointer cursors in tokenizers and stack machines; reserve capacity and write through a raw pointer, then set the length once. • Web / backend: Batch PRNG fills (`crypto.getRandomValues` on one buffer) and vectorized per-batch callbacks instead of per-row calls. • Embedded: Byte-copy loops with a known non-zero length as do-while.
Watch out: Modern compilers do some of this automatically; measure before rewriting. Pointer loops make off-by-one errors easier, so keep asserts on the bounds.
Seen in SQLite: 61d2fe955c 2011-06-03 Cell-pointer arrays shifted two bytes per operation with an end-pointer bound; c3f1d5f095 converted `dropCell` to a pointer loop. • 3a61a5a2b5 2011-06-03 Tokenizer blob literals: one predicate loop `for(i=2; sqlite3Isxdigit(z[i]); i++){}`, then a single check for the closing quote and even length, instead of several tests per character. • cf5ff12105 2013-08-21 Inlined `randomByte()` into the `sqlite3_randomness()` loop. • 0b9dadacd8 2013-11-25 A do-while in record compare (with an assert proving the condition on entry): "about a 10% performance boost in that routine, or a 0.9% boost overall". Also 3f5b199eb5. • f56fa4676e 2015-04-13 Removed the program-counter index from the interpreter; `pOp` is the iterator and jumps are `pOp = &aOp[p2-1]`: 0.6%. 118ab65859 changed the parser stack from an index to a pointer: "about 6.6% parser performance gain". • b982bfea37 2016-05-20 Linked-list merges assert both inputs are non-empty and handle exhaustion inside the loop: "10 bytes smaller and use 0.03% fewer CPU cycles". • 8b471e7e79 2017-06-28 The parser stores a stack-end pointer for overflow detection; 6be95366c7 stores the rule's symbol count negated so one add adjusts the stack; 0e6f67b754 precomputes `2*nDim` in R-tree loops. • a9e1e4edaf 2023-01-03 Hash probe loop decrements only on a miss: "Small performance increase in the symbol hash table". • 77e097ea68 2026-10-14 With b99380fccf: `balance_nonroot()` writes `*ppCell++` and computes the count once afterwards: "Reduce per-cell bookkeeping."

### sqlite-108 — Keep calling conventions lean: fewer arguments, results returned by value, distinct status codes
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Remove unused or redundant parameters, return a result code instead of writing it through a pointer, and return dedicated status values (done, empty) instead of making callers inspect state.
Why it is faster: Each argument costs a register move at every call site, and spills once registers run out. An out-parameter forces a memory store and makes the compiler assume aliasing. A routine that returns plain OK and leaves "was the table empty?" to the caller makes every caller repeat that check; a distinct code lets them branch once. Removing locals from a huge function shrinks the frame set up on every call.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Return `Result` by value in Rust rather than filling error slots; avoid `ref`/`out` in hot C# paths. • Web / backend: Iterator protocols that return a sentinel instead of `hasNext()` plus `next()`. • Embedded: Keep hot functions within the argument registers of the ABI.
Watch out: The sticky-error style (one check after many calls) and return-by-value both have a place: the first saves code in long sequences of fallible calls, the second is faster in a single hot call. SQLite uses both, and moved `insertCell()` from the first to the second once it became hot.
Seen in SQLite: 98add2ed74 2009-07-20 Btree helpers take `int *pRC` and do nothing if it already holds an error, so callers make N calls and check once (smaller code with fewer branches). • 4261096124 2012-09-17 `sqlite3Utf8Read(z,&z)` became `sqlite3Utf8Read(&z)`: "Slight performance increase." • 75179ded97 2014-09-16 Record compare went from 4 arguments to 3; 0c8f760ab3 removed a local from OP_Column ("modest size reduction and a performance increase"); 2c77be054a removed locals from the interpreter to cut its stack use. • 44548e7218 2017-08-14 `moveToRoot()` returns `SQLITE_EMPTY` for an empty table, which "simplifies the callers, making them smaller and faster". 2ab792e4c0 made Next and Previous return `SQLITE_DONE` instead of using an out-parameter. • b53d8fa9f2 2022-11-21 `insertCell()` returns its result code instead of using `int *pRC`; 06164b2340 made a function return void; 54bc63815a removed a parameter from the parser entry: "about 100 bytes smaller and 1.4M cycles faster"; a0318fd7b4 removed a local.

### sqlite-109 — Collapse several run-time conditions into one precomputed flag test
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite · Also: Flycast
Principle: When a hot path tests several independent conditions, maintain one combined bit or mask that is updated where the conditions change, and test only that.
Why it is faster: Configuration changes rarely while hot paths run constantly, so it is cheaper to recompute a summary on each change than to re-derive it on each use. One masked test replaces several loads and branches. Keeping both a state bit and its complement lets "is it clean?" be a positive test and lets a state transition be a single XOR.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: A single "any hooks registered" flag in an event emitter, recomputed on subscribe and unsubscribe. • Native / systems: Kernels fold pending work into one mask tested on syscall exit; cache entries keep combined "needs work" bits. • Data / databases: Summary flags on table metadata (has triggers, has foreign keys, has NOT NULL columns).
Watch out: The combined flag is derived state. Every writer of the underlying conditions must update it, so funnel those writes through one setter.

It now returns a global `mmuOn`, set in `MMU_reset`/`UpdateMMU`. `mmuOn` becomes true only when the AT bit is set and the string “SH-4 Kernel” is found in RAM at the known Windows CE locations. The FullMMU option, which used to force the slow MMU path on, is gone. afcb3b7ad 2023-02 “mmu: get rid of FullMMU option. Detect wince with string id in ram”

### sqlite-110 — Fold range checks and small decisions into arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Turn two-sided range checks into one unsigned comparison, fold signs before threshold tests, and compute small decisions with bit arithmetic instead of branches.
Why it is faster: `lo <= x && x <= hi` is two compares and two branches; `(unsigned)(x-lo) <= hi-lo` is one, because values below `lo` wrap to huge numbers. Taking the absolute value first halves the threshold tests when classifying magnitude. Bit tricks such as `h += 9*(1&(h>>6))` for hex digits remove hard-to-predict branches entirely, and the subtracted value can often be reused (the digit value in a number parser).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Number and date parsers; `x >>> 0` tricks for index validation in JS. • Native / systems: Array bounds checks as `(size_t)i < n`; enum-range validation; choosing varint or zigzag widths. • Embedded: Hex and base64 decoding with arithmetic instead of if-chains.
Watch out: Signed overflow is undefined in C; do the subtraction in unsigned types. Comment the trick, because the intent is not obvious to the next reader.
Seen in SQLite: 5742b63e4a 2005-01-26 `sqlite3VdbeSerialType`: `u64 u = i<0 ? -i : i; if( u<=127 )` instead of two-sided tests per width. • 06fbb733b9 2008-04-11 Hex digit conversion `h += 9*(1&(h>>6)); return h&0xf`, which "uses no branches". 7ee27b07c3 replaced a wrap-around conditional with `%`. • 403f00219b 2022-08-03 Parameter binding passes `(u32)(i-1)` and tests `i>=p->nVar`: one comparison covers both ends. • 0944b3efad 2026-03-25 `sqlite3Atoi64()`: `(c=(unsigned)zNum[i]-'0')<=9; u = u*10 + c;` reuses the subtraction as the digit.

### sqlite-111 — Take an ASCII fast path, and test several bytes per operation inside a register
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Most text is ASCII. Handle bytes below 0x80 with a cheap test or a small bitmap before falling back to full Unicode logic, and where possible test 4 or 8 bytes at once with word arithmetic.
Why it is faster: Full UTF-8 decoding and Unicode tables cost loads and branches per character, but nearly every byte in real text needs none of it. One unsigned compare (`(u8)(c-1) < 0x7F`) checks both "not NUL" and "ASCII". SWAR goes further: load 8 bytes into a 64-bit word, and a few arithmetic and mask operations answer "does any byte need attention?" for all 8 at once. A rolling 32-bit accumulator can similarly match several short keywords in a single pass.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: UTF-8 validation and length counting, JSON string scanning, HTTP header parsing; JS engines' one-byte string fast paths follow the same idea. • Embedded: Fixed-keyword scanners and MIME-type sniffers using a rolling accumulator. • Data / databases: Case folding and tokenization in full-text indexers.
Watch out: Word loads need either alignment care or `memcpy`-based loads and must not read past the buffer. Results on malformed UTF-8 may change; SQLite accepted that explicitly ("GI/GO"), but you must decide whether your API can.

### sqlite-112 — Decode variable-length integers with an inline one-byte fast path, unrolling and branch-light tricks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Variable-length integers are almost always one byte. Test that case inline with a single compare, unroll the longer cases, and keep the general decoder out of line.
Why it is faster: A general decoder is a function call plus a loop with a data-dependent exit. For a one-byte value the whole job is "is the high bit clear? then the byte is the value", so an inline test removes the call and the loop from nearly every decode. Longer values benefit from unrolled straight-line code in 32-bit arithmetic, with 64-bit math only for the rare long tail. Further refinements remove instructions from the dependency chain: reading the byte as signed folds the continuation test into a sign test, XOR-ing raw bytes and cancelling the continuation bits with one constant replaces a mask per byte, and a value-only variant skips computing and storing the length.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Protobuf, LEB128 and MessagePack decoders in JS or Go: test `b < 0x80` before the general loop. • Native / systems: WASM binary readers, emulators decoding variable-length opcodes, UTF-8 decoders (the same shape with an ASCII fast path). • Data / databases: Record headers, compressed posting lists and time-series codecs.
Watch out: Check the generated code: an elegant macro can compile worse than a plain if/else. Corrupt input can produce over-long encodings, so the slow path must still bound its reads.

### sqlite-113 — Pick a type-specialized comparator once per search or sort
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Inspect the key types before a search or sort starts and choose a comparison function specialized for the dominant case, so generic per-field type dispatch leaves the inner loop.
Why it is faster: A generic record compare decodes each field's type, dispatches on it and handles collations, on every comparison. A binary search or sort makes O(log n) or O(n log n) comparisons with the same key shape, so deciding the shape once and calling a comparator that assumes it removes most of that work. When leading fields are already known equal, comparing only the remaining fields saves more.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JS, build a typed comparator once (`(a,b)=>a-b` for numbers) instead of `typeof`-switching per comparison. • Data / databases: Columnar engines choose a typed comparison kernel per column; hash joins specialize single-integer keys. • Native / systems: Use a radix sort when every key is an integer.
Watch out: The fast comparator must give exactly the same order as the generic one, including edge cases such as integers that do not round-trip through a double. Keep the generic comparator as the tie-breaker.

### sqlite-114 — Select the variant once: state-specific function pointers, and direct calls when the target is fixed
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: If a routine branches on object state that changes rarely, store a pointer to a variant written for the current state and update it on each state change. If the target never varies at a call site, call it directly instead.
Why it is faster: A series of state tests (error? memory-mapped? codec?) on every call becomes one indirect call, and every variant is shorter. The trade-off reverses when the target is effectively fixed: an indirect call blocks inlining and needs a predictor entry, and some compilers optimize code with function pointers poorly. So a pointer pays off for state that changes; a direct call pays off for a fixed target.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: A connection object swaps its `send` between TLS, plaintext and closed implementations; a JS class reassigns `this.read = readBuffered` when a buffer is attached. • Embedded: A driver switches its ops table on power state instead of testing the state in every operation. • Native / systems: Devirtualize hot calls whose receiver type is fixed; replace a callback parameter with an enum and a switch.
Watch out: Every place that changes the state must re-select the pointer, or the object silently uses a stale variant. Centralize the selection in one function, as `setGetterMethod()` does.
Seen in SQLite: a205a486d2 2011-08-27 OP_Next and OP_Prev carry a `P4_ADVANCE` function pointer (`sqlite3BtreeNext` or `Previous`) instead of a ternary on the opcode. • 25ada07ab4 2015-06-19 `cellSizePtr()` became the per-page `xCellSize` method, with a no-payload variant for interior table pages. • 12e6f68279 2016-12-13 With fc4111f7d6: `sqlite3PagerGet()` became `pPager->xGet` with normal, mmap and error variants, re-selected wherever the error or mmap state changes. "About 25% faster" for that routine. • 8cf92890f2 2019-02-20 Replaced a function-pointer argument with a flag because "function pointers appear to be a source of consternation to LLVM". • a7c9dd546b 2022-02-24 OP_Next and OP_Prev went back to calling `sqlite3BtreeNext()` and `Previous()` directly: "improved performance and a decrease in code size".

### sqlite-115 — Unroll small fixed-work loops, and process full blocks with a separate tail
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: When each iteration does very little (a table lookup, an add), unroll by the natural group size and handle the remainder once after the main loop.
Why it is faster: Loop control (increment, compare, branch) can cost as much as the body. Unrolling amortizes it and exposes independent operations the CPU can run in parallel. Processing whole groups (3 bytes for base64, pairs of coordinates, 64-byte checksum blocks) removes partial-group logic from every iteration.
Applies elsewhere: Embedded: CRC and Adler checksums, DSP loops over complex pairs. • Web / backend: Hex and base64 codecs, delimiter scans in CSV and HTTP parsers. • Native / systems: Scalar tails after SIMD block loops.
Watch out: Unrolling grows code and can hurt the instruction cache; the compiler may already unroll. Keep the factor small and confirm with measurements.

### sqlite-116 — Turn recursion into loops, especially tail recursion on the common chain
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Rewrite recursive descent along a single child (or a fallback chain) as a loop that updates its state and jumps back.
Why it is faster: A recursive call pays frame setup and teardown and grows the stack with input depth. A loop pays neither, can be inlined into its caller (compilers rarely inline recursive functions), and cannot overflow on deep input. Merging two consecutive calls so the second becomes a tail call achieves part of the same effect.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Iterative destructors for linked structures in C++ and Rust, which also avoid stack overflow on drop. • Web / backend: JS engines lack tail-call elimination; trampolines or loops for recursive descent over deep input. • Data / databases: B-tree and trie descent, AST simplifiers.
Watch out: General tree recursion needs an explicit stack, which brings back allocation; only the single-chain case is a free win.
Seen in SQLite: a441404f92 2015-11-09 A fallback recursion in the parser's shift-action lookup became a loop "so that routine can be inlined". 95c91e145a lets a hash-removal routine also free the page, so the caller ends in a tail call: smaller and faster. • ebf9702b34 2024-05-06 Expression deletion frees the node and jumps back to handle the left child, bounding stack use on long unary chains. • 8fafd53c09 2026-08-21 `sqlite3ExprIsInteger()` rewritten as a loop that tracks the sign through unary operators: "2M cycles faster on x64".

### sqlite-117 — Pad buffers and tables so hot code can over-read and skip bounds checks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Guarantee spare bytes after buffers, or extra entries in lookup tables, so inner loops can read whole words or index freely without testing the limit on every step.
Why it is faster: Bounds checks cost a compare and a branch per access, and they prevent wide loads. If any index the code can produce is guaranteed to land inside the allocation, the check can go. The price is a few bytes per buffer or a few hundred bytes of table, paid once.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: SIMD parsers that require input padding (simdjson does); network receive buffers padded so decoders read whole words. • Embedded: 256-entry byte tables instead of `if (c < 128)` checks. • Data / databases: Record buffers with slack so field decoders use fixed-size loads.
Watch out: The padding guarantee must hold for every allocation path, including buffers from callers and memory-mapped pages; one unpadded source makes the over-read a real memory error. Document the invariant next to the allocator.

### sqlite-118 — Use masks instead of division and instead of up-front validation
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: With power-of-two sizes, replace `% n` with `& (n-1)`; and instead of validating every offset when data is loaded, mask each offset into range when it is used.
Why it is faster: Integer division costs 20-40 cycles; an AND costs one. Validating every pointer in a structure on load is a loop over data you may never touch; masking at use costs one AND per actual access and still guarantees that corrupt data cannot index outside the buffer.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Hash maps and ring buffers with power-of-two capacity; sharded lock arrays. • Embedded: Masked pointers in sandboxes (WASM, eBPF use the same idea). • Data / databases: Slotted pages that mask offsets read from disk.
Watch out: Masking keeps reads in bounds but does not make corrupt data correct; you still need detection somewhere. Power-of-two tables with weak hashes cluster, because the mask keeps only the low bits.
Seen in SQLite: 1688c86f5a 2008-07-18 Dropped the cell-pointer consistency loop from page initialization "for a 2.5% performance gain"; `findCell()` now masks with `maskPage`. f061be9c6f put a full over-read check behind a compile-time option. • bf66606d4f 2026-08-05 Page-cache hash lookup uses a mask; 6c085d9745 and 92f270eb0a converted the remaining eight `%` sites, each with an assert that the bucket count is a power of two: "Small performance enhancement and size reduction."

### sqlite-119 — Use hardware instructions through intrinsics: byte swap, count leading zeros, wide multiply
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Where the compiler exposes a single instruction for an operation (bswap, clz, 64x64-to-128-bit multiply), use it, keeping portable code as a compile-time fallback.
Why it is faster: Assembling a big-endian integer byte by byte takes four loads, shifts and ORs; a native load plus bswap is one or two instructions, and on a big-endian host just a load. A shift-and-test loop for log2 becomes one clz. A 128-bit product from four partial products becomes one multiply. Knowing the host byte order at compile time picks the cheapest form.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In JS, `DataView` with an explicit endianness or a typed array when native order matches; `Math.clz32` and `Math.imul`. • Native / systems: Network and file-format readers (PNG, ELF), hash functions that rely on 128-bit multiply, allocator size-class selection via clz. • Embedded: Native-endian overlays on DSPs with a portable fallback for other targets.
Watch out: Intrinsics vary by compiler and version (SQLite gates them on GCC and MSVC versions). Always keep and test the portable path, and use `memcpy` for unaligned loads rather than pointer casts.

### sqlite-120 — Prefer unconditional cheap work to conditional avoidance
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: When skipping a small operation needs a branch and extra arithmetic, just do the operation, or do a slightly larger but simpler version of it.
Why it is faster: The test that avoids a 4-byte copy can cost more than the copy, especially if it mispredicts. Copying a whole aligned block whose size is known up front lets the copy routine run at full width with less address arithmetic and register pressure than an exact, oddly aligned sub-range.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Always copy a fixed-size header; copy whole cache lines; branchless clamping. • Embedded: Fixed-size DMA transfers instead of exact lengths. • Web / backend: Clone a whole small object instead of selected fields.
Watch out: This holds only for small, cache-resident data; copying a large block "just because" wastes memory bandwidth.
Seen in SQLite: d6176c4131 2014-10-11 `insertCell`: "the extra work involved in sometimes avoiding an memcpy() of the first four bytes of a record takes more time than just unconditionally copying those four bytes." • 7520116441 2023-06-09 `defragmentPage()` copies the whole page instead of the sub-range from the cell-content start; the message credits 8-byte alignment and lower register pressure, faster and smaller.

### sqlite-121 — Match arithmetic width to the hardware, with cheap overflow checks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Use native-width integers and detect overflow with a wraparound test, handle the common small-operand case of wide arithmetic natively, and avoid operations the target CPU lacks.
Why it is faster: 64-bit arithmetic on a 32-bit CPU costs several instructions, and 64-bit division there is a slow library call. When values almost always fit in 32 bits, native-width arithmetic with a single wraparound check (`a+b < b`) keeps correctness without the cost. A checked 64-bit multiply can use one native multiply when both high halves are zero.
Applies elsewhere: Embedded: Cortex-M firmware statistics and formatting; avoid 64-bit division in interrupt paths. • Native / systems: Checked-add idioms for offset accumulation in binary parsers; `size_t` versus `uint64_t` choices in portable libraries. • Web / backend: WASM32 targets have the same 64-bit costs; safe-math money arithmetic with a small-operand fast path.
Watch out: Narrow types need explicit overflow handling at every accumulation point; one missed check is a security bug in a parser. Select platform-specific paths only for targets you can test.

### sqlite-122 — Replace a width switch with one wide load or store plus a table-driven shift
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a value is stored in one of several byte widths, read or write the maximum width once and shift by a per-width amount, instead of branching on the width.
Why it is faster: A loop over bytes has a data-dependent trip count; a switch per width removes the loop but its branch mispredicts when widths vary from row to row. One unaligned 8-byte load, a byte swap and an arithmetic shift (looked up by width) is straight-line code that also sign-extends for free. The store side is symmetric: shift, swap, store 8 bytes, advance the pointer by the real width. Both need slack bytes so the over-read or over-write is safe.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Variable-width integer columns and bit-packed encodings in columnar formats. • Web / backend: Protocol parsers with 1/2/4-byte length prefixes; serializers that keep 8 bytes of output slack. • Native / systems: Tail handling after SIMD loops.
Watch out: Only valid with guaranteed padding or slack; and on targets without fast unaligned loads, the wide load can be slower than the switch.

### sqlite-123 — Write narrow number parsers that classify as they go
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Replace general library parsing with a routine specialized for your input form that accumulates digits in integers, reports what kind of number it saw, and requires one canonical input form.
Why it is faster: Library routines handle locales, errors and every encoding. A narrow parser can accumulate decimal digits in a 64-bit integer and apply the exponent once at the end, avoiding a floating multiply-add per digit. It can also return "pure integer or not" in the same pass, so the caller does not parse again with a second routine. Accepting only NUL-terminated UTF-8 removes per-character encoding increments and end checks from the inner loop, and packing several results into one bitmask return replaces out-parameters.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON and CSV number parsing with an integer fast path that returns int-or-float in one pass. • Embedded: Fixed-point parsing without an FPU; normalize text encoding once at ingress. • Data / databases: CAST and affinity conversion; avoid `sscanf` in log parsers.
Watch out: Hand-written float parsing is easy to get subtly wrong (rounding, huge exponents, denormals). Test against a reference parser over many random inputs before replacing the library call.

### sqlite-124 — Format numbers without a general formatter: dedicated routines, two digits at a time, bulk fills
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: For hot conversions to text, write the digits directly instead of interpreting a format string, emit two digits per division, and fill runs of padding or digits with one bulk operation.
Why it is faster: A printf-style engine parses the format string, dispatches on each directive and copies through generic paths on every call. A dedicated integer-to-text loop is a few divisions and stores. Dividing by 100 and copying a pair from a 200-byte "00".."99" table halves the slow divisions and stores. Choosing an output precision that always round-trips (17 significant digits for a double) removes a print-parse-compare-reprint loop.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON serializers and log timestamp formatting (precompute or write digits directly). • Embedded: printf replacements on small targets; formatting without 64-bit division. • Data / databases: CSV and text exports of integer and date columns.
Watch out: The digit-pair store needs an aligned table and destination, or a `memcpy`. Keep the general formatter for unusual widths and flags rather than growing the fast path.

### sqlite-125 — Treat floating-point values as integers when testing or splitting them
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Answer questions about an IEEE-754 double (is it NaN? what is its exponent? what are its high bits?) by copying its 64 bits into an integer and masking, instead of floating-point arithmetic or library calls.
Why it is faster: NaN detection through volatile compares or libm defeats optimization; a mask on the bit pattern is two integer operations. The exponent field gives an approximate log2 directly. Splitting a double into high and low halves for exact (double-double) arithmetic by masking 26 mantissa bits is one AND, where the classic multiply-and-subtract split costs more and overflows for large inputs.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Columnar float decoders and JSON number validation. • Native / systems: NaN-boxing VMs; compensated summation and exact rounding in numeric libraries. • Web / backend: `Float64Array` plus `BigUint64Array` views over one buffer give bit access in JS.
Watch out: Use `memcpy` or a union for the reinterpretation, not pointer casts, to avoid strict-aliasing bugs. Platforms with non-IEEE or mixed-endian doubles need a fallback.

### sqlite-126 — Convert between binary floats and decimal with integer arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Represent decimal scaling as a 64-bit mantissa times a normalized power of ten and do the arithmetic with integer (128-bit) multiplies, instead of extended-precision or double-double floating point.
Why it is faster: Long double is slow or absent on many targets, and double-double arithmetic needs several floating operations per step. Integer multiplication with a wide product is exact, fast on 64-bit hardware and portable. The power-of-ten table can be compressed: an exact table for small exponents plus a coarse table every few decades gives any power with at most one extra multiply, instead of hundreds of stored entries.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON number parsing and printing (the Ryu and Eisel-Lemire family use the same idea). • Embedded: The compact two-level table where flash is tight. • Data / databases: Exact text round-trips for REAL values in CAST, export and replication.
Watch out: Correct rounding is subtle; SQLite needed follow-up fixes for table rounding and moved to 96-bit approximations for accuracy. Validate against exhaustive or very large randomized round-trip tests.
Seen in SQLite: a1b0ff1735 2023-06-30 With 12b198f1a2: float-to-decimal conversion scales by powers of ten into a u64 range and extracts digits as integers, using exact Dekker products (5270d7424d). • 5b5d4492f2 2023-09-13 Probed once at start-up whether long double really carries extra precision on this hardware; later 92e71573ce and e8b2c92240 removed long double entirely. • db84cdb747 2026-02-14 A 696-entry power-of-ten table; adbb756155 replaced it with 27 exact entries plus a coarse scale table, removing about 730 lines. c47bc0ffca replaced double-double multiplication with a 128-bit integer multiply; dfd31e158c credits the fpfmt algorithm family; 7ac310d0c6 and a6640846dd refined rounding and precision.

### sqlite-127 — Prefer a register-based virtual machine over a stack machine
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Let instructions name their operand slots explicitly, so each value is computed once into a register and read in place, instead of being pushed, popped and duplicated on an operand stack.
Why it is faster: On a stack VM every operation pushes and pops, and reordering values needs extra dup and copy instructions. A register VM needs fewer instructions, fewer copies and less memory traffic, and its operands can be reused across many instructions. It also makes subroutines and coroutines cheap, because return addresses and coroutine state can live in ordinary registers. If you do keep a stack, a pointer to its top and one struct per slot (instead of an index and parallel arrays) save address arithmetic, and a static pass over the program can compute the exact stack depth.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Compile a DSL, rules engine or template expression into closures that read and write an array of numbered slots instead of a stack. • Data / databases: Expression bytecode in query engines that writes into numbered column slots. • Embedded: A scripting VM in which each script's resume point lives in its own slot, allowing many interleaved coroutines.
Watch out: Register machines have wider instructions and need register allocation in the code generator. Values that share a register must not be clobbered while live, which is a common source of bugs.
Seen in SQLite: 00706be366 2004-01-30 Merged the value stack and a parallel string array into one `Mem` struct; 6810ce677d replaced the top-of-stack index with a `Mem *pTos` pointer, making the VDBE "about 15% smaller". • bc04f8529d 2005-03-29 Each opcode is tagged with whether it can grow the stack (bitmasks generated by the build from comments in the source), and the worst-case depth is summed over the program instead of allocating a fixed generous stack. • d4e70ebd6f 2008-01-02 Began the conversion to a register machine; steps include 66a5167b47 (new operand layout), b1fdb2adea (Dup/MemLoad/MemStore replaced by Copy/SCopy/Move) and 98757157ee. 9cbf3425fb finished it: "The operand stack has been removed from the code. All instructions operate out of registers only" (394 insertions, 680 deletions). d847eaad91 then reused registers better in the SELECT inner loop. • 2eb95377ef 2008-06-06 "Remove the subroutine return stack from the VDBE. Return addresses for subroutines are now stored in registers", which made coroutines practical.

### sqlite-128 — Do no generic work per instruction in the dispatch loop
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Remove shared per-instruction preprocessing (operand decoding, flag tests, output preparation, per-run local initialization) from the dispatch loop, and let each handler do exactly the work it needs.
Why it is faster: A generic prologue that decodes "input 1, input 2, output 3" for every opcode costs loads and branches on each dispatch, including for opcodes that need none of it. Pushing the decoding into the handlers removes those tests from the common path. Opcode metadata needed elsewhere should be a build-time constant table rather than a field filled in for every instruction. Features used by one rare opcode should not need a local initialized at every program start; the opcode can find its data at a fixed place, such as the previous instruction.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In middleware pipelines, avoid generic per-request decoding that most routes never use. • Native / systems: In a hand-written interpreter or emulator, avoid a "fetch operands" helper; decode once into a decoded-instruction cache if metadata is needed. • Data / databases: Packet or record parsers that decode only the fields a given handler reads.
Watch out: Moving decoding into handlers duplicates a few lines per opcode; keep them uniform (or macro-generated) so errors are easy to spot, and keep debug assertions that check operands.
Seen in SQLite: 3a40f696d4 2008-01-04 Opcode properties became a bit vector generated by the build from source comments. a6c2ed91ca made it a global constant table copied into each instruction at prepare time, removing a function call from the loop. • 4c583128bd 2008-01-04 The "out2-prerelease" step: one shared action for output-only opcodes. 93952eb8c5 simplified the prologue "for a speed boost ... and a reduction in code size". 3c65721a5b removed the prologue's operand setup so each handler loads its own operands: "reduce the number of machine-code instructions evaluated by about 10%". • 27a348c7a0 2015-04-13 Removed the generic out2-prerelease flag test; opcodes that need it call `out2Prerelease()` themselves: 0.5%. 7cc84c2cdd removed the per-instruction properties field and its initialization; properties are looked up only in debug builds. • b7dab70a92 2017-01-26 Removed the `aPermute` local from the interpreter; OP_Compare reads its permutation from the previous instruction's operand, avoiding "using CPU cycles for initialization in prepared statements that do not use that opcode". fae58d51ce, d59bcbdee7 and 78a9e92ecf removed other locals and initializations.

### sqlite-129 — Fuse common instruction sequences into superinstructions, and add type-specialized opcodes
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: When the code generator usually emits instruction A followed by B, define one instruction that does both; when an opcode is often used with one operand type, add a variant that assumes it.
Why it is faster: Each instruction costs a dispatch (an indirect branch that may mispredict), an operand decode and a bytecode slot. A fused instruction pays these once and keeps the intermediate value out of a register. A type-specialized copy skips the type checks of the generic one. Repeat-count operands achieve the same for runs of similar operations.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Superinstructions in bytecode VMs (CPython's combined loads, compare-and-branch fusion in WebAssembly engines, char-plus-loop in regex VMs). • Web / backend: Multi-row INSERT or `MSET` instead of one command per item; merge state-change commands in GPU command buffers. • Data / databases: Expression evaluators that fuse load-compare-jump for filters.
Watch out: Every new opcode grows the interpreter and its test matrix. Fuse only sequences that profiles show are frequent, and check that the larger dispatch switch does not cost more than the fusion saves.

### sqlite-130 — Evaluate constant subexpressions once, in the program prologue
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Expressions whose value cannot change during a run are computed once into a register at statement start, identical constants are shared, and each row reads the register.
Why it is faster: A constant expression evaluated per row costs its whole instruction sequence per row; in the prologue it costs once. Deduplicating identical constants saves registers and instructions. The trade-off: hoisting a single-instruction constant can be a loss if reading it back costs as much as recomputing it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Hoist regex compilation, date parsing and Intl object construction out of per-row callbacks and request handlers. • Native / systems: Loop-invariant code motion; shader uniforms instead of per-vertex work. • Data / databases: Evaluate `now()` and deterministic functions of constants once per statement.
Watch out: Only deterministic expressions can be hoisted, and a hoisted expression may run even when the loop executes zero times, which matters if it can fail or is expensive.
Seen in SQLite: 678ccce8b3 2008-03-31 "Factor constant subexpressions out of loops." 47de955ea9 avoids factoring single-instruction constants that would be replaced by a copy anyway. • 48b5b041d1 2010-12-06 "Initialize all constants at the very beginning of a prepared statement", extended into subqueries; f78baafe41 applied the same rules in triggers. • f30a969b80 2013-11-15 Reworked factoring: a constant is coded once in the init section and identical expressions are matched and reused. b1fba2868b and d1a01edac9 factor deterministic functions with constant arguments; 759e858811 factors constant parts of WHERE; 991a198511 folds constant conditionals; e0e261a473 extends factoring to queries without tables.

### sqlite-131 — Fold constants at compile time and specialize code for known literals
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: When an operand is a literal, emit a specialized instruction, a constant, or nothing at all, instead of code that evaluates and validates the value at run time.
Why it is faster: The compiler already knows a literal's value. A constant `LIMIT 0` means "jump to the end"; `expr AND false` is false; `literal IS NULL` is known. Folding removes code generation work and per-row instructions together.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Template engines and SQL builders that drop always-true predicates and precompute static fragments. • Native / systems: Immediate operands in JITs; constant expressions resolved at build time by a config compiler. • Data / databases: Specialize compiled plans on literal values.
Watch out: Folding must respect the language's semantics exactly (NULL logic, outer-join ON clauses, collations); SQLite excludes LEFT JOIN ON terms for that reason.
Seen in SQLite: 9b918ed1d8 2009-11-12 A constant `LIMIT n` emits `OP_Integer n`; `LIMIT 0` emits a single Goto to the exit, skipping the type check and counter. • 5fb52caadf 2012-03-31 `sqlite3ExprAnd()` returns integer 0 if either side is always false (except LEFT JOIN ON terms). • 5be3ac87db 2025-12-03 With 27a7770f52: `literal IS NULL` and `IS NOT NULL` become TRUE or FALSE at parse time, and a binary `IS NULL` becomes unary ISNULL. 1e235b9b02 added a cheaper constructor for integer nodes, and 8597eee119 always stores 32-bit integer literals in the node's value field rather than as token text.

### sqlite-132 — Generate coroutines instead of materializing intermediate results
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Run a producer (a subquery, a view, a VALUES list, one side of a compound select) as a coroutine that yields one row at a time to its consumer, instead of filling a temporary table first.
Why it is faster: Materializing costs a full write and read of the intermediate rows plus memory or disk, and output cannot start until the producer finishes. A coroutine passes each row directly through registers. With return addresses in registers, the switch between producer and consumer is a jump. Materialization still wins when the same result is read several times.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Generators, async iterators and Node streams in ETL pipelines instead of building arrays. • Data / databases: Volcano-style iterators; merge pre-sorted shards; streaming bulk insert from a generator. • Native / systems: Stackless coroutines (state machines) for parsers that feed consumers incrementally.
Watch out: A coroutine re-executes its producer if the consumer needs to read the rows again; choose materialization for multi-use results.
Seen in SQLite: e00ee6eb37 2008-06-20 "Use co-routines in the INSERT processing logic": INSERT...SELECT yields rows one at a time. • 92b01d53c2 2008-06-24 With 0acb7e4849: ORDER BY over a compound select runs both sides as coroutines and merges them like a merge sort, instead of building and sorting a temp table. • 21172c4cc0 2012-10-30 A FROM-clause subquery used once is coded as a coroutine; 6e24ff83d8 the same for views; 5f08526937, 832ee3d4cc, da998c8ec0. • 642364a48b 2014-02-07 `OP_InitCoroutine` and `OP_EndCoroutine` removed per-coroutine flag registers. • 70ac36e877 2022-12-14 The planner became "more aggressive about using co-routines rather than materializing"; a79e2a2d28 materializes a CTE only when it is used more than once. • 43a511927d 2024-03-18 VALUES-as-coroutine: large VALUES clauses "prepare and run in about half the time and with half the memory". f0f4323824 places the coroutine's output registers where INSERT reads them directly; 56be6f6c08, 1c5257dafc.

### sqlite-133 — Keep rare checks out of the instruction loop: poll at back-edges, piggyback, jump to the error handler
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Check interrupts and progress only on backward jumps, fold rare conditions such as out-of-memory into a flag that is already polled there, and have failing instructions jump straight to an error handler instead of testing a status in the loop condition.
Why it is faster: A test executed on every instruction is paid millions of times. Any long-running program must pass through a backward jump, so checking there is enough for responsiveness. A rare condition can raise the already-polled interrupt flag when it occurs, so it costs nothing per iteration. Removing `rc==OK` from the loop condition removes a compare and branch per instruction from the happy path.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Safepoint polls on loop back-edges in VMs; kernels fold pending work into one flag checked on syscall exit. • Web / backend: Check a cancellation token every N iterations or once per event-loop tick, not per element. • Embedded: Kick the watchdog from the main loop's back-edge.
Watch out: A piggybacked flag must be raised in every context where the rare condition can occur; SQLite's first attempt missed reentrant calls and had to be reverted. Straight-line code with no back-edges must be short enough not to need a poll.
Seen in SQLite: 49afe3aaa0 2013-07-10 The progress-callback check moved to a shared `check_for_interrupt:` label reached only from OP_Next, OP_Prev and similar jumps, which "makes up for the extra CPU cycles used to increment the cycle counter". • fabe393da8 2016-02-03 Moved the out-of-memory check from every opcode to the interrupt label ("about a 0.5% performance increase"); e514f651d0 backed it out because it missed reentrant cases; 4a642b6060 reinstated it properly: `sqlite3OomFault()` sets the interrupt flag when statements are running. The merge 0539702b8e reports "almost 1% performance gain". • 9467abf3dd 2016-02-17 The loop changed from `for(...; rc==SQLITE_OK; pOp++)` to an unconditional loop, and every opcode that can fail does `goto abort_due_to_error`; merged as 7f149e5173: "a little smaller and faster". • 94effc8a05 2023-01-12 A progress check in the code generator fires only when the label count crosses a multiple of 100.

### sqlite-134 — Keep hot opcodes simple: split variants, and lower rare cases into instruction sequences
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Give each variant of an operation its own opcode, and move optional behaviours out of frequently executed instructions into extra instructions that the code generator emits only when needed.
Why it is faster: One handler serving several opcodes, or carrying option flags, re-tests which variant or option applies on every execution. Separate opcodes choose once, at dispatch. Rare options compiled as a slightly longer sequence cost extra instructions only in the statements that use them, while every other statement runs the simpler handler. This is the RISC argument applied to bytecode.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Keep JIT IR operations simple and lower complex cases into sequences. • Web / backend: Separate request handlers per variant instead of one handler that branches on flags. • Data / databases: Separate insert operators for indexes and sorters, each without the other's checks.
Watch out: More opcodes mean a bigger interpreter and more code-generator logic; split only opcodes that are hot in real workloads.
Seen in SQLite: e1349cb0f0 2008-04-01 "Break up the implementation of OP_Move, OP_Copy, and OP_SCopy to reduce the number of branch instructions." The new OP_Move also swaps buffers instead of freeing one. • c879c4eac0 2020-02-06 Split OP_IdxInsert and OP_SorterInsert into separate opcodes. • 4bc20452b5 2021-03-29 With 269b7d09bd: removed two option flags from the comparison opcodes, "allowing them to run faster"; vector comparisons compile to new `OP_ElseEq` and `OP_ZeroOrNull` instead. • e17ab401f0 2026-04-22 Skipping an unchanged index entry on UPDATE: "Instead of adding a branch to OP_IdxInsert, use an extra OP_IfNull" (with 2a07c6de2c and 57ee077c5e).

### sqlite-135 — Put a common-type fast path at the top of polymorphic opcodes
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: In an opcode that handles any operand type, test the overwhelmingly common pairing (both integers) first using raw type flags, and decide the result directly; fall through to the generic path for everything else.
Why it is faster: Generic comparison and arithmetic must handle NULLs, type affinity, collations and mixed types. Most executions see two integers. One flag test plus a direct compare or add handles them in a few instructions, and the generic machinery runs only for the rest.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Small-integer fast paths in dynamic-language VMs (tagged small ints, fast int add). • Web / backend: A JS comparator that checks `typeof a === 'number'` first. • Data / databases: Expression evaluators that specialize int-int arithmetic and comparisons.
Watch out: The fast path must give the same answer the generic path would, including overflow behaviour for arithmetic.
Seen in SQLite: 64caee4086 2016-09-09 Comparison opcodes compare two integers inline and skip `sqlite3MemCompare()`; 5fd0c12049, e5520e2f06 and 5f6eb1a0dc trimmed them further. • 8546497383 2021-05-17 `if((flags1&flags3&MEM_Int)!=0)` compares the integers and picks the jump from lookup tables, avoiding "a lot of useless work". 3cf46ee508 math opcodes test raw flags for integers before calling the costlier type classifier.

### sqlite-136 — Peephole and whole-program passes over generated bytecode, with measured reverts
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: After code generation, merge adjacent single-slot operations into multi-slot ones, delete instructions with no effect, and remove scaffolding the whole program provably does not need; then measure, and remove passes that cost more than they save.
Why it is faster: Fewer instructions means fewer dispatches. Extending the previous instruction's count instead of emitting another copy, or using one multi-register NULL instead of several, shrinks loops that run per row. A whole-program scan can prove that no instruction can trigger a partial rollback, so the expensive journal setup can become a no-op. But the pass itself runs at prepare time, and removing a cheap instruction can cost more cycles than executing it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Peephole passes in regex VMs, template compilers and JITs. • Web / backend: Coalesce DOM writes and pipelined Redis commands; elide savepoints in an ORM when no partial rollback is possible. • Data / databases: Skip undo-log setup for statements that provably cannot fail midway.
Watch out: Prepare time is part of total cost for short statements. It is usually better to not generate redundant instructions than to generate and then remove them.

Later 2831c4d123 removed that elimination, because removing them "uses more cycles than just running them"; 3d4143a38a stopped removing no-ops at the end of programs because it "does not improve performance". 65f38d960d and 60ffc80756 instead stop generating unnecessary closes in the first place.

### sqlite-137 — Self-patching instructions: do one-time work on first execution and rewrite the opcode
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: An instruction that needs one-time setup does it on its first execution, stores the result in its own operand, and rewrites itself into a faster opcode that skips the setup thereafter.
Why it is faster: Conversions or context setup that do not change between executions are paid once per statement instead of once per row. This is the bytecode form of an inline cache: the call site remembers what it learned.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Inline caches in JS and Smalltalk VMs; lazy PLT symbol binding. • Web / backend: Memoize a compiled regex or template at the call site on first use. • Data / databases: Cache per-call-site function state in prepared query plans.
Watch out: Rewritten programs must be safe to re-run (reset or re-execution must see a valid state), and self-modification is unsafe if one program is executed by several threads at once.

### sqlite-138 — Reuse decoded values in generated code, and remove the cache when it stops paying
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Remember which register already holds a value (a decoded column, an aliased result expression) and reuse it instead of emitting code to compute it again; and re-measure such caches as the rest of the system gets faster.
Why it is faster: Decoding a column from a record or re-evaluating an aliased expression costs instructions per row. A compile-time cache turns the second use into a register read. But the cache needs invalidation whenever a register is overwritten, its type changes or control flow branches, and that bookkeeping costs compile time, code size and bugs. Once the operation it saves becomes cheap, the cache can cost more than it saves.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Value numbering on loads in compilers, with alias invalidation. • Web / backend: Memoize field decoding (JSON path, protobuf field) within one row callback; GraphQL resolvers that memoize computed fields used by several selections. • Data / databases: Dataframe engines that compute derived columns once and reference them.
Watch out: Invalidation is the hard part, as the stream of fixes shows. Re-measure old caches periodically: SQLite's removal made the engine both smaller and faster ten years after the cache was added.

### sqlite-139 — Compile lazy operators to jumps, and evaluate the cheap operand first
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Compile COALESCE, AND, OR and similar operators into conditional jumps so later operands are evaluated only when needed, and order operands so the cheap one is evaluated first and can make the expensive one unnecessary.
Why it is faster: Calling COALESCE as a function evaluates every argument up front, even when the first is non-NULL. Jumps stop at the first non-NULL argument, which matters when a later argument is a subquery. For most binary operators, a NULL operand makes the result NULL, so evaluating the side without a subquery first can skip the subquery entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `a ?? b()` rather than `coalesce(a, b())`; order filter predicates by cost. • Data / databases: Compile CASE, AND and OR as jumps in any query engine. • Native / systems: Rule engines that evaluate cheap guards before expensive conditions.
Watch out: Reordering is only valid when operands have no side effects and the operator's semantics allow it (three-valued logic for NULL).

### sqlite-140 — Use static knowledge to omit runtime checks and conversions
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: When the compiler can prove a value is never NULL, already has the right type, or will be checked by a later instruction anyway, emit no check or conversion for it.
Why it is faster: Each generated NULL test or type conversion is an instruction per row. A conservative analysis ("can this expression be NULL?") removes them where the answer is certainly no. Erring on the safe side only costs speed, never correctness.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Type-guided null-check elimination in compilers and validators; skip re-validating data that already passed a schema check. • Data / databases: Avoid redundant casts in generated SQL or query plans. • Native / systems: Use type information to remove bounds and null checks.
Watch out: The analysis must be conservative; a wrong "never NULL" is a wrong answer, not just a slow one.

### sqlite-141 — Have producers write straight into the consumer's registers
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Allocate registers so that a producer's output lands exactly where the next stage reads it, and hand callees a pointer to the final slot instead of a temporary that is copied back.
Why it is faster: Every copy between stages is an instruction per row and often a buffer copy. Laying out registers so SELECT output is already the INSERT input, or giving a function a pointer to its result register, removes those copies and the code that manages them.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Return-value optimization and emplacement in C++; out-parameters for SIMD kernels. • Web / backend: Streaming parsers that write into the caller's buffer. • Data / databases: Operator pipelines that share column vectors between stages.
Watch out: Shared registers couple stages tightly; a later change to one stage's layout can silently clobber the other's values.

### sqlite-142 — Let instructions take arrays of registers instead of serialized records
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: When an operation needs a multi-field key, pass it the already-decoded register array rather than building a serialized record for it to parse back.
Why it is faster: Serializing values into a record only for the callee to unpack them again is pure overhead: a record-building instruction, a buffer, and a decode. Taking registers directly removes all three.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: In-process calls should not JSON-encode and decode; pass objects or zero-copy views. • Data / databases: Index lookups that take a key tuple rather than an encoded key where both sides are in memory. • Native / systems: Cap'n Proto or FlatBuffers-style views across layers.
Watch out: Where the serialized form is needed anyway (it is stored), building it once is still required; this only removes round-trips.
Seen in SQLite: e14006d0de 2008-03-25 IdxDelete takes a register array, which "avoids a needless packing and unpacking of the record"; 751de567c1 does the same for seek opcodes. • 8cff69df11 2009-11-12 OP_Found and OP_NotFound accept unpacked registers, avoiding MakeRecord during IN processing; 91fc4a0c4d converted more call sites. • 4b727f17d6 2016-11-11 OP_IdxInsert takes unpacked key registers directly: speedtest1 2.6% faster.

### sqlite-143 — Embed constants in the instruction in binary form
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Convert literals to their binary value at compile time and store them directly in instruction operands, rather than as text to parse at run time or in a separately allocated operand.
Why it is faster: Parsing a number from text on every execution is wasted work. A constant held in a separately allocated operand costs an allocation, a pointer chase on each use and a free. A value split across two existing 32-bit operand fields, or a specialized opcode with the constant as an immediate, avoids all three.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Immediate operands instead of constant-pool lookups in bytecode designs; small values stored inline in tagged words. • Web / backend: Template and config DSLs that pre-parse literals when compiled. • Data / databases: Store parsed values, not text, in cached query plans.
Watch out: Packing values into existing fields needs careful handling of signedness and byte order, plus debug output that can still show the value.
Seen in SQLite: 598f134020 2007-10-23 64-bit integers and doubles are stored in opcodes "in binary, not as text. Performance improvement", with negation folded in at code generation. • e05c929b78 2009-10-29 `rowid IN (1,2,3)` uses a new `OP_InsertInt` with the integer in an operand, replacing evaluate, MustBeInt and Insert, and "reduce[s] the memory required by prepared statements". • 255f7220fd 2026-08-20 With d7a233c837: OP_Int64, OP_Real and OP_ColumnsUsed carry their 64-bit value in two 32-bit operands instead of an allocated operand: "Slightly smaller and slightly faster."

### sqlite-144 — Guard one-time initialization with a run-once instruction, and reset it with an epoch counter
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Wrap setup that must happen once per statement (building a subquery table, an automatic index, a CTE materialization) in a run-once instruction, and implement "has it run?" by comparing against a per-run generation number instead of clearing flags.
Why it is faster: Setup inside a loop that is only needed once costs a full re-run per iteration. A run-once guard skips it after the first pass. Clearing an array of "has run" flags at every start is a memset; bumping one generation counter and comparing each instruction's stamp with it makes reset O(1) and removes the array.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Visited marks in graph search that store a stamp instead of being cleared between queries; frame-number invalidation in game engines. • Web / backend: `call_once` or module-level memoization for lazy setup. • Data / databases: Materialize a shared subexpression once per query.
Watch out: Generation counters wrap; handle overflow with a full reset, as SQLite does at a threshold.
Seen in SQLite: 48f2d3b10a 2011-09-16 `OP_Once` guards one-time initialization such as subquery tables and automatic indexes. • 9e5eb9c8cc 2016-09-18 OP_Once compares its own operand with a counter on OP_Init that increments on each run; the flag array and its memset were removed, with a full reset only when the counter passes a threshold: "smaller, faster, and uses less memory". • fd8be0ca94 2026-10-22 With 31ebfdf4a1: a materialized CTE referenced inside a loop runs its setup and cursor duplication under OP_Once, leaving only a cheap reset per pass.

### sqlite-145 — Shape the grammar and the LR engine so each token needs fewer actions
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Reduce the number of parser actions per token (merge a shift followed by a reduce, eliminate unit and empty reductions, write the result in place) and tune the parser's own loop like an interpreter's.
Why it is faster: An LR parser executes one table lookup and one action per step. An empty production (`opt ::= .`) adds a reduction to every input where the option is absent; a unit rule that converts one nonterminal into another adds a reduction with no effect. Combining shift and reduce into one action, or adding grammar alternatives for the common no-clause form, removes those steps. The engine's loop benefits from the same tricks as a VM: pointer-based stack, stored end pointer, hot test first, no per-call context argument, and a small initial stack embedded in the parser that grows on the heap only when needed.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Tune yacc, Bison or Lemon grammars by removing epsilon and unit rules; improvements to a parser generator help every grammar built with it. • Web / backend: PEG and hand-written parsers that build target objects directly instead of generic syntax nodes (as serde does for JSON). • Data / databases: Query-language front ends where prepare time matters for short statements.
Watch out: Grammar restructuring can change precedence or introduce conflicts; diff the generated tables and run the full parser test suite after each change.
Seen in SQLite: 490a73bcde 2005-02-01 Skip the stack-overflow check on a shift that follows a reduce, since the reduce already popped entries. • 3bd48ab213 2015-09-07 With 3921d8be5d: a SHIFTREDUCE action merges a shift immediately followed by a reduce, compressing tables and speeding parsing. c173ad8011 avoids no-op unit reductions; cf82f0d3d2 lets reduce code write the left-hand side into the right-hand-side stack slot; b7997fe3a9, dab943cf3d (skip error-recovery bookkeeping when disabled), abecc0b883, 743606c3d3, 2881ab6298. • 118ab65859 2016-05-23 Parser stack index became a pointer: "about 6.6% parser performance gain". a441404f92 turned a fallback recursion into a loop so the lookup inlines. • bd8fcc130a 2017-06-28 Promote SHIFTREDUCE on nonterminals to pure REDUCE. ef53a9f0af "avoid unnecessary tests for the acceptance state"; 8b471e7e79 stored stack-end pointer; 6be95366c7 negated symbol counts; a5746e0317 "faster and slightly smaller parser"; fb32c44e41 stores the parse context in the parser object instead of passing it per token; 9b38d6660f the tokenizer loop advances a pointer. • 550a33091b 2018-07-27 Fewer empty-rule reductions; 2424aa723b removed an empty production. 200adc9e75 adds a FROM-clause alternative without INDEXED BY: "faster parsing of the FROM clause". e81f879f51 moved the stack-overflow check out of `yy_reduce()`, "a single conditional in the main loop of the parser". • ad2689fe4e 2024-01-27 The parser stack starts in a small embedded array and grows with realloc, eliminating "parser stack overflow"; 7659ce22c5 made it "faster than trunk and is less than 300 bytes larger"; 21bdfe5884 dropped two tests. • fa7abfdf0b 2025-12-04 A rule that built a generic name list and patched it was split into four productions that build the final object directly: "Performance optimization in the language grammar."

### sqlite-146 — Compress parser tables
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Shrink generated LR tables with small integer indices instead of pointers, a shared row-displacement array checked by a lookahead array, fallback tokens instead of duplicate rules, and the narrowest element type that fits.
Why it is faster: Parser tables are large and sparse. Smaller tables mean a smaller binary and better cache behaviour during parsing. Overlaying all state rows into one array with per-state offsets (a comb vector) stores only the non-empty entries. Keeping the token count under 256 lets tables use 8-bit entries. Letting keywords fall back to the identifier token removes dozens of grammar rules.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Sparse-table compression for DFA lexers and Unicode property tables. • Embedded: Index-based instead of pointer-based tables in ROM; minimal element widths for generated tables. • Web / backend: Smaller generated parsers mean smaller JS and WASM bundles.
Watch out: Compression must keep lookup cheap; a scheme that saves bytes but adds a loop per lookup can slow parsing. Padding a table to drop range checks (see the padding entry) is the opposite trade and can be worth a few hundred bytes.
Seen in SQLite: b29b0a5227 2002-02-23 Collision-chain pointers became small integer indices: "reduces the size of the library by 50K". 0bd1f4ea5a added `%fallback`, saving 15K by removing `id ::= KEYWORD` rules. • 8b582012de 2003-10-21 A single shared action array indexed by a per-state offset and validated by a lookahead array. 2d3917da29 numbers tokens used together close to each other; 0bb132bedc merges identical destructors and reduce actions; ada354db0e sorts states so trailing ones need no entries (about 1.5KiB); fd405314d3 about 0.5KiB more; fa3b19ef3d keeps the token count small enough for one byte. • 7ba5bc5bf2 2009-09-22 Removed two tokens so "the parser tables can go back to using 8-bit values instead of 16-bit values". 1441f3e083 omits unused trailing fallback entries; f16371d650 makes action tables more compact and removes "array bounds tests that can never fail"; 4dc8ef520a and 32c4d743bc improve sharing. • 8395b7b6cb 2012-01-28 Removed unnecessary grammar nonterminals. f59b12fbc1 with ecaa9d399c added `%token_class`: "small size reduction and speed increase". • cfc45b1021 2018-12-03 Split an array of `{u16, i8}` rule info into two parallel arrays to "reduce the size of the parser tables" by removing padding.

### sqlite-147 — Generate the keyword recognizer offline: packed strings, a tuned hash, early rejects
Lens: jit-interpreters · Source: SQLite
Principle: Build keyword lookup tables with a generator script: overlap the keyword strings, hash into short chains ordered by frequency, and reject most non-keywords on length or first characters before any string comparison.
Why it is faster: Keyword lookup runs for every identifier-like token. A generated hash gives near-constant lookup; ordering each chain by keyword frequency ends common lookups after one comparison. Rejecting on length, on the first two characters, or on a character that cannot start a keyword avoids comparisons entirely. Overlapping strings (a short keyword embedded in a longer one) shrinks the table, and knowing the input is letters lets case-insensitive comparison be one mask.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Reserved-word tables in compilers and lexers; tail-merged string tables in linkers. • Web / backend: HTTP method, header and command lookup with length and first-character prefilters. • Embedded: Compact command tables in CLI firmware.
Watch out: Generated tables must be regenerated whenever the keyword set changes; keep the generator in the build so the table never drifts from the grammar.

### sqlite-148 — Let builds remove whole features and debug machinery at compile time
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Put optional features, debug checks, statistics and threading support behind compile-time switches so they vanish from builds that do not want them, and place optional APIs in separate translation units so linkers drop them when unused.
Why it is faster: Code that is compiled in costs bytes and often run-time checks even when idle (mutex calls in a single-threaded build, debug bookkeeping, statistics counters). Removing it at build time costs nothing at run time. A function in its own object file is dropped by a static linker when nothing references it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Feature flags with dead-code elimination (`DefinePlugin`, `process.env.NODE_ENV`), tree-shaking, and stripping dev-only asserts from bundles. • Embedded: Kconfig-style options; a no-threading variant of a library; logging stripped below a compile-time level. • Native / systems: Cargo features and `cfg`; one function per object file with `--gc-sections`.
Watch out: Every option multiplies the build matrix; untested combinations break silently (SQLite had to fix the no-float build). Retire options that no longer earn their keep.

### sqlite-149 — Delete rarely used code and merge near-duplicates
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Remove undocumented extras and niche subsystems, merge near-duplicate routines into one parameterized function, and collapse structures that are always handled together.
Why it is faster: Code that few users need still adds bytes, branches and tests for everyone. Two near-copies cost twice the instruction-cache footprint of one routine. Combining always-paired fields into one node removes duplicated handling in every pass over them. These changes often make the common path faster too, because there is less to branch around.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Drop polyfills for unsupported browsers; collapse near-duplicate route handlers and helpers. • Embedded: Trim printf implementations and API surface to cut flash use. • Native / systems: Retire custom allocators once the system allocator is good enough; remove platform shims for dead targets.
Watch out: Merging specialized routines into a generic one can slow a hot path; that is the opposite trade to specialization. Merge cold code, specialize hot code, and measure both size and cycles.

Unused retry code was also removed (0bb56392d4). • 2f44e94efb 2019-04-05 Removed HTTP/1.1 pipelining (28 files, -1394/+183 lines).

### sqlite-150 — Price every optimization in bytes as well as cycles
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Report the code-size change next to the speed change for each optimization, and choose the variant with the best cycles-per-byte rather than the fastest one at any size.
Why it is faster: Many speedups (inlining, cloning, unrolling, bigger tables) grow the binary, and growth eventually costs instruction-cache misses and flash space. Measuring both makes the trade explicit: a change worth 2.5 million cycles for 100 bytes is clearly good; one that needs 18% more code for most of the gain may be better as a partial version. Many changes turn out to improve both at once.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Bundle-size budgets in CI next to performance budgets. • Embedded: Firmware flash budgets with per-change size reports. • Native / systems: Track per-function size after LTO alongside instruction counts in benchmark CI.
Watch out: Optimization level changes the picture (SQLite benchmarks at -Os, where the compiler inlines less); measure with the flags you ship.
Seen in SQLite: 375beb0ec0 2015-06-27 "2.5 million cycles at a cost of less than 100 bytes". 1ffd247c0c lazy cell-size caching: "overall 1.7% performance gain for about 1000 extra bytes"; 9c739e26da "2.6% faster overall with a size increase of less than 750 bytes". • 19ae01be13 2022-02-23 A specialized cell-size routine: at least 0.5M cycles for under 200 bytes. 0150c81cd3 reordered value fields for "about 750K cycles with only a 4-byte increase in code size". • d3b165a85e 2026-08-07 A deliberate trade in the other direction: a few cycles given up for a smaller binary. • 4616815533 2026-08-16 Partial inlining: "96% of the performance increase with only 18% of the object code size increase"; 5a16bb33d6 traded a little more speed for under 700 bytes.

### sqlite-151 — Keep constant data read-only and shared, and encode tables compactly
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite · Also: curl
Principle: Declare lookup tables `const` so they live in the read-only segment, keep process-wide constant registries in one static structure instead of rebuilding them per connection, and store tables in compact forms.
Why it is faster: Non-const tables occupy the writable data segment and are copied per process; const tables can be shared between processes and need no initialization. A registry rebuilt for every connection costs time and memory per connection. Compact table formats (inline short strings, narrower element types, two-level tables) cut both binary size and cache footprint.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `static const` tables in C; shared immutable registries in multi-tenant servers. • Web / backend: Module-level frozen constants instead of per-request tables; prune unused locale data. • Embedded: Tables in flash, not RAM; minimal element widths.
Watch out: Two-level or packed tables add a little arithmetic per lookup; keep the hottest tables flat.

SASL `mechtable` made static (2d4413fefd).

### sqlite-152 — Shape internal APIs for small call sites
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Remove return values nobody uses, prefer natural-width integer types, merge alternative function pointers into one, and provide combined helpers for operations always done together.
Why it is faster: Each call site pays for its arguments and for handling the return. An unused return value still generates code; `u8` or `u16` parameters force zero- or sign-extension; two separate calls (add an instruction, then set its operand) cost two call sequences everywhere. Small savings per call site add up because helpers like these are called from hundreds of places.
Applies elsewhere: Embedded: Firmware size budgets: void returns, natural-width parameters, fewer helper variants. • Web / backend: Smaller API surfaces and merged helpers shrink JS bundles. • Native / systems: Builder-style "emit and set operand" helpers in compilers and code generators.
Watch out: Dropping a return value removes error information; only do it when no caller can act on the error.
