### sqlite-153 — Put hot struct fields at small offsets so instructions encode shorter
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: SQLite
Principle: Order struct fields so frequently accessed ones come first, within the range of the shortest address displacement, and move rarely used fields to the end.
Why it is faster: On x86, a field offset below 128 fits in a one-byte displacement; larger offsets need four bytes. Every access to a hot field pays this in instruction bytes, so moving debug counters and rarely called function pointers to the end of a struct shrinks the code that touches it. Platform-conditional helpers that compile to nothing when no work is needed (alignment that is already guaranteed) save similar bytes.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Kernel and runtime structs with hot fields first; generated structs reordered by access frequency. • Embedded: Architectures with short-offset addressing modes (Thumb, many MCUs) reward the same ordering. • Data / databases: Hot fields of per-row and per-cursor objects at the front.
Watch out: Field order also affects padding and cache-line sharing; check both, and do not reorder structs that are part of an ABI.
Seen in SQLite: 6d156e464d 2005-05-20 "Reorder structure fields to reduce code footprint": hit and miss counters and several function pointers moved after the hot flags in the pager struct. • cf6e3fd787 2022-04-01 `ROUND8P(x)` is just `(x)` when pointers are 8 bytes, removing rounding arithmetic on 64-bit platforms.

### sqlite-154 — Pay for durability only where it is needed: fewer, weaker, better-placed syncs
Lens: io · Source: SQLite · Also: Netty
Principle: Treat every flush-to-media call as the dominant cost of a commit. Use the weakest primitive that still gives the guarantee you need, sync once per durability boundary rather than per write, and offer modes that move the sync to a coarser point.
Why it is faster: An fsync waits for the device to acknowledge stable storage, often milliseconds on rotating disks or flash with a write cache to drain. `fdatasync` skips metadata such as mtime that recovery does not need. Syncing a log only at checkpoint time instead of each commit turns N flushes into one. The trade-off is explicit: weaker modes risk losing the last few transactions on power loss, but never corrupting the database, so it becomes a per-application choice instead of a fixed tax.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Group commit in job queues and event stores; expose a per-write durability level (like Postgres `synchronous_commit=off`) for data that can be regenerated. • Native / systems: Use `fdatasync` for append-only logs; reserve `F_FULLFSYNC` / `FlushFileBuffers` for commit points; batch fsync across many producers. • Embedded: On flash, sync at transaction boundaries, not after each record; each sync costs a program/erase cycle and battery. • Data / databases: Sync the WAL, not the data file, per commit; sync the data file only at checkpoint.
Watch out: Know exactly what each primitive guarantees on each OS. `fdatasync` semantics differ by platform, which is why SQLite gated it to Linux. Weaker modes must still keep the store consistent after a crash, so place the remaining syncs where ordering matters.
Seen in SQLite: 0b647ffd47 2009-03-21 Always use `fdatasync()` instead of `fsync()` when available, even when the file size changes, because fdatasync flushes the inode for size changes. 4a7384a25f 2009-03-21 made it the default; 892404323f 2009-03-25 limited it to Linux. • d138dd8687 2008-10-15 Option to skip the directory sync after creating or deleting a journal. • 04335886a1 2008-09-26 `journal_mode=TRUNCATE` commits by truncating the journal to zero length without calling fsync. • 6703239d2f 2010-04-17 In WAL mode with `synchronous=NORMAL`, the WAL is not synced after every transaction, only at checkpoint. • d992b150c7 2011-12-20, 4e5e108e83 2011-12-23 When padding a WAL commit to a sector boundary, sync once at the crossing point instead of again after the padding ("Avoid excess syncs"). • c97d84638e 2010-11-19 `checkpoint_fullfsync` applies the very expensive macOS `F_FULLFSYNC` barrier to checkpoints only, not every commit. • Also seen in Netty (netty-150): 65876fa7fb 2012-04-30 `DiskFileUpload` moves `fileChannel.force(false)` out of the write loop: "optimize force() position after multiple writes not at each step".

### sqlite-155 — Never write or log data whose content does not matter
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: An undo/redo log only needs entries whose old or new content will ever be read. Freed blocks, blocks about to be truncated away and blocks beyond the final size can skip both the log and the data write.
Why it is faster: Every journaled page costs one extra write plus its share of a sync. Large deletes and inserts touch many pages that end up on the free list; skipping them removes most of the I/O of the operation. The decision is made from metadata already in memory (free-list membership, the final file size), so the check is essentially free.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Skip WAL records for deallocated extents; drop log records superseded within the same transaction; TRIM instead of zeroing freed space. • Native / systems: Copy-on-write snapshots and incremental backups that skip unallocated blocks; filesystems that do not journal data blocks being freed. • Web / backend: Event sourcing compaction that never replicates events for entities deleted in the same batch.
Watch out: The "irrelevant" judgement must hold across crash recovery. If a freed page can be reused and written within the same transaction, the reuse path must journal it. The SQLite commit that restored a lost optimization shows how easily such shortcuts silently regress.
Seen in SQLite: 30e58750c1 2002-03-02 Trunk/leaf free list: "do not write or journal free pages. This results in a 2x performance gain for large INSERTs and a 5x performance gain for large DELETEs." • 4b845d7ef4 2002-03-05 Page 1 (the header) is written to the journal and database only if it actually changes, not just because a transaction started. • d3627afc47 2006-12-18 Pages pulled off the free list are not journaled or rolled back. 0787db6519 2007-03-04 avoids journaling pages of a table that is being deleted. • 45b355dc4f 2010-04-01 Free-list leaf pages fetched with `noContent` are not journaled, "restores an optimization that was accidentally undone". • 93b4fc78cf 2011-04-07 While searching the free-list trunk chain, only the trunk actually modified is marked writable, not every trunk passed. • ce8e5ffe1c 2011-04-05 A WAL commit skips pages numbered beyond the database's final size. • bc1a3c6ce2 2013-02-23 Delay truncating the file until after commit, so pages being removed "need not be journalled". 51f0b6d568 2013-02-22 moves each page only once during incremental vacuum.

### sqlite-156 — Don't read what you will overwrite, discard, or not inspect
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Before fetching a block, ask whether its current content will be used. Content about to be overwritten, freed, or needed only for its metadata (type, length, key) should not be read from storage.
Why it is faster: A read miss is a blocking device round trip. Large values in SQLite span chains of overflow pages, so reading just to learn a length or type can cost megabytes of I/O. Handing back a zeroed buffer for a page that will be fully rewritten, or answering from the record header, removes the read entirely. A deferred "needs read" flag lets the page be filled later only if someone actually looks at it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HEAD or range requests instead of full GETs; return object metadata from an index instead of fetching the blob from object storage. • Native / systems: Map buffers with discard semantics (GPU `DISCARD`, `O_TRUNC`) when the whole region will be rewritten; skip copy-on-write reads for blocks that are fully overwritten. • Data / databases: Store length and type in a fixed header ahead of variable payloads, and push projections down so readers skip unselected Parquet columns.
Watch out: A page fetched without content must never be read by anyone who expects real data; SQLite tracks this with an explicit flag and fills it lazily. The format must carry the metadata in a header for metadata-only reads to work at all.
Seen in SQLite: 0787db6519 2007-03-04 Do not read the original content of pages being pulled off the free list. • 9444081a3b 2007-03-06 Do not read the last page of an overflow chain when deleting the chain; compute the chain length instead. 8c0a959f35 2007-04-30 avoids reading overflow pages when moving chains to the free list. • 538f570cd1 2007-04-13 Generalizes this into a `noContent` fetch plus a pager `needRead` flag, so the read happens later only if the page is written; fixes several performance regressions. • c461a455c4 2012-03-28 `length()` and `typeof()` set `OPFLAG_LENGTHARG`/`OPFLAG_TYPEOFARG` so `OP_Column` avoids "loading overflow pages just to satisfy typeof() or length()". • 921acff927 2022-10-13 `IS NULL`/`NOT NULL` tests read only the serial type, which "avoids loading large strings or blobs off of disk". • 077efc2730 2023-06-22 `octet_length()` reads the byte length from the record header with no overflow I/O. 4bf24c8830 2024-11-04 the pre-update hook returns the integer key without loading the old record.

### sqlite-157 — Compare before writing: dirty only what really changed
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: When the new content may equal the old, compare first and skip the write. A no-op update should not mark storage dirty, enter the log, or trigger index maintenance.
Why it is faster: Marking a page dirty commits you to journaling it, writing it back and syncing it. A `memcmp` of a few hundred bytes is orders of magnitude cheaper. Many real updates rewrite a row with mostly identical values; with the comparison, unchanged pages never reach the journal or WAL.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORM dirty tracking that omits unchanged columns; skip the UPDATE entirely when nothing changed; idempotent config writes that avoid waking file watchers. • Embedded: Read-compare-write for EEPROM and flash to save erase cycles and wear. • Data / databases: Search indexers and materialized views that skip re-indexing documents whose indexed fields are byte-identical. • Native / systems: Build systems that write an output file only when its bytes differ, so downstream steps are not invalidated.
Watch out: The comparison is wasted work when updates almost always change content. It pays when the compare is cheap relative to the write path (journal plus sync), which is true for storage but not necessarily for small in-memory values.
Seen in SQLite: 4b845d7ef4 2002-03-05 The header page is journaled and rewritten only if its content changes. • 3de5d16cf7 2018-05-03, e3c05a5597 2018-05-07 `btreeOverwriteCell`: "Use memcmp() first to avoid dirtying pages that are unchanged", plus a zero-run check. 89ee229810 2018-05-07 extends it to index b-trees. • 2a07c6de2c 2026-04-22, e17ab401f0 2026-04-22, 57ee077c5e 2026-04-22 On UPDATE of an expression index, `OP_IdxDelete` compares the stored key with the new record and, on a match, the delete+insert pair is skipped. The skip is an extra conditional jump in generated code, so the hot insert opcode stays branch-free.

### sqlite-158 — Overwrite in place instead of delete-plus-insert or append
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a replacement has the same size and shape as the original, write it over the old bytes. Keep encoded sizes stable on edits so the overwrite remains possible, and let a log overwrite its own earlier entry for the same key within a transaction.
Why it is faster: Delete-plus-insert frees space, may defragment the page, searches for new space and may split or rebalance. An in-place overwrite is one `memcpy` on an already dirty page. In a log, overwriting a frame written earlier in the same transaction stops the log from growing with dead versions that the checkpoint would otherwise copy.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Fixed-width record updates in files; HOT-style updates that stay on the same page; write buffers that coalesce repeated writes to the same key before flushing. • Native / systems: Patch serialized formats (protobuf with padded varints, BSON, MessagePack) in place instead of re-serializing. • Embedded: Rewrite configuration records in place on NOR flash when the new value fits in the reserved slot.
Watch out: Denormalized encodings (wider headers than needed) trade a few bytes for stable sizes. Checksums or derived data that are deferred must be recomputed before anything can read them.
Seen in SQLite: 80159da6c6 2016-12-09 `sqlite3BtreeInsert()` overwrites the existing cell when the new size equals the old and there is no overflow (bound fixed in 2d08343a91 2016-12-09). • c6c9e158c5 2016-11-10 REPLACE on a WITHOUT ROWID table without secondary indexes overwrites directly instead of the delete sequence. • d6f7c97952 2016-01-09 (merged 87dc2bc841 2016-01-11) A page written to the WAL more than once in a transaction overwrites its earlier frame, which keeps the WAL from growing. • c9a9022b21 2016-01-09 When frames will be overwritten, frame checksums are not computed on append; `walRewriteChecksums()` recomputes once at commit, starting at the first overwritten frame. • 3a7042e0e2 2025-04-01 (merged 2cbc485ea3 2025-04-01) `jsonBlobOverwrite()` pads a slightly smaller JSONB element with a wider length header so the total size is unchanged, avoiding "unnecessary page updates and memmove() operations".

### sqlite-159 — Create logs and coordination files only when what they protect can actually happen
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Undo journals, statement savepoints and multi-file commit records exist to handle a specific failure or rollback. Prove statically or at runtime when that case cannot occur, and skip creating the file entirely; otherwise create it on first real write.
Why it is faster: Creating a file costs open, directory metadata updates and possibly a directory sync, and deleting it costs as much again. A statement that writes one row or cannot abort halfway never needs a statement journal; a commit that touches one database never needs a master journal. Skipping them removes whole syscalls and syncs from the commit path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ORMs that skip savepoints for single-row statements; skip two-phase commit when only one participant writes. • Native / systems: Create log files on the first message; skip undo-log setup for read-only request handlers. • Data / databases: Elide statement-level rollback scaffolding when the compiled plan cannot fail midway.
Watch out: The proof that rollback is impossible must be conservative: a missed abort path turns a speedup into silent corruption. Lazily created files also move errors (disk full, permissions) to later, less expected points.

### sqlite-160 — Scale commit work to what was actually written
Lens: io · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: A commit protocol should cost nothing for participants that wrote nothing. Skip the commit when no changes were made, and run expensive phases only for writers.
Why it is faster: Commit is where journals are finalized, headers rewritten and files synced. Read-only transactions and unmodified databases need none of it, but a generic loop over all participants pays the full price unless it checks. The check is one flag per participant.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Skip `flush()` on clean ORM sessions; distributed transactions with the read-only 2PC optimization. • Data / databases: Write-back caches that do nothing at commit for clean pages.
Watch out: Read-only participants may still need to release locks or end snapshots correctly; skip the durable work, not the bookkeeping.

### sqlite-161 — Invalidate and recycle log files instead of deleting and recreating them
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Deleting a file is a directory-metadata operation, often followed by a directory sync. Marking a log invalid by overwriting its header, or truncating it, and reusing the same file next time is far cheaper.
Why it is faster: Overwriting 28 bytes in an already open file is a single write. Delete costs an unlink, an inode update and, for durability, a sync of the parent directory; the next transaction then pays a create. Keeping the file and its in-memory size also avoids `stat` and `truncate` calls between uses.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Recycle WAL segment files instead of deleting them (as Postgres and Kafka do). • Embedded: On flash filesystems, mark records invalid instead of erasing; keep log files open and append. • Native / systems: Reuse temp files across batches; ring-buffer log segments.
Watch out: A persisted but invalidated file must be unambiguously invalid after a crash (zeroed header, checksum), and it holds disk space between uses.

### sqlite-162 — Keep scratch data in memory or fast storage; spill only past a threshold
Lens: io · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Data that never has to survive a crash should not go through durable storage. Hold it in RAM and spill to a file only when it outgrows a threshold; when it must hit a file, put that file on the fastest available medium.
Why it is faster: Most temporary data (statement journals, sort runs, temp tables) is small and short-lived. In memory it costs no syscalls and no syncs; if it never grows past the threshold, the file is never created. The threshold bounds memory use for the rare large case.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Buffer uploads in memory below a size limit (Python `SpooledTemporaryFile`); spill-to-disk thresholds for sort and hash-join operators. • Native / systems: Point build intermediates and test scratch at tmpfs. • Data / databases: Unlogged staging tables; in-memory temp spaces with a size cap.
Watch out: Pick the threshold by measurement and cap total memory; many concurrent spillable buffers can exhaust RAM before any one of them spills.

### sqlite-163 — Ask the storage what it guarantees and drop safeguards it makes redundant
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Journaling and padding exist to survive torn or non-atomic writes. If the device or filesystem reports atomic writes, safe appends, ordered writes or power-safe overwrite, skip the protection it already provides, and cache the probe result.
Why it is faster: A rollback journal doubles writes and adds syncs. If a batch of page writes is atomic at the device, the journal is pure overhead. If a crash cannot damage unwritten bytes in a sector, the engine can stop padding and journaling whole sectors around each change. The capability is a bitmask queried once, so the decision costs nothing per write.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Disable double-write buffers on filesystems or SSDs with atomic writes; rely on ZFS/btrfs copy-on-write instead of an application WAL where safe. • Embedded: Choose journal mode by storage type; align block size to the physical erase or sector size. • Web / backend: Use object stores' atomic multi-part commit instead of client-side staging plus rename.
Watch out: Capabilities reported wrongly cause corruption, not slowness. Keep a tested fallback path (SQLite retries in journal mode on I/O error) and make the trusted capabilities explicit and configurable.

### sqlite-164 — Make storage access sequential: allocate adjacent and ascending, bulk-load in order
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Place logically adjacent data at physically adjacent, ascending offsets, and when building a structure from sorted input, fill blocks fully in order.
Why it is faster: Sequential offsets benefit from OS read-ahead, write merging and, on disks, no seeks; even on SSDs large sequential I/O beats scattered small I/O. A B-tree built in key order with full pages has fewer pages, so every later scan does less I/O. The cost is a little allocation logic at write time.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Sorted bulk index builds with high fill factor; time-ordered keys (UUIDv7) to keep inserts at the right edge; table rewrites (`pg_repack`) that copy in index order. • Native / systems: Extent-based file allocation; arena allocators that cluster related objects. • Embedded: Write whole erase blocks sequentially in flash loggers.
Watch out: Ordering writes is not automatically a win: SQLite tried sorting dirty pages before writing in 03eb96a7b2 2002-11-10 and reverted it a day later in 4d9ef4c4ef 2002-11-11 because "Additional measurements showed no performance gains". Full pages also make later random inserts split more often.

### sqlite-165 — Write back from a log in target order, newest version only, resuming from a watermark
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: When folding a log into the main store, sort entries by destination, keep only the latest version of each block, start after what is already applied, and choose the sorting method by input size.
Why it is faster: A WAL is ordered by time, which makes replay random I/O against the data file and repeats writes for blocks changed several times. Sorting by page number makes write-back sequential and deduplicated. Skipping frames already backfilled turns repeated checkpoints of a large log from O(log size) to O(new frames).
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: LSM memtable flushes and compaction in key order; elevator ordering of dirty-page flushes in buffer pools. • Web / backend: Log shippers and stream consumers that resume from a committed offset; batch key-value writes sorted by key. • Native / systems: Incremental GC or compaction that never rescans already-processed regions.
Watch out: The watermark must be updated only after the corresponding writes are durable, or a crash leaves a gap that is never replayed.

### sqlite-166 — Read without intermediate copies: memory-mapping, direct reads, borrowed pointers
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Let consumers read data where it already sits, in a memory-mapped file or a cache buffer, or read large values straight into the destination instead of staging them in a cache.
Why it is faster: A buffered `read()` copies kernel page cache into a user buffer, then often into an application cache, then into the consumer. mmap removes the syscall and one copy; a direct read of a large blob avoids polluting and churning the cache with pages read once. An ephemeral pointer into a cache page removes a `memcpy` when the consumer uses the bytes immediately.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `sendfile` for static assets; zero-copy byte slices (Netty, Rust `Bytes`) between protocol layers. • Data / databases: mmap'd read-only index segments (LMDB, Lucene MMapDirectory); zero-copy formats (FlatBuffers, Arrow). • Native / systems: `O_DIRECT` for large one-shot reads so they do not evict hot cache pages.
Watch out: mmap turns I/O errors into signals (SIGBUS) and file truncation by another process into crashes; borrowed pointers are valid only until the buffer can move, so their lifetime must be provably short.

### sqlite-167 — Track file state in memory instead of asking the kernel
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: If the program can know a file's size, existence or reusability from its own state, keep it in a variable and skip `stat`, `fstat`, `truncate`, `open` and similar calls.
Why it is faster: Each syscall is a user/kernel transition plus kernel locking, typically microseconds, and some (truncate, open) touch filesystem metadata. On paths run per transaction or per file open, avoiding one call is a measurable share. A global counter can gate a lookup so it only runs when something could be found.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Cache file existence and size in static servers and build tools; keep file handles and connections pooled. • Native / systems: Use a short read to detect EOF instead of a prior `fstat`; avoid `os.stat` in hot Python paths. • Embedded: Keep filesystem metadata in RAM rather than re-querying a slow flash FS.
Watch out: In-memory knowledge goes stale if another process can change the file; this is safe only for state the program owns or validates by other means (locks, change counters).

### sqlite-168 — Do each I/O in as few, aligned syscalls as possible
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Assemble small fields into one buffer before writing, use positional I/O instead of seek-then-read, and buffer streams into aligned, block-sized transfers.
Why it is faster: Syscall overhead is per call, not per byte, so several 4-byte writes cost several times one 28-byte write. Seek-plus-read is two calls where `pread` is one. Unaligned or sub-block writes force the kernel or device into read-modify-write cycles.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Build response headers in one buffer; use `writev`; Node `fs.read` with an explicit position. • Native / systems: `pread`/`pwrite` or io_uring with offsets; 4 KiB-aligned log appenders. • Embedded: SD and flash drivers that write whole erase blocks.
Watch out: Buffering delays visibility and durability; flush explicitly at commit points.

### sqlite-169 — Grow files in large chunks and record the logical size separately
Lens: io · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Extend files by preallocated chunks rather than per write, and store the logical size in a header so the physical file may be larger.
Why it is faster: Each extension updates the file size and block maps, which must be journaled by the filesystem and synced for durability, and small extensions fragment the file. One `fallocate` of a large chunk amortizes that metadata work over many writes and keeps the file contiguous.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Preallocate log and LSM segments; size hints before bulk loads. • Embedded: Reserve flash for loggers and video recorders up front. • Native / systems: `fallocate` VM disk images and download targets.
Watch out: Preallocation wastes space for small stores and needs a separate logical size, or readers will treat the zero tail as data.
Seen in SQLite: ed37690afd 2010-03-13, dd3cd977cc 2010-03-27 Logical database size stored in header bytes 28..31, which "allows database space to be preallocated". • 9ff27ecdb2 2010-05-19 `SQLITE_FCNTL_SIZE_HINT` lets callers announce the expected final size. • 6e09d69c92 2010-07-27 `SQLITE_FCNTL_CHUNK_SIZE` rounds writes and truncates up to the chunk size via `posix_fallocate`. • fb3828c25f 2011-01-28 Extend files with full zeroed pages rather than writing one byte at the end. • 0fbb50ee15 2012-11-13 `posix_fallocate` for -shm files, also guarding against SIGBUS on a full disk. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-184): 269e780822 2013-07-05 New WAL files are preallocated with `posix_fallocate`, "more efficient than actually writing out zeroes". • 719c84c1be 2016-04-08 Relation extension adds `Min(512, lockWaiters * 20)` pages per acquisition and records each new page in the bottom level of the free space map at once, updating the upper levels only at the end. • 4d330a61bb 2023-04-05 adds `smgrzeroextend()`, `FileZero()` and `FileFallocate()`: fallocate for large extensions, zero writes for small ones. • 31966b151e 2023-04-05 adds `ExtendBufferedRelBy`, and 00d1e02be2 uses it for heap inserts. Concurrent COPY into one table runs 3x faster at 16 clients. Victim buffers are acquired outside the lock, and new pages stay private to the extending backend. • 18103b7c5f 2023-04-06 and 14f98e0af9 release the extension lock earlier and avoid pinning the visibility map while holding the buffer lock. • 82a4edabd2 2023-08-14 Extension grows more aggressively based on how many times BulkInsertState has already extended, avoiding the switch between fallocate and pwritev that XFS handles badly. 3e63e8462f makes the WAL-logged CREATE DATABASE strategy extend in bulk too.

### sqlite-170 — Evict what is cheap to drop, and start flushing before you must
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a cache needs a victim, prefer entries that need no write or no sync. Begin write-back before the cache is entirely dirty so eviction never stalls on a flush.
Why it is faster: Evicting a dirty page whose journal entry is not yet synced forces an fsync in the middle of the operation. Clean or already-synced victims cost nothing. Starting to purge at a high-water mark (90% dirty) spreads write-back instead of hitting a wall.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Buffer pools that prefer clean victims and run background writers. • Native / systems: OS writeback thresholds (`dirty_background_ratio`). • Web / backend: CDN or app caches that evict objects cheapest to refetch first.
Watch out: Biasing toward clean pages can evict hot data; keep the bias bounded and scan positions remembered to avoid quadratic victim searches.
Seen in SQLite: 341eae8d35 2003-01-21 A pointer to the first synced page on the LRU list "makes a fetch of a page that is not in cache go a lot faster when the cache is full". • a858aa2e8c 2008-08-22 "When recycling a page, try to find one that does not require a call to xSync() on the journal file". d491e1bfd1 2008-08-26 recycles only clean pages from other caches. • 6fa0fe13f0 2009-03-05 Begin purging once 90% of the cache is dirty. 062d4cb0ae 2008-08-29 free pages above the limit immediately. 21b7ce6151 2008-07-07 doubly linked page list for O(1) unlinking. • a963896796 2010-02-04 Remember where the last search for a no-sync page ended, fixing a slowdown in large transactions.

### sqlite-171 — Batch writes under one transaction; flush hot aggregates once per commit
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: Hold one transaction across a batch of operations, and keep frequently updated summary records in memory during the transaction, writing them once at commit.
Why it is faster: Each transaction pays locking, journal setup and a sync. A statistics record updated on every row write is rewritten (and journaled) N times when once would do. Write-behind collapses these into one write per commit.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Wrap bulk inserts in one transaction; aggregate counters in a unit of work and flush once. • Data / databases: Custom indexes that persist statistics at commit; batched metrics flushes.
Watch out: Long transactions hold locks longer and grow the journal; batch sizes should be bounded.
Seen in SQLite: 0bbd9c27c2 2014-08-12 FTS5: "Cache the value of the 'totals' record in memory during transactions." • 81415257ec 2017-02-27 FTS5 writes its averages record "once at the end of each transaction instead of every time the table is updated". • d43e7adca7 2024-02-18 `PRAGMA optimize` holds one write transaction across its work "for performance".

### sqlite-172 — Let the writer's native format be the fast path for on-disk integrity checks
Lens: io · Source: SQLite
Principle: For checksums over log data, let the writer use its native byte order and record which order it used in the header, so the common case is a plain add loop.
Why it is faster: A fixed-endian checksum forces a byte swap per word on half of all machines. With a flag, the reader on the same machine (the overwhelmingly common case) uses the plain loop, and only cross-endian readers pay the swap.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Native-endian on-disk formats with a marker (TIFF II/MM style). • Embedded: Fletcher-style checksums in firmware logs written and read on the same MCU.
Watch out: The format now has two code paths that both need testing; only worth it where the checksum is on a hot path.
Seen in SQLite: ce4f05f374 2010-04-22 Added a big-endian checksum variant. b8fd6c2fe6 2010-05-24 `walChecksumBytes(nativeCksum, ...)`: native path `s1 += *aData++ + s2; s2 += *aData++ + s1;`, other path uses `BYTESWAP32`; a WAL header flag records which applies. • 3f4a319934 2023-04-12 The native-endian loop was later unrolled 16 words at a time for 64-byte-multiple inputs.

### sqlite-173 — Skip synchronization entirely when the configuration rules out sharing
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: Decide once, at open or configure time, whether an object can be reached by more than one thread or connection. If not, record that in a flag (or a null mutex) and let every hot path bypass lock acquire/release and lock-conflict checks.
Why it is faster: Uncontended locks still cost atomic instructions, memory fences and a call. In a page cache or allocator they run millions of times per second. A single flag test replaces them, and the flag is cheap enough to test inline with the slow path kept out of line. Shared statistics counters that need a lock can be dropped or made optional for the same reason.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Single-threaded fast paths in allocators (glibc `SINGLE_THREAD_P`); `Rc` instead of `Arc`, or `Arc::get_mut` when the count is unique. • Web / backend: Worker-local caches in Node or per-request objects that need no locks; drop global stats counters or make them per-thread. • Embedded: A build flag that compiles out all locking for single-task firmware. • Data / databases: Skip lock-manager calls for temp tables and session-private objects.
Watch out: The "unshared" proof must be re-evaluated whenever configuration can change (attaching a shared-cache database, enabling memory management). Get it wrong and you have data races that tests rarely catch.
Seen in SQLite: 64e2bb7610 2008-05-07 Omit pager mutex variables when not threadsafe; 18472fa7b8 2008-10-07 "THREADSAFE=0 option always removes all mutex code". • 0f831771c3 2010-03-03 The single-threaded CLI disables all mutexes. b0c6a8884b 2010-09-02 do not hold the memory mutex around the user's malloc when memory statistics are off. • 9f8cf9da27 2011-01-17, bee29f4ac1 2011-01-18 Page-cache groups get a null mutex unless memory management is enabled ("This is a performance enhancement"). 4bd6952a77 2012-06-07 compile option to drop an overflow counter "in order to avoid a mutex". • 075ed30a9f 2010-10-14 Skip locks on unused database connections when committing a read transaction. dc5b047ec3 2011-04-06 a `lockMask` of only sharable b-trees with `if( p->lockMask==0 ) return; /* The common case */`, fixing a regression. • 982215a2c5 2015-06-13, 74a043607e 2015-06-13 `pcache1EnterMutex` becomes an assert unless memory management and threading are both on: "significant speedup". 55a46c9b0e 2015-06-12 a `pcache1FetchNoMutex()` fast path. 97a7e5e68c 2016-04-26 pcache and VFSes skip mutexes under `SQLITE_CONFIG_SINGLETHREAD`. • 38eef32172 2017-01-02 `db->skipBtreeMutex` (later `noSharedCache`) skips `sqlite3BtreeEnterAll` when no b-tree is sharable; 705e73344e 2019-10-05 skips the schema-lock check at prepare. 52df6f5e5c 2017-08-28 with no page-cache slots, `pcache1Alloc` never takes the mutex. • 9d06ff2cb7 2016-11-08 No table-lock opcode for non-sharable b-trees or TEMP. 40ee72947e 2023-06-20 skips table-lock code generation when `noSharedCache` is set. • aa39569616 2026-10-15, b495f75d81 2026-10-16 The `noSharedCache` fast path is enabled in `THREADSAFE=0` builds too.

### sqlite-174 — Exploit immutability and exclusive access to drop cross-process coordination
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: If data cannot change, or only one process can touch it, skip inter-process locks, existence checks for recovery files, and shared-memory machinery; keep coordination structures in ordinary private memory.
Why it is faster: File locks are syscalls; shared memory needs mmap'd files and memory barriers; recovery checks stat files on every transaction. None of it protects anything when the file is read-only media or the process holds an exclusive lock. Heap memory is cheaper to allocate and access than a mapped shm file.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Single-process modes in embedded stores (LMDB `MDB_NOLOCK`); open read-only snapshots and archives without lock files. • Web / backend: Skip distributed locks in single-node deployments; serve immutable, content-hashed assets without revalidation. • Native / systems: Skip IPC when server and worker run in the same process.
Watch out: Declaring a file immutable when something else can modify it gives readers torn or stale data with no error. Make it an explicit opt-in by the caller.
Seen in SQLite: 8c40800439 2010-11-01 In exclusive locking mode the WAL index lives in heap memory, skipping shm barriers. 3cb9339a6c 2011-03-12 the "unix-excl" VFS uses the heap for shm. • d1ae96d39e 2014-05-01 `immutable=1` / `SQLITE_IOCAP_IMMUTABLE` "prevents locking of the database and omits tests for existance of journal files". • 48cdabcb1a 2014-05-01 Avoid unnecessary unlock calls on temp files. 0bf8277196 2014-04-22, f34a9fef4c 2014-04-22 a nolock option on Windows.

### sqlite-175 — Keep hot paths off process-wide locks
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: Route frequent operations to per-connection or per-object resources instead of a global shared one, and skip calls that take a global lock whenever their result is provably unnecessary.
Why it is faster: A global allocator or PRNG mutex serializes every thread that touches it, and its cache line bounces between cores. Per-connection pools need no lock at all because a connection is used by one thread at a time. Removing a call that only exists to fill data nobody reads removes the lock with it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Thread-local allocator caches (tcmalloc, jemalloc arenas); per-thread PRNG state. • Web / backend: Per-request arenas; avoid a global logger lock for disabled levels; avoid `SecureRandom` per request where a counter suffices. • Embedded: Per-task memory pools in an RTOS.
Watch out: Per-owner resources multiply memory use and fragment caches; keep a shared fallback for global limits.
Seen in SQLite: db7ae89d5b 2015-07-06 Each connection gets its own page cache unless memory management or a shared buffer requires a unified one. • 6c96bf2c3f 2023-10-09 `writeJournalHdr()` no longer calls `sqlite3_randomness`, which takes the PRNG mutex, in `journal_mode=MEMORY` where the journal is always empty at that point. • 4093b29610 2024-01-03 JSON functions switched to per-connection `sqlite3DbMalloc` "to avoid hitting the global memory allocator mutex".

### sqlite-176 — One lock per independent object, not one global lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: When a global lock protects many objects that are independent of each other, give each object its own lock so threads working on different objects stop serializing.
Why it is faster: Contention scales with the number of threads sharing a lock. With a per-object lock, contention exists only between threads actually using the same object, which for many workloads is none.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Striped locks in concurrent hash maps; per-inode locks in filesystems. • Web / backend: Per-tenant or per-connection locks instead of one service-wide mutex.
Watch out: More locks mean lock-ordering rules and deadlock risk when an operation needs several; keep a global lock only for the lookup that finds the object.
Seen in SQLite: da6dc2404a 2018-07-23, ce705305b5 2018-07-26 "give every unixInodeInfo object its own mutex, rather than using the global VFS mutex, to improve concurrency ... many threads operating on separate database files".

### sqlite-177 — Use the least exclusive, lightest-weight lock that is correct, and fail fast on expected denials
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Take shared locks where exclusivity is not needed, prefer lightweight lock primitives over heavyweight ones, and do not retry operations that are expected to fail.
Why it is faster: An exclusive lock blocks other readers even when they would not conflict. Lightweight reader/writer locks avoid kernel transitions in the uncontended case. Retry loops intended for transient interference (anti-virus scanners holding files) add long delays when the failure is permanent, such as a permissions probe.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Futex-based locks (parking_lot) instead of heavier OS mutexes; reader/writer locks for read-mostly data. • Web / backend: Back off and retry only on errors classified as transient; fail fast on 403-style denials. • Data / databases: Shared intent locks for readers instead of exclusive table locks.
Watch out: Weakening a lock is a correctness change; verify the protocol (here, the pending-byte dance) still prevents writer starvation and races.
Seen in SQLite: 8bbce21ed7 2025-02-12 Windows: acquiring a SHARED lock takes a temporary shared, not exclusive, lock on the pending byte. • 87ceb6ba9e 2025-03-26 A `NORETRY` flag on `winAccess()` reduces "the delay caused by anti-virus retries" for probes with insufficient permission. • e1d4680fdd 2026-04-28 Windows non-reentrant mutexes use Slim Reader/Writer locks instead of CRITICAL_SECTION "for performance". c549e28c67 2026-04-29 trims the dynamically resolved syscall table from 81 to 58 entries. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-158): a615acf555 2005-12-06 `SimpleLruReadPage_ReadOnly` tries the page under a shared lock; racy LRU counts risk at worst "a nonoptimal choice of page to evict": "a significant win on the pg_subtrans cache". • 0ac5ad5134 2013-01-23 FOR KEY SHARE / FOR NO KEY UPDATE row locks so FK checks don't block non-key updates; 76837c1507 2012-06-26 hash AM metapage heavyweight locks replaced with buffer content locks plus a retry loop (two exclusive acquire-release cycles become one shared cycle); e5550d5fec 2014-04-06, 0ef0396ae1 2015-04-05 and 47167b7907 2015-08-14 lower DDL lock levels. • 218f51584d 2017-03-23 GIN vacuum takes the cleanup lock only if an empty page exists, and only on a subtree; 6d46f4783e 2016-11-30 hash bucket splits use a buffer cleanup lock so scans and inserts run during the split. • d2086b08b0 2018-07-28 `_bt_search()` locks the leaf exclusive directly for inserts: up to 4.5x faster in a multi-core corner case. • 07c73f4506 2026-09-22 heap redo requests a cleanup lock only when the record actually prunes; freeze- or VM-only records take a plain exclusive lock, avoiding replay stalls.

### sqlite-178 — Keep per-slot holder counts instead of scanning all lock holders
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: Decide lock compatibility from a counter on the shared object (number of shared holders, or -1 for exclusive) rather than by walking every participant.
Why it is faster: Walking N connections per lock operation is O(N) under a mutex, which makes lock cost grow with the connection count exactly when concurrency is highest. A counter makes acquire and release O(1).
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Reader/writer locks implemented as a signed counter; reference counts instead of owner lists. • Web / backend: Connection pools tracking resource holders by count.
Watch out: Counters can drift if any path forgets to update them; keep the slow scan as a debug-mode cross-check, as SQLite does.
Seen in SQLite: d49c0c83ef 2020-08-29 `unixShmNode` gains `int aLock[SQLITE_SHM_NLOCK]`; `unixShmLock()` consults it instead of looping over all connections, targeting "hundreds of connections to a single database within the same process". A debug-only check compares it against the full scan.

### sqlite-179 — Let readers ignore entries past their snapshot instead of locking an append-only index
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: For an append-only shared structure, give each reader a high-water mark captured at start, and have it ignore entries beyond that mark. Writers append; readers never block them.
Why it is faster: Readers and the single writer touch the same index, but because entries are only appended and each reader filters by its own bound, no lock is needed on lookups. A hash table at most half full keeps probes near one.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: MVCC version chains filtered by snapshot timestamp; append-only page tables in log-structured stores. • Native / systems: mmap-shared lookup tables between processes; lock-free append-only logs with a published length.
Watch out: Rollback and reset must not reuse slots a live reader can still see; reuse requires knowing no reader holds an older snapshot.
Seen in SQLite: bb23aff3df 2010-05-10 The WAL index uses a hash table over fixed-size blocks (2 slots per entry, multiplicative hash); readers ignore frames beyond their snapshot's `mxFrame`. 29d4dbefaf 2010-05-18 reuses hash space after rollback.

### sqlite-180 — Parallelize independent bulk work and stream the final merge
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: Split large sorts into memory-sized runs sorted on worker threads, merge incrementally, and feed the final merge directly to the consumer instead of materializing it.
Why it is faster: Run sorting is embarrassingly parallel and CPU-bound. Streaming the last merge pass saves writing and rereading a full final run, and lets the consumer start before all keys are merged.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Parallel sort-merge in query engines and pipelines (Spark, DuckDB). • Web / backend: k-way merge of sorted API pages streamed to the client. • Native / systems: `sort --parallel` style log processing.
Watch out: Threads cost startup and memory per run; for small inputs single-threaded is faster, so make parallelism configurable and tune run sizes by measurement.
Seen in SQLite: f87684189d 2014-03-17 through 0f8f26716c 2014-09-01 Worker threads for the external sorter (`PRAGMA threads`). • f77ceba594 2014-04-14 The final merge passes keys "directly to the VDBE, instead of going via a final PMA". 9c1586ec7c 2014-04-14 returns data before all keys are sorted. 4be4c406fa 2014-04-11 limits merge fan-in. • b11c3f22b8 2014-12-09 raised the minimum run size to 250x page size; 80cdfd199b 2014-12-09 lowered it back to 10 after measurement.

### sqlite-181 — Order side effects so concurrent observers never see a state that forces a retry
Lens: concurrency · Source: SQLite
Principle: Publish a version or generation number only after the change it describes is complete, so other actors see a consistent state and do not spin in restart loops.
Why it is faster: If a version bump becomes visible before the data, readers detect a change, retry, and may see the inconsistent state again, looping. Correct ordering removes the loop and any special-case escape hatches added to break it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Seqlocks and RCU: write data, then publish the pointer or sequence. • Web / backend: Write the new config or cache entry before bumping the version clients poll.
Watch out: Requires memory ordering guarantees (barriers or a lock) between the two writes on weakly ordered hardware.
Seen in SQLite: 9b3f957fe1 2026-10-26 DROP TABLE deletes the root page "prior to updating the schema cookie, and that avoids the need to early-out the reprepare loop".

### sqlite-182 — Measure with deterministic instruction counts on a fixed, representative workload
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite
Principle: Benchmark every change on a reproducible workload under an instruction- or cycle-counting simulator instead of wall-clock time, diff against the baseline automatically, and keep the workload representative of real use.
Why it is faster: Wall-clock timings vary by several percent run to run, which hides any change smaller than that. Instruction counts (cachegrind, callgrind) are deterministic to a fraction of a percent, so 0.1% gains can be accepted or rejected with confidence, and many such gains compound: SQLite's 2014 campaign summed to 7.58%. A fixed harness that diffs against trunk makes measuring every commit cheap enough to be routine.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `perf stat -e instructions`, callgrind or iai benchmarks in CI; report before/after counts in every perf PR. • Web / backend: A fixed request replay against a staging build, with CPU time per request rather than latency percentiles for micro-changes. • Embedded: Cycle counters (DWT on Cortex-M) on a fixed test vector. • Data / databases: Verify identical query output alongside timing so speedups cannot hide wrong answers.
Watch out: Instruction counts do not see cache misses, I/O latency or contention well; pair them with wall-clock and I/O benchmarks for those. A benchmark that is not representative optimizes the wrong thing.

### sqlite-183 — Attribute cost to the unit you can change
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite
Principle: Profile at the granularity of your design: per opcode, per source file, per query loop. Add build switches that make hidden costs (inlined library calls) visible to the profiler.
Why it is faster: A total tells you something got slower; a per-unit breakdown tells you which handler, file or plan step to fix. Library calls like `memcpy` appear as one lump in a profile; replacing them with inline loops in a profiling build reveals which call sites dominate.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Per-handler and per-middleware timing; per-resolver timing in GraphQL. • Data / databases: EXPLAIN ANALYZE-style per-operator counters exposed to users. • Native / systems: Per-function counters in interpreters; build variants with `-fno-builtin` to see library call sites.
Watch out: Instrumentation perturbs what it measures; keep it behind a build flag and confirm wins in the normal build.

### sqlite-184 — Delete "optimizations" the numbers do not support
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite
Principle: Treat every optimization as a hypothesis. If measurement shows no gain, or that its upkeep costs more than it saves, revert it, even if it is old or obviously clever.
Why it is faster: Special cases, caches and peephole passes have their own runtime cost, code size and maintenance cost. A cache taxes every writer; a peephole pass can cost more cycles than the instructions it removes. Removing such code often makes the program faster and smaller at once.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Re-benchmark memoization, object pools and per-request caches with low hit rates; `useMemo` on cheap values is a net loss. • Native / systems: Remove hand-unrolled loops and custom allocators that modern compilers and libcs outperform. • Data / databases: Drop derived-count caches whose invalidation costs exceed recomputation.
Watch out: A measured win can still be wrong: fabe393da8 2016-02-03 gained about 0.5% but was backed out in e514f651d0 2016-02-04 because it mishandled reentrancy. Re-measure on current hardware and compilers before removing something that once helped.

### sqlite-185 — Guard performance with regression tests and per-release baselines
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite
Principle: Hold every release to the previous release's speed, add tests that catch specific performance regressions, and treat a lost optimization as a bug.
Why it is faster: Correctness fixes and refactors silently undo optimizations; without a gate, the codebase drifts slower. Comparing against a stored baseline makes regressions visible within one commit, when the cause is still obvious.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Lighthouse budgets and benchmark diffs on pull requests; fail CI on bundle-size growth. • Native / systems: Criterion or pytest-benchmark baselines stored per release. • Embedded: Track code size and worst-case cycle counts per build.
Watch out: Gates on noisy wall-clock numbers produce false alarms and get ignored; gate on deterministic metrics and keep thresholds realistic.
Seen in SQLite: b62c335ed6 2006-11-23, abcddedb37 2006-11-29 Speed tests added to the test suite. 538f570cd1 2007-04-13 "Fix multiple performance regressions ... add tests to make sure they do not come back". • bb810a9a7b 2010-07-03 Speed tests print the source id, so results are tied to an exact build. • 41692e9d7c 2011-01-25 A change made "so that performance is better than 3.7.4 in speed tests". Explicit regression fixes: 547caad4c4 2010-10-04, d3842765ae 2010-12-10, dc5b047ec3 2011-04-06, 42acb3e597 2011-09-05. • 45b355dc4f 2010-04-01 "restores an optimization that was accidentally undone".

### sqlite-186 — Set defaults and thresholds by experiment, not by guess
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: Initial capacities, spill thresholds, run sizes and heuristic cutoffs should be chosen by benchmarking a range of values on representative workloads, and revisited when the code around them changes.
Why it is faster: Defaults are what most users run with. A preallocation that is too big wastes memory and setup time on every connection; too small causes repeated growth. The optimum is often far from intuition (SQLite cut an initial page-cache size fivefold and got faster).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Connection-pool sizes, batch sizes and timeouts tuned against replayed production traffic. • Native / systems: Initial hash-map capacity, GC nursery size, buffer sizes. • Embedded: Pool and queue sizes measured on target hardware, not a desktop.
Watch out: A value tuned for one workload can hurt another; document what it was tuned on and keep it configurable.
Seen in SQLite: 83a4f47d57 2017-01-02 `SQLITE_DEFAULT_PCACHE_INITSZ` 100 to 20, "experimentally determined to be slightly faster". 56d65cd7b9 2017-01-02 default lookaside raised to 1200,100. • 839fa6d814 2017-04-13 A skip-ahead threshold of 11 rows "was determined by experimentation". • b11c3f22b8 2014-12-09 raised the minimum sorter run size to 250x the page size; 80cdfd199b 2014-12-09 lowered it back to 10. • 6fa0fe13f0 2009-03-05 Start purging the page cache at 90% dirty, which "improves performance in some cases". 3298a641a2 2016-03-04 statement journals spill at 64 KiB; 0f52455a35 2016-04-13 temp pages flushed at 25% dirty. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-219): 70868c012f 2007-08-12 StringInfo's initial size goes from 256 to 1024 bytes, cutting repalloc calls in the regression tests from about 6,900 to about 3,900. • 9f9135d129 2012-01-25 Heap fetches in index-only scans are instrumented, so the effect of the visibility map can be seen in EXPLAIN. • a5fc46414d 2022-10-27 On the regression suite, new join clauses built drop from 9,673 to 5,142 out of 26,652 calls, which is the measured proxy for the change. • 8242752f9c 2022-11-29 KnownAssignedXids compaction triggers at 2x live entries and at most once per 128 commit records, a frequency chosen by benchmark. • 82c0cb4e67 2026-04-01 `pg_test_timing` compares against a precomputed end time instead of computing elapsed time each loop, so the measuring tool itself perturbs less. • 7f8c88c2b8 2026-04-02 The `jit` default changes to off, because compile cost can swamp short queries and cost thresholds produce cliffs. 931c9701f2 makes `wal_compression=on` pick the best available codec.

### sqlite-187 — Report speed and size together and make trade-offs explicit
Lens: observability · Source: SQLite
Principle: Every performance change carries a cycle delta and a code-size delta. Record both, prefer changes that improve both, and accept a trade only when its price is known.
Why it is faster: Code size affects instruction-cache pressure, download size and embedded fit. Removing a branch or argument usually helps both speed and size; inlining usually trades one for the other. Quoting both numbers lets reviewers see whether a 0.6% gain is worth 700 bytes, and lets partial approaches (inlining at a few call sites) capture most of the speed for little size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Report bundle-size and runtime deltas together in frontend PRs. • Embedded: Firmware flash budgets tracked next to cycle counts. • Native / systems: Track binary size in CI next to benchmarks; prefer profile-guided partial inlining over blanket inlining.
Watch out: Size and speed numbers are compiler- and flag-specific; state the toolchain and optimization level with each number.
Seen in SQLite: 1ffd247c0c 2015-06-23 Cached cell sizes: "overall 1.7% performance gain for about 1000 extra bytes". 9c739e26da 2015-06-24 "2.6% faster overall with a size increase of less than 750 bytes". • b53a5a9e50 2014-10-12 A cache removal that "Saves 100 bytes ... runs 0.2% faster": a win on both axes. • 47774fd90b 2026-08-20 Inlining saved ">10 million cycles (0.6%)" for under 700 bytes. 4616815533 2026-08-16 partial inlining gave "96% of the performance increase with only 18% of the object code size increase". • d3b165a85e 2026-08-07 A deliberate trade that gave up a few cycles for a smaller binary.
